JOHN GRIMES WALKER, U.S.N. 

1835-1907 

BOSTON, 1959 By Frances P. Thomas

CAREER OF JOHN GRIMES WALKER, U.S.N. 

1835 1907 by 

FRANCES P. THOMAS Boston, 1959

05 2058.23.2 US 2089.30 

HARVARD UNIVERSITY LIBRARY 

JUL 8 1962 63373 

COPYRIGHT BY JOHN G. W. THOMAS 

1959

John Grimes Walker was born on March 20, 1835 in the little village of Hillsborough Bridge, New Hampshire. The stock from which he came was English on one side and Scotch-Irish upon the other. From the earliest days of the American colonies his ancestors had been industrious, public spirited and patriotic, serving as selectmen, constables and deputies to the general court and, what seems especially interesting in connection with the subject of this sketch, as soldiers in almost every generation, certainly whenever there were Indians or other enemies to be fought. 

It would be interesting, if space permitted, to speak at length of some of the ancestors whose blood flowed through the veins of John Walker, but I shall only mention a few of whom we have public and private records, to give some idea of his inheritance and what went to make up his character. 

Philip Walker, the first ancestor of the name of whom we know anything, came to this country as a very young man with his mother and was settled in Rehoboth, in the "Old Colony" before 1643, but his English home and the date of his coming are unknown. He became one of the richest men in the settlement, his estate being exceeded by only one in a valuation of eighty-three estates in 1678. During King Philip's War he was one of the foremost to provide the sinews of war, but we do not know whether he took any active part in the fighting; as he was probably between fifty and fifty-five years of age at the outbreak of hostilities in 1675 it is quite likely that he contented himself with providing financial support for the defenders of the colony. His oldest son Samuel did fight and was lieutenant and captain under Major Bradford. 

Samuel's grandson, Aaron Walker, great-grandfather of John

Walker, was in Captain Samuel Bliss's company of minute men and served as Lieutenant under his first cousin, Colonel Timothy Walker but died in Roxbury of camp fever at the siege of Boston in 1775. His son 

Samuel served several years in the contenent al army during the American Revolution, and his son Alden, John Walker's father, served when quite young in the war of 1812. Surely the record of a family that did its share to protect their country in time of need. 

Philip Walker married Jane Metcalf, whose father, Michael

Metcalf of Dedham had left England to escape religious persecution. 

He wrote of the violent measures of Bishop Wren of Norwich, England and his Chancellor, Dr. Corbett, and that his enemies conspired to take his life so that to avoid them his wife hid him "in the roof of the house", covering him with straw. In Dedham he attained a position of some importance, holding the office of selectman and accumulating a property large for his day. 

Aaron Walker's maternal grandmother was Grace Morris, whose father, Edward Morris, came as a child with his parents from Waltham Holy Cross, Essex County, England and settled in Roxbury, of which town he was selectman for twelve years, and Representative to the General Court for nine years, until the vacation of the Colonial Charter. 

Edward Morris sat in the House of Deputies during most of the long contest which the colony had with King Charles II to preserve her charter, which continued to the end of his reign and ended in its abrogation in

1686, in the reign of James II. During most of the eight years of this struggle, when Roxbury stood firmly against the King's encroachments and supported the cause of popular liberty and the rights of the people under the charter, Morris was the only representative from Roxbury. In 1686 he moved to New Roxbury, Connecticut. He was made Lieutentant for the defense of the new settlement in 1689, after which his name always appears with the title of Lieutenant while before this he was always given the very honorable title of Mr. He was one of the committee of three that went before the General Court to ask to have the name of the settlement changed to Woodstock and for the confirmation of their land titles which Governor Andros had declared void. He died in 1690 and his gravestone still stands in the graveyard at Woodstock Hill. 

The husband of Grace Morris, Benjamin Child, also moved to Woodstock where he served as selectman, deputy to the General Court and part of the time Colonial Auditor. 

John Walker's paternal grandmother was Anna Carpenter, whose ancestor, William Carpenter, came to this country with his wife and children in 1637, calling himself a carpenter. His family has been traced back several generations in England, his forbears being educated men, and he himself had evidently followed in their footsteps for in his will he left his Greek and Latin books to his sons and other books, the Bible, Bible commentaries and others to his wife and daughters. He settled in Weymouth, later moving to Rehoboth, in each town being chosen as representative to the General Court, while at Rehoboth he was appointed captain to defend the settlement from Samuel Gorton. William Carpenter was a friend of Governor Bradford, who had married his cousin, Alice (Carpenter) Southworth, and they frequently corresponded. Tradition says that in her youth her parents objected to Bradford's suit on the grounds of his inferiority of birth, but when both were widowed he wrote to her from this country, "I am not the Bill Bradford I once was, I am now the Governor of the Colony...... and if you will come to America 

I am at your service".

Among the other ancestors who held commissions in the military forces one should be mentioned because of his serving as lieutenant with Sir William Phips in his romantic but unsuccessful expedition against 

Quebec in 1690, Lieutenant Preserved Abell of Rehoboth.

All these forefathers of John Walker, who served the colony so well in peace as well as war, were of English race and came to America in the "great exodus" of 1632-16 38, or shortly after that time, driven from their homes by the church's persecution of the puritans. Unlike the pilgrims, they were not separatists and would have preferred to remain in the English Church, if it could be freed from the abuses that had crept into it, but under Charles I the treatment of their ideas became unbearable and they emigrated in great numbers. 

On the side of his mother, Susan (Grimes) Walker, on the other hand, he came of a more recent but equally sturdy immigration, that of the Scotch presbyterians from the north of Ireland. Her immigrant ancestor on her father's side was Francis Graham, who came to this country in 1719, when three vessels reached Boston bringing presbyterians from the north of Ireland. These newcomers were a fearless, hardy race.

Efforts have been made to show that they were of Irish ancestry but this contention seems to be unsupported by the facts. They were of 

Scotch descent, their character formed during the stormy period of Scottish struggles, confirmed and strengthened after their emigration to the north of Ireland, by their long and sanguinary conflicts with royal and ecclesiastical tyranny. "The native Catholic Irish resented their settling on Irish soil and differences of language, race and religion kept the two peoples apart and at times resulted in fighting, bloody massacres and burning of farms, the Scotch living in constant fear of attacks by hordes of the wild native Irish. To escape from this danger, many emigrated to America."

Francis Graham settled in or near Boston, possibly in Medford, and was married to Mary Dickey on 20 January 1732 in the Scotch Presbyterian Church, later the Federal Street Church in Boston. He was a friend or acquaintance of Colonel John Hill of Boston, a man of considerable influence who, in January 1735-6 received from the courts of Massachusetts a grant of a tract of land in the wilderness of New Hampshire. The next information we have in regard to Francis 

Graham is in 1640 when he and his wife migrated with the second band of settlers to the part of Colonel Hill's grant known then as No. 7 

Township, later Hillsborough, the first party, consisting of eight or ten men and one woman, having made their way there one year earlier, in 1739. Here is 1743 a daughter, Ann, was born to Francis and Mary 

Graham, the third child born in the township. In 1744 the Indians were roused and began attacks on all the frontier settlements and the inhabitants lived in constant danger of torture and death; in the spring of 1746 towns very near No. 7 were attacked and when a war party of Indians was seen prowling in the nearby forest the few inhabitants, probably about fifteen families, decided that to remain in their homes was to court disaster and hastily burying their heavier household articles and tools and driving their livestock before them, the pioneers of No. 7 returned to their former homes and Colonel Hill very generously bought out the interests of those who had been driven from their homesteads. About a year after the return of the pioneers to 

Massachusetts in 1747, Francis Graham's son Francis was born on Noddle's Island, now East Boston. It seems probable that there were other children but so far no record of them has been found. The only other clues we have in regard to Mary and Francis Graham are merely possibilities. A Mrs. Mary Grimes died 8 May 1747, ae. 46, and is buried in the Granary Burying Ground in Boston and we know that the name of John Walker's ancestor was changed to Grimes, although it is supposed that the son Francis changed it, his father always signing himself Francis Graham. However, as the reason he gave for the change was that no one could spell the Scotch name properly, it may have been spelled sometimes one way and sometimes the other, or the stone on Mary Grimes' grave may have been placed years afterwards when the name had been permanently changed; possibly further search may give more positive proof. 

On March 3rd, 1755 "Francis Grayham of Medford, Husbandman, in the county of Middlesex and Province of Massachusetts Bay in New England" bought "in consideration of the sum of two hundred and fifty pounds lawful money of the province.........a certain tract of land with house and barn thereon situate and being in the township of Londonderry.......containing ninety acres more or less being upon the fourth range. range......lying

upon the west side of Beaver Brook". So after 9 years Francis Graham returns to New Hampshire. He may have moved to New Hampshire to be near relations of his wife, the records of Londonderry showing many inhabitants named Dickey. Here his daughter Ann married Deacon McKeen and Francis Graham, Jr. married Elizabeth Wilson, and later Francis Sr. and his two children with their families moved to Deering, N. H., lying across the Contoocook River from Hillsborough, where he had made his first attempt to settle in New Hampshire. Family tradition says that in his old age he lived with his daughter, Ann McKeen, and dying, was buried in Wolf's Hill burying ground, but that the family neglected to put up a stone. The son, however, Lieut. Francis Graham, Jr., and his wife, Elizabeth Wilson, great grandparents of John Walker, are buried in that peaceful old gods acre and beside them Elizabeth's brother, Capt. David Wilson and his wife, Sarah Cochran, also greatgrandparents of John Walker. Living as we do in an era of wonderful scientific and mechanical inventions which almost do away with the consideration of time and distance, railroads, automobiles, airplanes, the telegraph and radio, and of material comforts undreamed of until recent times, it is hard for us to imagine life in a small New England community one hundred years ago; but to understand the early surroundings of the subject of this sketch let us try to picture to ourselves the little village of Hillsborough Bridge, New Hampshire at the time of the birth of 

John Grimes Walker. Situated in the midst of beautiful, hilly country and on the banks of the lovely Contoocook River, which here rushes over two series of rapids or falls, this soon became the most important of the four hamlets in the township of Hillsborough, for the excellent water power led to the erection of several small mills. It must not be thought, however, that it at all resembled a modern mill town; an account of the village written about 1840 when John Walker was a child of five years old, says that it then consisted of 56 dwellings, 2 churches, all of wood, heated only by open fires or air tight stoves and of course plumbing was unknown, the Saturday night bath being taken in a wooden tub in the kitchen; there were also 2 hotels, 2 shops, 2 cotten factories, 2 grist mills, 2 sawmills, one axe factory and a post office while one doctor and one lawyer were sufficient for the needs of the village. There were no foreign mill operators in those days and very little communication with other parts of the country, most villages were self-sufficient, travel was by stage coach, slow and fatiguing and few journeyed beyond the neighboring towns. Hillsborough on the post road, a loop road, between Concord and Keene, one of the first four routes authorized in New Hampshire in 1791 about midway between Concord and Keene. Four days were allowed for making the round trip and there were quick changes of horses every twenty miles; later a second post road ran from Nashua to Claremont, passing through Hillsborough, and in 1831 was laid out the "New Road to Keene", running from Hillsborough Bridge to Keene. But not until 1849 did a railroad reach Hillsborough and then it only extended from Concord to Hillsborough Bridge. was Here, then, in these peaceful surroundings John Walker was born, and here he spent the first ten or twelve years of his life, a little tow headed fellow, shy and self-conscious but strong willed and

independent; he always remembered his embarrassment when he was very small and his mother one day dressed him in a new red dress to see some friends who were coming to spend the afternoon; he was so ashamed to be seen in dresses that he hid behind the sofa and refused to come out. 

His mother died when he was eleven years old and he went to live with his grandparents, across the river in Deering, in the house still standing built by his great-grandfather, Francis Graham, the first of the family to give up definitely the proper Scotch spelling of the name Graham and permanently adopt that of Grimes. Even before his mother's death the boy had spent much time with his grandparents. Here the youngster followed "Squire Grimes" about the farm, much petted by his grandfather, his grandmother being the one to manage and discipline him. 

Life on the Deering farm was almost patriarchal in its details. 

The long, airy kitchen, extending across the whole width of the house behind the front rooms, with windows at both ends and a great fireplace in the chimney, was the centre of family life and here at meal-times the "Squire" sat at the head of the table with his wife at his right hand and his eldest son at his left, his six other children arranged according to age down both sides of the table, while the "help" sat below the large salt cellar. 

The life of the community was very quiet, with simple amusements largely connected with the church, and few books or papers. The first book owned by John Walker entitled "The Sabbath School, embellished with colored engravings" was brought to him by his grandfather from Concord and, in these days of beautiful books for children, seems very quaint and crude but was much prized and treasured by the child, and is still in the possession of the family. 

The boy's restless spirit showed itself early. When he was ten years old a man stopped at the farm and wanted to buy a yoke of oxen and after an examination of the animals and the usual bargaining with Squire Grimes an agreement was reached but the sale nearly fell through when it proved that the purchaser wanted a man to drive the animals to Keene, about 28 miles distant and would pay him one dollar for the job, but Squire Grimes had no man that he could spare. Little John had been an interested listener to the men's talk and now eagerly volunteered to undertake the job. His grandfather would not hear of it at first but the boy was very confident that he could do it and at last his grandfather yielded, possibly thinking that he would not get far, but the tenyear old child drove the oxen safely to Keene, sleeping on the way by his grandfather's direction with people known to his grandparents. Undoubtedly the youngster felt very grown up as he trudged along the country road armed with a long stick or whip, shouting gee and haw to his clumsy charges, and finally reaching home, the proud owner of one whole dollar earned by his own efforts. 

After his mother's death and his father's second marriage the boy seems to have imagined that his step-mother was unkind to him but in later life, looking back upon his childhood he was convinced that he had been mistaken, that the feeling had come from gossip about stepmothers. However that may be he began to think of going into the world to seek his fortune and escape from the necessary restraints of home, and the opportunity came when he and a young cousin were taken to

Concord for the day. When the time came to return to Hillsborough 

John announced that he had secured a job in the hat store of a Mr. 

Truesdell and the cousin was obliged to go home alone. Squire Grimes at once went to investigate and finding Mr. Truesdell a respectable and reliable man he decided to let his grandson try supporting himself for a time, convinced that when the novelty wore off he would return home, and it was not long before he was proved right. But soon the boy was again restless and Marryot's novels having filled him with ideas of the romance and thrill of a sailor's life, he again ran away, this time in search of adventure at sea. His Uncle James Wilson Grimes who had moved to Iowa in 

1836 had come back to Hillsborough to visit his parents and at once went in pursuit of his nephew and brought him back telling him that before thinking of going to sea, he must complete his education, and that if he would to go Iowa with him and finish his schooling, and then was of the same mind, he would try to get him a commission in the navy. This plan was carried out and in Burlington, Iowa, he spent the next four years of his life with his uncle and aunt, who having no children of their own, looked upon him as their son. His preparatory schooling finished 

Mr. Grimes, a successful lawyer, already interested in public affairs, who had served a term in the State legislature, wrote to Hon. William B. 

Preston, Secretary of the Navy, "I have a nephew under my charge, the son of a deceased sister, fifteen years of age, poor, who is anxious to obtain a warrant as a midshipman. He is a smart, likely lad, good scholar, well-disposed and promising. He has no means whatever. Now 

I am anxious to obtain the appointment for him. This is the first and in all probability the last application I shall ever make for an office for myself or any of my relations or friends."

An appointment to Annapolis having been declined by a young 

Mr. Ketchell, Mr. Daniel F. Miller of Fort Madison recommended in his place John Grimes Walker and the appointment was offered to him. In these days of rapid transit it is interesting to see from the following letter to the Secretary of the Navy, dated September 11, 1850, how remote Iowa then was. "I beg leave to call to your attention the limitations of time in the appointment of John Grimes Walker as midshipman, to the first of October or within five days there after. The youth is at Burlington, Iowa, and it is absolutely impossible for him to report himself at Annapolis during that period. I have therefore to ask the favor of an extension of permit to November 1st following. 

I have the honor to be 

With high respect 

Fit zHenry Warren."

When Mr. Grimes handed his nephew his appointment he told him that his advice was to tear it up and remain in Burlington, a growing town with great opportunities of advancement for an energetic young man, but young John was determined to go to sea and felt no interest in entering his uncle's very successful law office.

Judging from young Walker's letter of acceptance now in the records of the Navy Department but unfortunately partly burned, this

extension was granted, for he writes to Hon. William A. Graham, Secretary of the Navy, under the date of September 25, 1850, "Yours .......9th and 12th instant have been received this......ing. I shall leave here tomorrow and report myself to the superintendant of the Naval School at Annapolis as speedily as the journey can be made."

It was a long and tedious trip, there was no railroad to Iowa and the journey must be made largely by stage coach and canal boat, but the boy of fifteen undoubtedly found it all novel and interesting and the fates were propitious for the following report to Commander Stribling, Superintendent, U. S. Naval Academy dated 

October 5, 1850 shows that he must have reached Annapolis within the limits originally set by his appointment. "Sir: In obedience to your order of this date, the Academic Board has examined J. G. Walker of Iowa in Reading, Writing and Arithemtic and has found him duly qualified for admission into the Navy in conformity with the "regulations which govern the admission of candidates into the Naval Academy at Annapolis, Maryland" approved to take effect from the 1st of July 

1850. "

Very respectfully for the board, William Chauvenet Secretary"

On his way to Annapolis young Walker, by his very youthful appearance, attracted the attention of General Ramsay who was taking his son to the Naval Academy for the entrance examinations; they made the rest of the trip together and the boys became fast friends and young Ramsay years later was one of Walker's groomsmen at his wedding. 

Admiral Walker seldom talked of his early life and was never a great letter writer; until the end of the Civil War his correspondence was confined almost entirely to letters to his uncle and adopted father, Mr. Grimes, and most unforunately after Mr. Grimes's death his executors burned his papers unread so that our source of information about the next ten years are meagre and we must depend upon official papers and the memory of occasional remarks of Admiral Walker himself. The halo of romance which surrounded the life of a sailor in the boy's mind soon vanished and before a year had passed only his pride and the knowledge that it would please his uncle, and adopted father, to have him stick to work he had undertaken and stand well at Annapolis, kept him faithful to the career he had chosen. This incentive made him work hard at his studies but he never looked back upon the years spent at Annapolis as a happy time; he was troubled by severe headaches and being shy he did not enter much into the social life of the Academy, limited at best by strict regulation of the midshipman's hours. 

In November 1851 Acting Midshipman Walker was ordered to Boston to report for duty on the sloop of war Portsmouth, the practice cruise of the midshipmen of that time coming in the middle of the Naval Academy course instead of at the end as now. The acting midshipmen formed a mess of their own and could either provide their own food using the government allowance of about thirty-three cents a day, adding to this

as much as they chose, or eat the regular sailors' rations of salt beef and hard tack. The youngsters naturally chose the former alternative but this cruise in the Portsmouth in 1851 took them around Cape Horn to California where the discovery of gold had brought great mumbers of men from all over the world and caused such a demand for foodstuffs that the prices soared to almost prohibitive heights, quite prohibitive for these young midshipmen with their small pay. Unfortunately the ships stores of the Portsmouth just returned from a cruise had not been renewed before starting on the new cruise and proved to be spoiled, the meat the color of old copper and the hard tack full of weevils; they eked out this bad food as well as they could but the provisions they could afford were not of the most nourishing sort. Young 

Walker went ashore once in search of supplies with what money the members of the mess could contribute and the best he could do was to bring back a boatload of cabbages and a demijohn of rum! This was better than nothing for they ate the cabbages and after killing the weevils by baking the hard tack and then hitting it on the table to knock out the corpses, they soaked it in the rum to disguise the taste and could then swallow it. 

It can easily be seen that this diet was not good for growing boys sixteen to eighteen years old and when Midshipman Walker was detached from the Portsmouth in April 1855 and given three months leave he had grown to his full height of six feet but was extremely thin and not in vigorous health. His leave was passed in his native village of Hillsborough Bridge and seems to have been extended, for orders from the Secretary of the Navy dated July 24, 1855 direct him to proceed to Annapolis by October 1st and report for attendance at the Naval Academy for examinations. During this winter he became a victim of tuberclosis, a sudden hemorrhage from the lungs coming while he was on drill with a heavy musket; he refused to give up to the disease but kept on with his studies, keeping a bottle of cod liver oil in his room and taking it frequently while he always had salt in his pocket to check any tendency to hemorrhage. On June 20, 1856 he graduated at the head of his class, his friend Ramsay holding third place, and being detached from the Naval Academy went to Grafton, Vermont where his father then lived and where his warrant as Passed Midshipman was sent to him, on June 30th. A letter from the Navy 

Department dated December 5th, 1856 went to him in Burlington, Iowa ordering him to duty on the sloop of war Falmouth and he joined her on January 1st, 1857 at New York and proceeded in her to South America where in June he was transferred to the flagship St. Lawrence with the rank of Acting Master. In February 1858 he received his warrant as of 

January 22, 1858 appointing him Master (in the line of promotion); while still serving on the St. Lawrence in South American waters and in April of that year he received his commission as Lieutenant in the U. S. Navy from January 23, 1858. At the end of December, 1858 he was transferred to the brig. Bainbridge for temporary duty. In May, 1859 orders reached him detaching him from the St. Lawrence and granting him leave of absence. Unfortunately the lack of letters makes it impossible to tell how Lieutenant Walker passed his leave; some of it at least was spent in Hillsborough for in September orders were sent to him there to proceed to Annapolis, Maryland and report for duty at the Naval Academy as an instructor. In July, 1860 he was ordered to 

New York for study on the Steam Sloop of War Susquehanna, his experience so far having been in sailing vessels. While this vessel tras lying in Boston Harbor, the commanding officer was invited to the home of

Mr. Henry Pickering in Roxbury and asked to take with him one or two young officers. Lieutenant Walker was of the party and there met for the first time Miss Rebecca White Pickering who later became his wife; he had been pointed out to her, when she and her parents had visited the Susquehanna as a fine young officer, dying of consumption. Young 

Walker's insistence in living the regular life of a young Naval Officer, going to sea and carrying out all his duties, was, in those days, considered suicidal but was rather in line with the modern treatment; on board ship he could take very little exercise and had plenty of fresh air, and although he once heard the doctor say that he thought he would not live until morning, he recovered entirely and lived to the age of seventy-two.

In June, 1861, shortly after the outbreak of the Civil War he was detached from the Susquehanna and ordered to the Boston Navy Yard for ordinance duty, and from July 17th to October 28th he served on the steamer Connecticut as Executive Officer. On this vessel he went with supplies along the coast from New York to Galveston, Texas and back to New York, communicating with every blockading vessel, and then to Bermuda in pursuit of the Nashville, returning once more to New York. 

On November 7th upon the arrival of his relief Lieutenant Walker was sent as Executive Officer to the J. S. Steam Gun Boat Winona of the

West Gulf Blockade Squadron and joined the blockade off the mouth of the Mississippi. At this time plans were being made for the capture of New Orleans by the Navy and its occupation by Federal troops but it was necessary for the government to buy and fit out vessels suitable for carrying mortars and twenty mortars with their thirty thousand bombshells had to be cast at Pittsburgh and taken to New York and Philadelphia while the mortar carriages were made in New York. Then, too, ships must be collected from various stations and fitted out and suitable officers selected to command and officer them, so that it was the middle of March, 1862, before Admiral Farragut had all his vessels ready off the Mississippi; but before the number was complete he had sent Captain Bell, his chief of staff, up the river to discover what had been done by the enemy to prevent the passage of the forts and also to do anything possible to prevent news of the activities of the Federal fleet being sent to Forts Jackson and St. Philip. The following extract from Captain Bell's diary shows that Lieutenant Walker accompanied some of these expeditions. The "passes of the Mississippi" were the various branches some ten or twelve miles long into which the river divided before reaching its various mouths, the Head of the 

Passes being where they joined. 

March 9th, 1862, "9:10 A.M. started in the gun boat Winona,

Lieutenant Commander Nichols, for the head of the passes of the Mississ ippi......the object of this visit to the passes being to secure every man found in them, particularly the tele graph operators, Bohn and Cubett, the telegraph instruments, and to cut the wires. To secure also the pilots that may be found......Lieutenant Walker landed at Cubett's and made the examination there. He reports the connection broken and brought off two men." 

Unfortunately the Confederates had had ample warning of the proposed attack and had worked hard to strengthen Forts Jackson and

St. Philip, one on each side of the river, and had moored eight bulks, connected by heavy chains, in a line across the stream, reenforced by log rafts, while fire rafts were ready to be fired when the attack came. At last on April 24th, after great difficulty had been experienced in getting the larger vessels over the bar and the carrying out of much preparatory work and several days' bombardments by mortar boats the attempt to pass the forts took place, the leading vessel getting under fire at 3.00 A. M. After

The report of Lieutenant Commander Nichols to Admiral Farragut, written on the 30th of April, 1862, tells of the part taken by the Winona in this engagement. "Sir: I beg leave respectfully to present the following report of the operations of this vessel in the engagement with 

Forts Jackson and St. Philip on the morning of the 24th instant. getting underway I took my station as soon as possible in the line, astern of the Itasca, and followed her red light, but suddenly found myself involved in a mass of logs and drift stuff held by the chains and moorings of the hulks. While trying to back clear, the Itasca backed and fouled me on the starboard bow. After a delay of from twenty minutes to half an hour, I proceeded on my way, though I felt pretty sure that the bulk of the fleet had passed. Day was breaking fast, and my vessel was brought out in bold relief against the bright sky, presenting a fair mark for the gunners of the fort. Fort Jackson fired at me as I appoached, and the first gun killed one man and wounded another. The third or fourth gun killed or wounded every man at the rifle gun except one. Judging that the burning raft was on the Fort 

Jackson side, I steered to pass it on the port hand and did not discover my error until the whole lower battery of Fort St. Philip opened on me at less than point blank range. Sheering off with the starboard helm, I shot across to the Fort Jackson side, but owing to the obscurity caused by the smoke, got so close to shore that I had no room to turn head upstream and was forced to head down. At this time both forts were firing nearly their entire batteries at me. It would have been madness to attempt turning again on such a fire........It was with reluctance 

I gave the order to turn down stream and run out of the fire, first ordering the officers and crew to lay down on the deck. I cannot, sir, speak too highly of the conduct of all on board. My orders were obeyed with alacrity, and (considering the suddenness of the fire opened on us from Fort St. Philip and the naturally depressing effect of the first few shots) with but little confusion. Mr. Walker, the first lieutenant, was very active and vigilant and gave his personal attention to every part of the vessel. He was slightly wounded in the ear......Since the 

24th I have been acting under the orders of Commander Porter, and on the 28th instant I had the satisfaction of receiving the surrender of Fort St. Philip, and hoisting in its proper place once more the flag of our country. I was unfortunate, sir, in not passing the forts, but, I trust, not censurable. I am, sir.........

Another account of this action is taken from a letter by A. F. O'Neil, Captain's Clerk, in the Winona. "We followed the Itasca's stern light as it was quite dark, all the squadron going ahead at full speed under cover of a most tremendous fire from our mortar vessels. 

Just as the foremost vessels were engaging the forts, we suddenly found ourselves entangled in a mass of logs and drift stuff let loose by the breaking away of the chains and hulks which had been stretched across

the river above us, and in trying to extricate ourselves the Itasca, which was backing to clear the same stuff, fouled us on the starboard bow and our anchor tore her waist boat and davits off. About twentyfive minutes elapsed before we were clear and by that time most of the fleet had run the gauntlet of the forts......We rushed on at full speed and, dawn now breaking, we presented a fair target to the enemy, distinctly relieved.....against the bright eastern sky, and they were not slow to take advantage of it. When within about a quarter of a mile ......Fort Jackson opened upon our vessel. I was standing in the forecastle at the time with Hunt and Mr. Walker, our first lieutenant.... 

The shots were now whistling around us quite lively, keeping up a constant shriek over our heads.....Just then we discovered a fire raft coming down upon us and supposing it, in the smoke, to be on the Fort Jackson side of the river, we steered to leave it on our port hand, but discovered our mistake on finding ourselves directly under the guns of Fort St. Philip and adjoining water batteries which, having withheld their fire, now opened upon us with forty guns........The noise was now deafening and the smoke blinding. Steering across the river to avoid if possible being sunk by Fort St. Philip, we suddenly found ourselves under the guns of Fort Jackson and its water batteries, and worse than all that had got somehow or other, headed down stream. 

It would now have been little short of madness to attempt turning around under such a fire and in a current running six miles an hour, so we were reluctantly compelled to steam down stream and get out of range as soon as possible."

For the next two years Walker served under Porter on the Mississippi River and its tributaries, very important and trying work. 

The Federal leaders were trying to cut all communication between the southern armies and the western slave states from which they received food and munitions, and the Federal vessels, being entirely in enemy country, were exposed, in addition to regular battles, to constant fire from the banks of the rivers where concealed sharpshooters fired upon passing vessels trying especially to pick off officers and pilots. 

Lieutenant Walker had a narrow escape from death when he once stepped into the pilot house of his vessel and found the iron shutters open. 

He reprimanded the pilot for this breach of regulations and reached out to pull down the shutter when he thought he saw something shine among the bushes on the bank; he stopped to look under the partly lowered shutter when a bullet hit its edge in front of his face and dropped harmlessly to the deck. in 

On June 11th, 1862, the Winona being then off Grand Gulf below Vicksburg, Lieutenant Walker was sent ashore in charge of two armed boats and fired the city of Grand Gulf in several places, and later in the month, the 26th, he ran past the forts at Vicksburg with Farragut's squadron, taking part in the continuous bombardment of the city, and after the rebel ram Arkansas came out of the Yazoo and passed down the river in July, ran back again past the batteries; this engagement the Winona was disabled and it was necessary to tow her to New Orleans for repairs. By July 1862 the only strong foothold retained by the Confederates on the Mississippi was Vicksburg, but this place was a serious impediment to the passage of the Mississippi by the Union vessels and the question of how to take this strong position was constantly under consideration by the Union leaders.

Walker was promoted to the rank of Lieutenant Commander in July and in August was detached from the Winona and ordered north; two months later he was sent to join Admiral Porter at Cairo, Illinois, the headquarters of the Mississippi squadron, where that officer was in command of a fleet of gunboats built in the upper Mississippi, and upon his arrival there, was ordered to Jeffersonville, Indiana, to relieve the commanding officer of the Chillicothe and report to Admiral Porter upon the "condition of that vessel for service, her capacity to endure a heavy fire her defects and what is required to make up deficiencies in every department. Also report to me if she can get down here through the canal". 

He was also to enlist men in the Naval Service. The report was most discouraging. Lieutenant Walker considered her vary weak; her deck leaked badly and, when underway, she leaked forward. The position of the wheel, in the gun tower interfered with the guns when in action, while at the same time the pilot could only see ahead, which for river work was a serious objection. Furthermore the pilots reported that she could not be taken through the Louisville canal and it would require a rise of six feet to take her over the falls. She was eventually put in commission but, owing to poor and hasty workmanship, was not always equal to the demands upon her. A week later Walker was ordered back to Cairo to take command of the Gunboat Baron de Kalb and get her ready for service "with all dispatch" and in November we find him in command of that vessel in Captain Walke's squadron at the mouth of the Yazoo, employed in preparing the way for attack on Haynes' Bluff on the Yazoo. In December Walker commanded an expedition, consisting of the Baron de Kalb and three other vessels, sent up the Yazoo by Captain Walke to take up torpedoes and make the river safe for the vessels of the projected attack, which duty was performed under heavy fire. His report to Captain Henry Walke, dated 

U. S. Gunboard Baron de Kalb, Mouth Yazoo River, December 23, 1862 gives some idea of the work constantly carried on by the navy in the Mississippi and its tributaries, when sharpshooters on the banks constantly tried to pick off officers or pilots, thus leaving the vessel temporarily helpless. "Sir: I have the honor to report that, in obedience to your order of the 

21st, I left here this morning in a reconnaissance up the Yazoo River, taking the ram Queen of the West, gunboat Signal, tugboat Laurel and this vessel. I saw nothing to excite suspicion until abreast of Johnson's 

Plantation, about a mile up the Yazoo proper, where I found buoys with wires attached. While examining and dragging with the boats of this vessel, they were fired upon with musketry from both banks. Proceeding with great caution I passed up the river to a point some twelve or fourteen miles from this place, under a galling fire of musketry from the right or west bank. At this point meeting with more torpedoes not safe to pass without dragging, and my orders being to go only ten or fifteen miles and to turn back at any apparent danger, I returned. When about a mile above Johnson's Plantation I met Lieutenant Commander Gwin with his expedition. By his order I returned to Johnson's Plantation and assisted Lieutenant Commander Shirk of the Lexington to drag the river and fire the buildings at this point. The buildings with a considerable quantity of cotton, corn fodder and farming tool, were entirely destroyed. 

I then returned to this place, arriving about 8 P. M. Our loss is one man killed and one severely wounded, both belonging to the tug. 

Lieutenant Commander Gwin mentioned to me his desire that I should remain with or return to the expedition. I shall be glad to do so, and think that I can be of service there.

Very respectfully, your obedient servant,

John G. Walker 

Lieutenant Commander, U. S. Navy" 

Admiral Porter reported to Secretary of the Navy Welles, dated "Yazoo River, December 31st, 1862......On the 27th instant, Lieutenant

Commander Walker of the Baron de Kalb had cleared the river Yazoo of torpedoes to the place nearly where the Cairo was sunk (twelve miles up) and had obtained possession of two landings. The enemy, to the number of two thousand, were contesting every inch of ground from rifle pits and from behind levees, where from high, overhanging banks, they could fire upon our vessels almost with impunity." 

On December 23rd, 1862 Admiral Porter, with eight vessels, started up the Yazoo to cooperate with the army at Haynes' Bluff and after three days incessant labor under musket fire from the banks the fleet came within range of the enemy's heavy batteries whose fire the Benton, the flagship, sustained for two hours. After the failure of the army to take the post an attack was planned on Arkansas Post un the Arkansas River by army and navy combined; there was a large rebel force stationed there and the place was being heavily fortified. For this attack Admiral Porter detailed the iron-clads De Kalb, Lieutenant 

Walker; Louisville, Lieutenant Commander Owen; and Cincinnati, Lieutenant

Bache; the ram Monarch, Colonel Eliot; the gunboats Black Hawk, Lieutenant Commander Breeze, and Tyler, Lieutenant Commander Shirk; and the tinclads Rattler, Lieutenant Commander Watson Smith, and Glide, Lieutenant

Woodworth, the troops accompanying them in transports. The expedition set out on January 4th, 1863 and so rapidly had the preparations been made that it arrived at the White River without the news of their coming having reached the rebels. It had been decided to go up the White River to deceive the enemy as to their objective and cross to the Arkansas by a cut off. No time was lost at the mouth of the White River and by evening of January 9th, 1863 the transports tied up to the bank three miles below Fort Hindman and all night and all the next day troops were landing. Fort Hindman at Arkansas Post was a strong work mounting besides some smaller pieces, eleven heavy guns, nine of which commanded the shallow channel in the river, here narrow and full of shoals. At five o'clock on the afternoon of the tenth, the army having marched up past the abandoned outworks of the fort, the three ironclads advanced to the attack, the Baron de Kalb, Lieutenant Commander Walker, leading, the Cincinnati and Louisville following. The fort opened fire on the boats as soon as they came opposite the range marks which the Confederates had put up, but the vessels did not return the fire until they were within seventy yards of the fort, when the superiority of the Federal fire was manifest. Darkness came on and the fire of the fort lessened very much; it finally became so dark and the smoke was so thick that it was impossible for either side to see anything, the enemy ceased firing and the vessels dropped down the river. The 

De Kalb had lost seventeen killed and wounded. The army me anwhile had made no move and allowed the rebels to work all night repairing the damage so that in the morning they were ready for another fight.

The attack of the gunboats had caused great havoc in the works, killing all the artillery horses in the rear, smashing in the casemates, killing twenty gunners and wounding many more. If the light had lasted a little longer, the fort would have been disabled altogether. At one o'clock on 

January 11th the fleet moved to a second attack in the same order as on the previous day, the De Kalb leading, opening fire at sixty yards. 

After a bombardment of two and one half hours, every gun in the fort was dismounted or disabled and the fort badly wrecked; not a gun was fired from the enemy and not a sharpshooter dared show his head on the water side, and the post surrendered to Porter and McClernand. This victory was of great importance to the northern cause, coming as it did when the Federal arms were unsuccesful on three or four battlefields; it rejoiced the Union men in the North to have one complete victory and the Navy might well feel proud of the share and that a large one that belonged to them. Walker was in command of the entire naval forces in this brilliant attack for which he received special mention. 

In his report to the Secretary of the Navy, Admiral Porter stated that "the condition of the fort showed the accuracy of fire by the gunboats and that those inside gave the Baron de Kalb, Lieutenant Commander Walker, the credit of doing the most execution......no fort ever received a worse battering and the effect upon its defenders is shown by their statement that no men could be expected to stand up against the fire of those gun boats. Just as the army was preparing to attack white flags were run up and the fort surrendered." 

On January 13th Admiral Porter sent the following special report to Secretary of the Navy, Gideon Welles, "Arkansas Post, January 13, 1863. Sir: The general report of an action embraces all those engaged in it, and although on this occasion the conduct of all the officers met with my approbation, I must give a little more credit to some than others. Lieutenant John G. Walker is a man of more than ordinary intelligence, with cool, calm judgment in time of action and one on whom the government can rely to perform any duty. He managed and fought his vessel most beautifully and I never had to correct a movement of his during the action. I look upon him as one of the most reliable officers in the service. The rebels admit that they never had such firing in their lives as came from the 10-inch guns of the Baron de Kalb, and I know of no instance on record where every gun in a fort was dismounted or destroyed."

The following letter from Lieutenant Commander Walker's friend and former shipmate, Dr. Arthur Mathewson, although of later date, is inserted here as it refers to this action: "Your letter of 

January 25th was a long time in reaching me, only coming last week, but very welcome at last. I had been longing to hear from you and especially after reading the accounts of the glorious affair at Arkansas Post in which you bore so conspicuous a part, and thank you sincerely for taking time so soon to write me about it. You may be assured that all your old messmates on the Winona felt proud of you on hearing what you had been doing, and everyone said they knew that was the way you would fight when you got a command. We knew from the number of your killed that the fighting had been severe but could only be surprised that it was not greater when we learned how

closely you had engaged the fort. Fighting heavy guns at sixty yards must be hot work but it is the way to win". 

On January 24th Admiral Porter sent a private letter to

Senator Grimes (who was at that time chairman of the Naval Committee of the Senate) from which the following interesting and amusing extract is taken "........ At Fort Hindman your nephew Walker distinguished himself highly. I refer you for particulars to my report to the Department, where he is particularly mentioned. This Fort Hindman affair had been the fairest stand up fight of the war. It was a naval fight altogether as regards the fort. The army did not assault, although I believe they tried it once and were replused. The Colonel commanding the fort surrendered his sword to me.

We are now opposite Vicksburg, with the whole army landed on the neck of land in front of the city. What they are doing there they only know. I suppose they will swim over when they are ready, and when they get in position, call upon the gun boats to go in and pick them out. 

Vicksburg is very strongly fortified in front and the forts are out of the reach of my guns. They are on the hilltops and we cannot even elevate that high. My plan is to work up the Yazoo and to get in there and for the army to come down the Yazoo, cut off supplies and attack their rear....... Certain it is that we will never take Vicksburg in front by looking at it across the river......I do not think this army good for an assault. They are too green. They have to be coaxed up to a rifle pit as Rarey coaxes his wild horses up to a drum. I do not think they would make a good business of assaulting Vicksburg in front across the river if they could not be brought to the assault of Fort 

Hindman by land".

Immediately after the fall of Fort Hindman Lieutenant Commander Walker received the following orders: "Ad Porter to J. G. Walker 

Arkansas Post, Jan. 12, 1863

Sir: 

You will proceed to White River near the cutt off where you will await the arrival of General Gorman and his troops. You will, when he arrives, proceed in advance of him up the White River, cleaning out 

St. Charles. The Cincinnati will accompany you. I need not tell you that close quarters will the soonest finish the work. Make the commander of the Chillicothe grease well his casemates. Co-operate with the army all you can, but, making your attack, do it in your own way. 

See that the defenses are demolished by our guns to let the troops go in easy. 

Respectfully your obedient servant (Signed) D. D. Porter"

They found St. Charles evacuated, the troops having left on the Blue Wing, taking two 8-inch guns with them. Walker pushed on to 

Devall's Bluff in the Baron de Kalb and found that the two 8-inch guns

were about to be put on the cars for Little Rock. The rebels had taken alarm and fled so suddenly that supper for the men was cooking and they had left blankets and all sorts of possessions behind them; he landed his men and took possession of all rebel property, including two 8-inch guns and carriages in good condition and many new Enfield rifles. 

Admiral Porter sent three swift light draft vessels to increase Lieutenant Commander Walker's force and with these and the Baron de Kalb he continued up the river in pursuit of the Blue Wing and to capture any other steamers that might be there. Captured Des Arc and before January 20th he had gone 50 miles up the river and besides the two 8-inch guns had taken 200 stands of arms with accoutrements and three platform cars, as well as seven prisoners and rebel mail. For his work in this expedition 

Walker was again especially recommended by Porter in his report to the 

Secretary of the Navy.

When at the end of January, 1863 General Grant took command of the army before Vicksburg, he and Admiral Porter planned an attack on that city from the rear. They decided to send an expedition into 

Tallahatchie through Yazoo Pass, a bayou which twenty years before connected the Mississippi and Coldwater Rivers, opening a way to the Tallahatahie and Yazoo for the use of small boats, but which had been unused for many years. Only one pilot could be found who had ever traveled that route but he thought the passage could be made practicable again, so it was decided to undertake it and on the second of February the work of cutting the levee was begun. The naval expedition commanded by Lieutenant Commander Watson Smith consisted of two ironclads, the De Kalb, the first ironclad to enter the pass, Lieutenant Commander Walker, and 

Chilliocothe, Lieutenant Commander Foster, with six tinclads, two rams and a light draft motor boat; each vessel was to carry one hundred soldiers besides their own crews and the plan was to push through rapidly and take Yazoo City by surprise; but it was soon discovered that the Confederates had blocked the way to Coldwater by felling trees on the banks of the pass and the difficulties encountered were far greater than were expected and a good deal of time was lost in cutting away the trees and removing the logs and debris that had collected during the past twenty years, although a party of six thousand soldiers was sent in to help with the work. Both soldiers and sailors worked with a will and though the workers were sometimes attacked by musketry from. the banks, the rebels having heard something going on there, the spirit of the men was of the best. The navigation of the Pass was very difficult, the current was like a mill race until the water reached its level, the stream was only wide enough to allow of the vessels proceeding in single file, and so winding that backing and checking with lines was the only means of rounding the numerous turns and avoiding collisions with the trees and shore; even with all possible care they had suffered, some having damaged wheels or having lost their smokestacks due to overhanging branches of the dense growth of trees on the banks. 

Admiral Porter relates that by the second day the vessels had only hulls and engines left and that had to suffice. 

In telling of his experiences on this trip Admiral Walker used to say that the vessels indulged in every sort of gymnastics except climbing the obstructing trees.

The expedition was to have been a surprise and was meant to reach points on the Tallahatchie before the enemy should have time to fortify them but owing to the very slow progress in the pass, sometimes not more than three miles a day and to a change in the programme about which Admiral Porter had not been consulted, they did not reach the Tallahatchies until March 6th, and its mouth four days later; and in the meantime the rebels had been warned of what was happening in the Pass and, although rather incredulous, had hastily put up a work of cotton bales and earth at the junction of the Tallahatchie and Yazoo which was armed and garrisoned by the time the Federal gunboats arrived, and named Fort Pemberton and, as further protection, a vessel had been sunk in the river as an obstruction. Here for several days, from the 

11th to the 16th of March, there was severe fighting between the fort and the De Kalb and Chillicothe, in which both vessels suffered severely, the De Kalb, Lieutenant Commander Walker, being struck six times, one shot going through the forward casemate iron. Walker complained bitterly of the closing of the vents of the 9-inch guns, never having more than one free and being obliged to devise means of blowing them out while under fire. Finding that the fire of the two ironclads alone was not enough to reduce the fort and the troops not finding firm ground for landing, it was decided to withdraw and on the 5th of April the expedition left, reaching the Mississippi on the 10th about two months after it had started. After this unsuccessful expedition there were no actions on the Yazoo for several months except a demonstration upon Drumgould's 

Bluff, during the attack on Grand Gulf on April 29th, where a Confederate

Lieutenant was taken prisoner, and this although carried out with great gallantry, was only a feint to deceive the Confederates. Lieutenant 

Breese wrote in his report to Secretary Welles "The De Kalb was fired on by the enemy's pickets when Captain Walker landed his boarders, and with the loss of only one wounded, captured a wounded lieutenant of the Third

Louisiana Regiment and killed a major and three privates; the others escaped", and to Admiral Porter, "The De Kalb was handled with the skill always exhibited by Lieutenant Commander Walker. The lieutenant captured by Captain Walker being wounded I turned over to General Sherman."

The fertile Yazoo valley was a rich storehouse of supplies for the Confederate armies which could be carried in small boats through the many small bayous or channels to Yazoo City and Vicksburg and many attempts were made to close this source of supplies. 

Porter now decided to send up the river an expedition consisting of the De Kalb, Choctaw and four light draft tinclads. This fleet proceeded to Haynes' Bluff, Walker leading in the De Kalb and found that the enemy had begun to evacuate the fortifications which were of great strength and the small force still there hastily left. 

Walker, with the DeKalb and Choctaw and three tinclads, now steamed up to Yazoo City, of which he took possession. The citizens evidently feared looting, the military authorities having evacuated the city, for they appointed a committee to act for them and to their communications 

Captain Walker sent the following reply:

Gentlemen: 

U.S.S. Baron DeKalb 

Yazoo City, May 21st, 1863

I have received your communication of this date enclosing your

appointment as a committee to act on this occasion. In reply I have to say that no indignity will be offered any citizen, and that I hope all will remain quietly in town. I shall expect that my officers and men meet with no insult or opposition. You will please appoint someone to point out all public property in town and the vicinity. The police of the town remains in the hands of the town authorities. 

Very Respct. &.

John G. Walker 

Lieut. Comdr. Senior Officer"

The large navy yard, the only one left to the rebels, was burned with its machine shops, three vessels in process of construction and stores of lumber. In hospital were 115 soldiers who were too ill to move and were paroled. On May 24th another expedition under 

Lieutenant Commander Walker, consisting of the DeKalb and four smaller vessels started up the Yazoo. His report to Admiral Porter tells of the doings of the vessels. 

"Baron DeKalb, Mouth of Yazoo River, June 1, 1863. 

Sir: 

I have the honor to report that I left this place on the morning of the twenty fourth of May, with the De Kalb, Forest Rose, Linden, Signal and Petrel. I pushed up the Yazoo as speedily as possible for the purpose of capturing or destroying the enemy's transports in the river. The Signal knocked down her smokestacks and returned the same night. Leaving the DeKalb with orders to come on as fast as possible, I pushed on with the Forest Rose, Linden and Petrel to within about 

15 miles of Fort Pemberton, when I found the steamers John Walsh, R. J.

Lockland, Golden Age and Scotland sunk on à bar, completely blocking it up. Failing in my efforts to make a passage through the blockade, I fired them, destroying all but such parts of the hulls as were under water. These steamers were fine boats, in good order, and if I had had the means, could have been raised and saved. I remained at that point during the night, and the next morning at daylight was attacked by a force of the enemy, but after a sharp fire of a few minutes they beat a hasty retreat. Our only loss was two men, belonging to the Petrel, wounded. Returning down the Yazoo, I burned a large sawmill, twentyfive miles above Yazoo City. At Yazoo City I landed and brought away a large quantity of bar, round and flat, iron from the navy yard. 

Arriving at the mouth of the Big Sunflower, I proceeded up that river about 150 miles, until stopped by shoal water. At Indian Shoot I sent 

Volunteer Lieutenant George W. Brown of the Forest Rose with boats through Rolling Fork. He found a quantity of corn belonging to the rebels, which he burned. At the mouth of Bayou Quiver (Quiver River ?) hearing of steamers, I sent Lieutenant Brown, with the boats of the Forest Rose and Linden, up after them. Ascending 10 miles he burned the Dewdrop and Emma Bett. The Linden burned the Argo in a small bayou about 75 miles up Sunflower. I also found the Cotton Plant sunk in 

Lake George with nothing out of water but the tips of her smokestacks.

At Garvin's Landing on the Sunflower found, and brought away, a cutter which was lost on the Deer Creek".

At this time Captain Walker had command of the Naval Forces in the Yazoo. Admiral Porter telegraphs General Grant "Captain Walker has command at Haynes Bluff and will give any convoy required". A report of 

Lieutenant Murphy to Admiral Porter on June 1st, 1863, in which he tells of remaining at Perkins Landing to protect a force of three hundred men under Colonel Owen until the arrival of a transport which would enable him to evacuate, and repulsing an attack of 1400 Confederates continues, "The picket guard under the command of Captain Walker who was left in the escape of our forces, was rescued" and this may perhaps be the occasion of which the Admiral used to tell, when he was riding through woods with his pistol in his riding boct, having no idea the enemy was so near, and suddenly rode into a rebel picket of several men; all he could do was drive spurs into his horse and rush through the woods as fast as possible, hoping to get away before the rebels recovered from their surprise, in which he was successful. On June 25th Walker relieved Lieutenant Commander Selfridge in command of the battery of naval siege guns in the 15th Army Corps before Vicksburg, two 8-inch columbiads, which had been asked for by the army and placed by General Sherman "nearly on the extreme right, not far from the river"; here he remained conducting the firing with marked ability until the surrender of the city on July 4th. 

After the fall of Vicksburg, Lieuteneant Commander Walker proceeded up the Yazoo, to co-operate with Major General Herron in the expedition to 

Yazoo City. I give Walker's report to Admiral Porter. "Vicksburg, July 22, 1863.

Sir: I have the honor to report that I left this place on the 

12th, with the DeKalb, Kenwood, Signal and New National, to cooperate with the force under General Herron, in an expedition to Yazoo City. 

Arriving near Yazoo City on the afternoon of the 13th, I steamed up within range of the enemy's works to reconnoitre, drawing the fire of six guns in position. Having ascertained the force of the enemy, I dropped out of fire to give General Herron time to land his troops. 

Before the troops were all landed, the enemy began evacuating his works. I immediately steamed up to prevent the removal of guns, while the troops advanced along the shore. When abreast of the Navy 

Yard at the lower end of the town, the DeKalb, leading, struck a torpedo which, exploding, caused the sinking of the vessel in about fifteen minutes;nc one on board was injured by the explosion. Before sinking the vessel was made fast to the bank and the small arms and much of the movable property saved. On the fourteenth I commenced getting out the battery and on the twenty-first got out the last gun. 

An 8-inch columbiad, four rifled thirty pounders and a howitzer with about two hundred and fifty prisoners captured. The steamer 

St. Mary was captured, and five of the finest steam boats in the west burned to prevent their falling into our hands. The names of the steamboats burned were, the Magneta, Magnolia, Prince of Wales, Pargoud and Peytona."

The torpedo tore away about two feet of the vessel's port

bow, and as she was sinking another exploded under her stern. Many of the crew were bruised by the concussion, which was severe, but fortunately no one was seriously hurt, but officers and men lost everything. General Herron reports to Lieutenant Colonel Rawlins "to the coolness and efficiency of Captain Walker and his subordinate officers may be attributed the fact that none was drowned." 

"To Lieutenant Commander J. G. Walker U.S.S. Black Hawk, Vicksburg, Mississippi 

New York, August 11, 1863 

My dear Walker

I was delighted a few days ago in the receipt of your favor of 28 July. The torpedoing of the DeKalb was published in the city papers almost as soon as it occurred, the telegraph despatch to the associated press said "Nobody injured" consequently the friends of her daring commander felt comfortable as far as he was concerned and regretted the occurance only for the gratification it probably afforded the Rebels and the loss of the steamer when most needed......It looks to me as though the fighting was pretty well worked out on the Mississippi; not so in the east. You western fighters will have to transfer your metal to Virginia and South Carolina before we shall see the beginning of the end of the Rebellion.... 

Truly your friend 

James McBride"

On August 5th, 1863, Lieutenant Commander Walker started up the Yazoo in the Rattler to raise the DeKalb; a letter from Admiral 

Porter told him to take the Champion as soon as she had finished pumping out the Cincinnati, using her on the way up to transport troops, which General Grant would supply to hold Yazoo City while the work on the DeKalb was being carried on; also to carry with him everything he could possibly need, tackle, old canvas, tar, planks, etc. and to complete the job as soon as possible and return to Vicksburg as he wanted to fit out an expedition to proceed up the Red River. The work probably took longer than was expected for on August 12th Admiral Porter writes "As soon as you get through the Yazoo business, report to me at 

Cairo, or wherever you may find me." However, when he left the Yazoo, where he had dstroyed shipping and supplies valued at $2,000,000 he was detached from the Mississippi squadron and went home to Burlington, Iowa, on leave, via the Mississippi; he was in Cairo from September 

10th to 14th as witness on court martial proceedings and upon returning to Burlington found orders to proceed to Boston and report for temporary duty at the Navy Yard there. 

Walker fully expected to return to the Mississippi aquadron upon the expiration of this temporary duty and Admiral Porter expected him as the following letter shows.

21 

Cairo, November 11, 1863 

Dear Walker 

Your vessel, the iron clad ram Oichita (lately the Louisville) will soon be ready for service; 400 men are at work on her. She will mount six 30 pounder rifles, twelve 24 pounder howitzers and ten 12 pound howitzers, in all 28 guns. Now if you have arranged that little affair with the young lady I heard you were "sweet" on, you will let the Secretary of the Navy know that you are ready to apply for duty and come out and take charge of the Oichita and have what is left of her fixed to suit you. We are all well. Breese has gone on a trial trip with (word omitted), the last I heard of them they had purchased four new steamers and would soon make up the bakers dozen I sent them to get; the iron clads will be coming down in the shoals soon and I think we can keep the Frenchmen out of the Mississippi. 

Yours in haste 

D. D. Porter"

But in December came orders to be ready to command the Saco, then preparing for sea at the Boston Navy Yard and on January 6th, 1864 he took command of her, still being fitted for sea; this took much time for at the end of August she was not yet ready but probably sailed early in September.

The service of the Navy in the Mississippi and its tributaries was of a most trying kind; the forces were in the midst of the enemy's country, exposed to summer heats to which the Northerners were wholly unaccustomed and to malaria which was rampant along the rivers and from which Captain Walker suffered for years after the war. In the raids up the smaller rivers they were constantly exposed to the fire of the sharpshooters. The fighting, also, consisted not of isolated battles but besides the more important engagements there was almost constant fighting of small units in the numerous rivers and bayous. What Admiral Porter thought of it is shown in his report to Secretary of the Navy Welles, dated 19th May, 1864. "I do not believe that a larger amount of work was ever accomplished than that which has been performed by the Navy in these waters it has been unceasing and laborious. Many of the deeds performed by the different commanders would in ordinary times be considered acts of the most gallant kind. A distance of three thousand miles has been opened and a large amount of territory placed in our hands, owing to the support given to our Army by the Navy, and the individual energy of the officers herein mentioned. My reports will bear witness to the ability zeal and gallantry displayed on all occasions. There has been no instance yet where a promotion has taken place in this squadron, amongst the regular officers, for gallant conduct, while the volunteers have been frequently advanced. Permit me, then, to urge the promotion of the following officers, which they all richly deserve." Then follows a list of fourteen names, that of Lieutenant Commander Walker among them.

When at last the Saco put to sea Commander Walker cruised along the coast as far as Halifax in search of the Confederate S. S. 

Tallahassee and on the lookout for any blockade runners but saw none although Halifax pilots who boarded the Saco reported that that port was full of them; during the cruise he spoke a number of English vessels and chased the Royal Mail Steamers Arabia and Alpha and two English men-of-war until assured of their character. Unfortunately the 

Saco proved very faulty, the boilers, condensers and feed pump all gave trouble so that it was necessary to return to Boston to have her put into the necessary repair. This was annoying to officers and men anxious to make a success of their work as well as to the government. "D. Walker

What is the matter with the Saco? Twelve days to repair is reported, then come light nights off Halifax and it will be rather late. 500 bales of cotton are going into Halifax per day and I was in hopes you would catch at least one of them. The City of Petersburg went in there ten days ago with 1800 bales. The Captain who could have captured her would have received $80,000. Two more vessels from Wilmington loaded with cotton reached there last night. 

I hope you have made a full report about your vessel and her engine and given her qualities under sail her draft of water, how she steers as to her apartments, battery, rolling, pitching, speed, crew, officers, etc. etc. 

Truly yours (Signed) G. W. Fox"

When early in October 1864 Admiral Porter was ordered to command the Naval expedition against Fort Fisher Lieutenant Commander Walker was one of the five officers formerly serving under him in the Mississippi whom he asked to have attached to his command and a telegram from the Assistant of the Navy on October 8th ordered him to take the Saco to Beaufort, North Carolina to join Admiral Porter. It is evident from the following letter to Admiral Porter that orders reached him on his way south. "U.S.S. Saco, Hampton Roads, Virginia 

December 16, 1864 

Sir: I have to report that in obediance to your order I left this place on the evening of the 13th for Beaufort, North Carolina. 

On the following day at three P. M. being about thirty miles north of 

Hatteras a part of the tubes in the third boiler on the port side blew out under a pressure of twenty-five pounds of steam, blowing the fire across the fireroom and filling it with steam and ashes. The fires were hauled but as soon as possible steam got on the boilers still remaining serviceable, five being entirely disabled and others leaking, and I continued on my course until nine P. M. At that time finding that with all the steam we could raise the ship barely had steerage way, and the wind serving to return to this place, I felt compelled most reluctantly to put

the ships head to the northward. I succeeded in reaching Hampton Roads under sail with a favorable breeze, working the engines as well as we could, at twelve last night. The vessel is entirely unseaworthy and in my opinion cannot be made serviceable without extensive repairs and alterations. I am much chagrined at this occurrance and most earnestly beg to offer the services of myself, officers and men for any service in the present expedition. Enclosed I forward a copy of the Engineer's report. 

This forced return to Hampton Roads was a great disappointment to all on board as they had hoped to take part in an attack upon Fort Anderson and the other rebel batteries on the Cape Fear River which seemed to promise success.

On January 9th, 1865 Walker was ordered to command the Shawmut, a vessel with which he was pleased as she was considered by Admiral Porter to be far the best vessel of her class in the Navy and probably quite as fast as, if not faster than, any in the service. 

He accordingly took command at the Portsmouth Navy Yard on January 13th. After completing the loading of stores, the Shawmut sailed on the 19th for Hampton Roads, Virginia arriving on the 22nd and sailed once more two days later for Fort Fisher where he reported to Admiral Porter on the 25th of January. In Company with Admiral Porter's squadron, the Shawmut proceeded up the Cape Fear River and spent the next month trying to reduce the Confederate batteries, especially Fort Anderson on the Neuse opposite New Berne and garrisoned by 300 men. 

Abstract of the log of the U.S.S.Shawmut, Lieutenant Commander Walker shows the following entry. "February 3, 1865. At eleven 

A.M. Admiral Porter came aboard. at 3:50 the steamer Tacony took her anchor and went up the river ahead of us and at 3:55 we engaged the rebel Fort Anderson and the rebels returned the fire......At 4:50 went to quarters with 30 pounder gun and fired twelve rounds at the rebel works. They opened on us and fired four rounds which came over us and fell a ship's length from us. "February 18. 

At 11:50 A.M. took up anchor and went up to engage Fort Anderson with 100 pounder, 30 pounder and 4-inch gun; at 

12 meridian engagement going on; at 2:45 retired from action. Ammunition expended. "February 20........at 3:30 P.M. 

Admiral Porter came on board. We hove up anchor and proceeded up river about two miles and came to anchor.......At 4:45 got our anchor and steamed ahead, came to anchor at 5 P. M. and engaged rebel battery; stopped firing at 5:45

P. M. ...... At ten P. M. lookout forward saw something floating down, it being ebb tide; all hands called to quarters by order of the captain. Saw other vessels ahead of us firing. Called away the first cutter; Acting Engsign William B. Trufant went in her and examined the objects, there being a large number in sight. The gig was called away, Mr. Davis went in her and captured five of them, which proved to be torpedoes; hauled them up to the vessel and made them fast astern. In trying to sink one of them by a shot from a pistol firing into it from the first cutter, exploded it and killed two men and wounded Mr. Trufant in the head and arm. All boats called away to pick up and save what we could.

The coxwain of the gig was lost overboard and a seaman, James Cobb, killed in the boat. At midnight hoisted all boats and piped down."

On February 25 the Shawmut dropped back down the river to Fort Fisher and on the 28th, sailed for Hampton Roads to take part in the campaign to reduce Richmond. Arriving on the 3rd of March, she proceeded up the James River where she spent ten days returning then to sail up the York and Pamaunkey Rivers. Getting far up the Pamaunkey the Shawmut grounded on a bar and after trying vainly for three days to float her, Commander Walker found it necessary to transfer all her stores and ammunition to another vessel in order to get her off the bar. On the 24th of March the Shawmut returned to Hampton Roads and proceeded thence to New York for repairs made necessary by her engagements with the Confederate batteries and by her grounding.

On April 7, 1865, upon the termination of the war, the Shawmut still being under repair in the New York yard Lieutenant Commander Walker was detached from her and place on "waiting orders" which gave him the opportunity to go to Burlington, Iowa, to visit his uncle Grimes before being ordered to the Shawmut again on the 25th. On May 28th of that year, upon his return from a trip to Boston, he received a notification from the Navy Department that his vessel was to go on foreign service and to prepare her for a two years cruise. This news determined him to "put his fortune to the touch" and he wrote to his future wife that same day. "I have known you for four years, I commenced by admiring, and that feeling has gradually strengthened and deepened....I went to Boston to tell you this and ask you to be my wife, but my heart failed me and I came as I went. While the war lasted I felt that I had no right to hazard your happiness, or impair my own usefulness to the government by anything of this kind, but now that the rebellion is fairly at an end and, I trust, the last gun fired, I have determined to tell you and know my fate......" 

On June 3rd he became engaged to Miss Rebecca White Pickering, the daughter of Henry White and Frances Dana (Goddard) Pickering of Boston.

As the Shawmut was fitting out at the Brooklyn Navy Yard Captain Walker must of necessity spend most of his time in New York and Brooklyn, making only one visit to Boston. To his fiancee he wrote, "Do you know I tremble some times when I think......how totally unfitted

I am to make your happiness. I have passed my life since boyhood without a home, moving about the world as the wants of the service sent me and almost as much at home in one part of the world as another, feeling that I stood alone, or nearly so, and shutting my feelings in my own breast until I have grown a cold, quiet man, selfish and incapable of showing the strength of my feelings and affections." "June 13, 1865.......I have been strongly tempted during the last few days to arrange for giving up my vessel and going to the Naval School. If I had not asked to go in command of her, I should do it. I hope you will be able to go to Europe this fall with some of your friends that in that way I may see some thing of you."

June 18, 1865. "It appears that an application was made to the Navy Department to send me to the Academy for duty. Mr. Fox must have written to Mr. Grimes about it as he says that he put his veto on it. This of course was done before he knew anything of our arrangements, but I wish he had left the matter alone. You see it is a bad thing sometimes to have friends. Admiral Porter is to take command of the Academy and our old Mississippi River party will, I suppose, be pretty strongly represented."
