The Shawmut went into commission on the fifteenth of June, but there was a long delay about her sailing orders, which Captain Walker was very anxious to receive as the cruise would begin only when they put to sea, and lying at anchor in the North River during the hot weather, annoyed by flies and mosquitoes was trying. Early in July he was ordered to hurry with the Shawmut to Harts Island to stop anticipated trouble between regiments of returned troops, but found everything as quiet as possible and after two days returned to the North River. At last on 

July 29th orders were received to join Admiral Goldsborough's squadron in Europe and on August 3rd they at last put to sea to the great relief of Captain Walker who then felt that he knew what he had to do and could do it. He went in to Halifax to fill up with coal before crossing the ocean and he had his first experience of the official visits required of a commanding officer; the governor must be called on and the senior 

British Naval Officer and later their return visits must be received with all due formality. While in this port Captain Walker met in the street a former officer of the U.S.S. Susquehanna who resigned soon after that vessel reached Boston in 1861 and went South to join the rebels; they talked for a few minutes but finding that he was as furious a rebel as ever Captain Walker soon left him. 

The much coveted cruise in European waters to which Captain Walker had looked forward with pleasure, proved, to his great disappointment, unexpectedly short, but he took his vessel to Antwerp where three days would be needed for work on board besides coaling and during that time he visited Paris before receiving orders detaching him from Admiral Goldsborough's squadron and ordering him to the South American station, but until he reported to Admiral Godon in command there he was his own master and could stop at what ports he pleased on his way. After going into Cherbourg to get mail, he went to Lisbon but found the city uninteresting with little to see in the way of art or places of interest. 

He received a call from the Captain of a Portugese sloop of war - "He spoke no English, I no Portugese and the mixture of French, Spanish, English and Portugese was something fearful. He understood a little 

French and Spanish, about as much as I, and when either of us got stuck he fell back upon his native tongue and profound bows. To a looker on it must have been a funny performance. However, we got on very well and he went away much pleased. I don't know which got ahead in the way of compliments, perhaps as he could talk faster than I, but as he was a little short fat fellow I could beat him all to pieces bowing.

After staying a half hour I bowed him over the side and into his boat and thought it was over, but the fellow wasn't satisfied and when a hundred yards clear of the ship pulled off his hat and gave me another. 

The executive officer was on deck with me and we both responded and he gave it up in despair. I suppose he thought the odds were goo great."

Letter of September 9th, 1865 from the Shawmut at Lisbon..... 

"When you were at Annapolis in 1856 I was there being examined for promotion and commonly supposed to be in a fair way of dying within a year or two of consumption.......I wonder what we would have thought of each other if we had met then? You would probably not have fancied the tall thin invalid as a companion for life, and love and marriage was far from my thoughts at that time. Then, although I had been so long at school, I never had been in a house in the town and knew none but the officers and midshipmen of the Academy with whom I was brought in contact. I led a solitary life and often think of it now. I was being treated by the surgeon who, with everyone else, thought I was to live but a short time. As a matter of course I was low spirited, not able to drill with my class and little inclined to take part in any of the amusements of my classmates but was much by myself and studied hard, more for the pleasure that I knew it would give Mr. Grimes if I graduated at the head of my class than because I cared much for it myself. dreary time and I believe I had more patience then than now to get on as well as I did." At Madeira he passed a few very pleasant days, meeting some old friends who were very hospitable. "I was dined and wined, rode on horseback and enjoyed the beauties of the island, but as I was under orders to this squadron (South American) after twelve days I........tore myself away from Funchal." A stop of a day at

Porto Grande (St. Vincent) to coal, was less pleasant and all on board were glad to leave as soon as possible. "The island is absolutely baked, sometimes rain does not fall for two or three years. During the last year they have had two light falls of rain.......Its only importance is from the fact of the harbor being a very good one and its being a coaling station for steamers." The town and negro inhabitants were dirty, the only whites being a few government officials and Englishmen belonging to the coaling station. From there he proceeded directly to Rio de Janeiro and reported to Admiral Godon on the 26th of October, 24 days from Madeira, including the stop at Porto Grande. 

Here he could receive letters from home in 31 or 32 days but hoped he should not be kept there long as he had found Montevideo a much pleasanter town when on this station in the Falmouth in 1857-8 and hoped to be sent to that city and to be able to make a trip from there some seven or eight hundred miles up the Parana River to Asuncion, the capital of Paraguay. He wrote home that "as there is quite an extensive war going on I may perhaps have an opportunity to see some powder burned. 

It will be rather an odd thing to look on and think I have nothing to do with it and that it really is nothing to me which whips - very safe though". While the Shawmut was still at Rio de Janeiro the Emperor returned from the seat of war and there was a three day celebration over it. "The city was illuminated, all work stopped and everybody was in the streets at night in what our backwoodsmen would call his store clothes.

I have never seen in Brazil such crowds, in some parts of the city the streets, which are very narrow, were almost impassible. During one evening the Emperor and Empress strolled about the streets arm in arm, to the great delight of the Barzilians who wouldn't let them do it quietly but shouted and cheered furiously and follwed them in a large crowd." 

After about four weeks in Rio, Captain Walker was ordered to Santa Caterina to fill up with coal; he was glad to move southward although he found Rio much improved since his last visit and at Santa

Caterino besides coal there was an excellent place to drill the men and good fishing and shooting for pastime. After taking in her supply of coal, the Shawmut set out in search of the Madeiras Rock supposed to be a small uncharted rock, just awash and reported to have been seen by two or three vessels. The search proved unsuccessful and after five very disagreeable days, the wind blowing half a gale, making it impossible to see the rock and increasing the chance of running into it and losing the ship, Captain Walker gave up the search and started for Mont Video under sail alone although he knew it would be a slow trip as the Shawmut was not half sparred for sailing; it would, however, be good training for the men and make the trip an inexpensive one, and all vessels were expected to use sails except in case of emergency, for the sake of economy.

Captain Walker found few old acquaintances of 1859 in Mont Video, but it was as pleasant a city as ever and he enjoyed the evenings in the Plaza eating ices, listening to a good band and watching the handsomely dressed ladies and pomatummed and waxed gentlemen who gathered there to listen to the music and flirt. 

By the middle of January, 1866 Captain Walker was at Buenos Ayres with the Shawmut. He enjoyed the town, being made honorary member of the club where he met pleasant men, could read the papers and was quite ready to spend a month or even two in that port. The trip up the 

Parana to Asuncion had to be given up for the time being as the allies had blocked the river at Corrientes and allowed no one to pass above that point. Memories of a visit to Corrientes just seven years before, the great heat and sand flies by day and mosquitoes at night, did not tempt Captain Walker to go up the river to that point although he would have been glad to visit Asuncion if that had been possible. The time at Buenos Ayres passed pleasantly; although most people were out of town for the summer, there was horseback riding and occasional picnics in the country.

Captain Walker was much pleased to hear from Senator Grimes that he had been shown confidentially his name on a list of officers recommended by the "Board of Admirals" for promotions; it was very gratifying to him as it was "a thing only to be got by service under fire". As these promotions had to be confirmed by Congress much time passed before they actually took place but this is perhaps as good a place as any to insert Senator Grimes' letters to Miss Pickering giving her the news which he knew would please her so much. "United States Senate Chamber 

July 24, 1866 

My dear Miss Pickering 

I have just received yours of the 22nd inst. and am now acknowledging its reception in the Senate Chamber with the thermometer at about 930. I should not perhaps do so so promptly were it not to give you the pleasure of letting you know that within the next fortyeight hours John will probably be promoted to the rank of Commander in

the Navy. I was shown this morning at the Navy Department a list of those who are to be sent to the Senate for promotion for distinguished services in the war and I am happy to be able to say that John's name was among them. I am still happier to be able to say that everybody recognizes the fact that if anyone deserves promotion he does." 

Letter to Miss Rebecca W. Pickering from 

Senator James W. Grimes. "Senate Chamber, July 27, 1866

I know it will please you to know that John has been promoted to the rank and pay of a Commander in the Navy. No officer in forty years has received a higher lift in rank says Admiral Porter and I think I am justified in saying that it is the uniform opinion of his profession that if anyone has deserved this sort of promotion no one deserves it more than he does. Certain I am that he has not secured it though any exertion of mine or by my influence.......I trust that the Rio steamer may arrive freighted with joy to you and satisfaction to me before this shall reach you."

While the Shawmut was in Buenos Ayres the President of Argentine Republic was at the front with the army. 

On February 22nd all the vessels of war in the harbor and many of the merchant vessels were dressed with flags in honor of 

Washington's birthday and at noon salutes were fired by Brazilian and

Italian vessels and also a shore battery. Captain Walker wrote home "I find the people of this part of South America enthusiastic in their admiration of the United States, which they rank above all other nations, and strongly in favor of the Monroe Doctrine. They don't like the idea of European governments interfering on this continent. I am directed to be as attentive as possible and offer any civilities in my power, to endeavor to make a favorable impression and do all that I can to increase the good feeling now prevailing." Naval officers have been much used diplomatically as they can be sent to any part of the world in the regular course of their duty and without causing the comment that would be evoked by the sending of a special envoy; it is also possible, if thought necessary, to repudiate their actions as unauthorized. 

During Admiral Walker's lifelong service in the Navy he frequently was sent upon diplomatic errands. It was once said by 

James G. Blaine, (many years later), "Walker is Grimes' nephew (Senator James Wilson Grimes of Iowa) and Grimes knew politics." 

In March Captain Walker accepted the invitation of a gentleman to go to Colonia about fifty miles up the river from Beunos Ayres and to visit his Quinta at Higeuritas to shoot, promising him a pair of young ostriches for pets, a gift of rather doubtful value, they might become white elephants. As no American vessel had visited either place since before the war, Admiral Goden authorized the trip and the Shawmut went to Colonia where she remained for two days experiencing

a severe pamperos. "At Colonia we had one of the pamperos of this country and one of the heaviest I have ever experienced. It swept over this section of the country with great fury, causing great destruction of property and, it is said, quite a number of lives. I was on shore when I saw it coming and at once started for the ship. 

When I left the beach, I had about a fourth of a mile to pull and saw that it was dangerous to attempt it, but felt that I ought to be on board, and thanks to a fast boat and good crew, reached the ship just as the storm struck her. In an instant it was so dark that faces could be distinguished but a few feet and I was obliged to hold on to stand on deck. The sails were all snugly furled but the wind tore one loose and in less time that I take to tell you it was split into a thousand pieces and flying to leeward before the gale. The strong canvas was like a spider's web when exposed to the wind. In about an hour the wind commenced going down and in two hours it was as pleasant as ever." Captain 

Walker found his stay of a week at Higueritas with Mr. and Mrs. Zimmerman very pleasant; Mrs. Zimmerman's father was governor of Buenos Ayres and both he and his daughter felt an enthusiastic admiration for the United States, in fact there was a very wide spread friendship and admiration for that nation throughout the country. 

In May he was sent on a more distinctly diplomatic errand of which he wrote home; "Friday, May 18, 1866.........I am to sail on 

Sunday or Monday for Saint Catherines and thence to Rio. I don't like the St. Catherines business a bit. There has been some trouble there about seamen deserting from American whale ships and enlisting in the Brazilian service, and also about Brazilian subjects serving in American whalers. The Consul, whom the Admiral calls an ass, has made a muddle of it and I am being sent up to see if I can straighten matters. I suppose the Governor of the province, with whom I shall have to deal, will be sure to draw me into correspondence, and this thing of writing intensely civil letters, quoting law and talking about the rights of belligerents is not much in my line. Our consul there is not at all liked by the authorities and can do nothing. My hope is that as there will be no personal feeling in dealing with me I may be able to get the matter into shape by interviews with the Governor." After a talk of an hour with the Governor and a courteous explanation of the stand taken by the United States in such cases, that they fully recognized the right of his government to the military services of its citizens and that a demand for any citizen of Brazil, liable to military service, found on board an American vessel would be at once complied with, but that the United States expected the same fair dealing from his government, that deserters from American vessels upon being identified and demanded should be surrendered, the Governor fully admitted that this position was correct. Captain Walker's report to Admiral Godon upon this matter closes, "I see no reason to doubt that the men would have been returned to their ships if the matter had been properly handled at the time, although, as they are much in want of troops, the Brazilian authorities cannot be expected to show any great willingness to discharge men."

The Governor visited the Shawmut, spending a couple of hours on board and offered to supply her with coal, provisions, or supplies of any

kind needed, while every attention and courtesy was shown to Captain 

Walker by the authorities.

With the middle of April there had come a sudden change of weather such as is more commonly associated with North America. "Winter is coming on and beginning to make itself felt now......Cold weather is very uncomfortable in small vessels as there is no way of warming them and one is always cold and uncomfortable. 

Captain Walker's trip to St. Catherine's in May, 1866 which has been referred to before was made under sail alone in three and one half days from Monte Video. They had very heavy weather for two days, splitting foresale, fore trysail and fore topsail (close reefed) while running before the sail. "After losing the square sails I scudded under fore trysail and fore staysail, the wind a little on the port quarter and no vessel could have behaved better.......I left the United States with but one topsail and course of No. 6 canvas which have now been in use for two years. I have repaired them with boats sails and hatch hoods (having no canvas) for temporary service and for exercising in port, but the sails are so worn and rotted that they cannot be trusted at sea."

At the request of the American Consul at St. Catherines seven destitute American seamen were taken back to Rio de Janeiro as passengers on the Shawmut as there was no American shipping then in that port and leaving them would be in fact forcing them into the Brazillian army, while from Rio they could ship for home. 

Captain Walker had been anxious to be ordered home as he planned to be married upon the expiration of the cruise, and in June orders reached the squadron in South American waters detaching Lieutenant Commander Walker from that squadron and ordering him to report to the Naval Academy at Annapolis for duty there. Admiral Godon in forwarding the orders, wrote "It is not without much regret that I have learned that you are to be removed from the squadron under my command where you have, for a long time, commanded the Shawmut with credit to yourself and advantage to the service. The state of efficiency and discipline in which you have always maintained your vessel would speak well for any commander, and especially so for one so young as yourself." He was also thoughtful enough to so indorse the orders as to give Captain Walker sea pay to the last minute. 

These orders were received very gladly for while he had at first hoped to reach home about a year from the time the Shawmut sailed from New York, there had been so much delay and so many rumors that the vessel would be kept at sea much longer than had at first been expected that Captain Walker had almost given up hope of reaching home before the next spring. His relief was great when the orders actually arrived and he wrote home of his happiness that his long engagement would soon be ended.

Now that he was ordered home and to pleasant duty at the Naval Academy plans could be made for his marriage to Miss Pickering before taking up his duties there at the beginning of the academic year.

Admiral Porter was then in command of the Academy and when Captain Walker went down to Annapolis to report he found work already under way on a house which the Admiral was having fitted up for him and his bride. The marriage took place at Greenbank, the Roxbury home of Miss 

Pickering's father, on the twelfth of September and after a short wedding journey the young couple went to Annapolis and settled in a comfortable house in "officer's row".

The three years of duty at the Naval Academy at Annapolis were very pleasant and happy ones. Upon his arrival at his post Commander 

Walker was assigned as Assistant to the Superintendent, and thus was brought into very close and intimate touch with Admiral Porter, the Superintendent at that time, under whom he had served so long during the war, first in the western rivers and later in the North Atlantic Blockading Squadron, and who had always had a high opinion of the young officer's ability. A number of officers who had served under Admiral 

Porter during the war were gathered at the Academy in various positions and the closeness of association in the small society at the post laid the foundation of several warm and lifelong friendships between the families assembled there.

Commander Walker's activities were many and varied, from showing young midshipmen how they could have their uniforms repaired and so save themselves the disbursement from their small pay of a pretty large sum for new ones, to plans for better administration of the Academy. His advice and help were frequently sought by officers in both professional and personal matters and his ability, and his readiness to give time and labor to help his friends or any cause that seemed to him worthy were recognized by all who knew him, and all his associates knew that Admiral Porter thought highly of his opinion on naval matters. The letters received by Walker at this time from people asking his help are amusingly varied. One man wanted his approval of a new device or method of firing the furnaces of men-of-war in order to reduce the engine room force; an officer asked him to use his influence to allow him to accept a French medal, and so on through a wide range of interests. In a letter written at this time he says, "I have a finger in everything going on from building the Chapel, down."

At this time came the first of many efforts of the staff officers of the Navy to induce Congress to give them "absolute rank" and do away with the titles of Surgeon, Paymaster and Engineer, substituting therefor the titles belonging to the "line" or fighting force. This was strongly opposed by the whole "line", not because of jealousy, as was sometimes claimed, but for several reasons, the principal one being that the changed titles would be misleading and without meaning, that the "line" was a distinctly combating force, educated and trained for handling men and vessels in battle and their titles were distinctly military and should always indicate men trained for war. On the other hand the members of the various staff corps, valuable and necessary as they were, had received no military training and were not fitted in any way to take the place of line officers and should therefor be content with "relative" rank and pay. A bill was passed by Congress establishing positions of higher rank in the staff corps, the officers filling which positions were to serve on shore only, to avoid possibility of serving on board ship

"All with commanding and executive officers their juniors in rank, a situation, it was felt, which would not conduce to good discipline. 

An anonymous article published at this time ends with the following words: this talk about injustice to individuals and corps is sheer nonsense. 

Let each person set to work and, by devotion to his duties, make his particular title or position as honorable and distinguished as possible. 

The words Surgeon, Paymaster, Engineer, are as good words as Commodore,

Captain, or any other words you may choose to associate with them. It rests with those designated by these titles to make them as distinguished as any others."

The life at the Academy was very informal and pleasant, with much neighborly intercourse among the officers and their families and many simple good times. The young officers formed a baseball nine called the "Silver Grays" and played the midshipmen; there were dances, amateur theatricals and of course the full dress parade of the battalion of midshipmen; with the big military band, brought together most of the little colony each day. With June came a time of great gaiety, when the official "visitors" came to Annapolis to inspect the Academy and there were many festivities, concerts, dinners and a ball for which the houses of the officers were filled with guests from other cities; the ball especially was a very brilliant occasion with all the men in uniform and the ladies in bright evening dresses and relatives and friends of the midshipmen and officers came from far and near for the occasion. 

In April, 1869 Commander Walker was detached from the Naval Academy and ordered to command the frigate Sabine in which the graduating class was to be taken to European and South American waters for a "practice cruise" of a year to give them practical experience in seamanship. They sailed from Boston on July 10. Some quotations from his letters to his wife may give an idea of life at sea in a "crack" boat sixty or seventy years ago. "U. S. S. Sabine. At sea, July 12, 1869 - 9.30 P.M.. 

9.30 P.M..........We came out of Boston harbor with a light fair wind and pleasant weather which continued until about four o'clock the next morning when the wind came out strong from the southward and we had a very rough uncomfortable day of it yesterday. Ryan did not close the heavy wooden ports in his room soon enough and a sea smashed every pane of glass in his window and about a hogshead of water came in completely drenching everything. I got some water into the cabin, the ports not closing perfectly tight, but not enough to do any particular harm. I shall have to take up my carpets to get them dry, as the carpet paper under them holds the water like a sponge. It was quite rough with the wind blowing quite hard all day, half a gale in fact, and such a sea-sick party I have not seen for years. Mr. Rich went to bed at once and has scarcely been out of it since. I felt a little out of sorts two or three times during the day, but by staying on deck in the fresh air it passed off and today I have felt perfectly well. "I think of my home and dear ones constantly, imagine what they are doing and thinking and I find myself already beginning to look forward to my return next summer. I hope to see and enjoy much of this cruise but I find my feelings very different from what they would have been a few years ago if starting upon such a trip. I am more than ever convinced that a man happily married should not follow the sea.

"We were, at sunset tonight, just south from Sable Island and are running along towards the "great bank of Newfoundland" as fast as the very moderate breeze will carry us. I am expecting to reach England by the 1st of August but of course it depends almost entirely upon the winds... "14th...The bell has just struck three (half past nine) and I am again seated at the table in the forward cabin to scribble to you for half an hour or so before going to bed for the night. We are making slow progress. Last night the wind died out about sunset and we had a dead calm until a little before sunset tonight when a light breeze sprang up right ahead. I suppose easterly winds will not hold long however in this quarter of the world and I hope very soon to be again running along with a fair wind......So far, with the exception of the rough Sunday, we have had very smooth water and pleasant weather, unusually so for a northern passage, and we are not in any particular hurry, it suits us very well. "We are getting the men's clothes in order, the band into uniform, drilling at the great guns, and in every way trying to get the ship to rights. No one but a Naval man can have any idea of the amount and variety of work always being done aboard a ship like this. 

We are at work all day long at all kinds of things and the work will last as long as the cruise. "I wish I could see you tonight, and the dear little children.

I miss them very much and I am very sorry to lose all their little doings and ways for a long year and to go back to them a stranger........ "22nd. The light easterly wind of which I wrote lasted about 

36 hours and since that time we have had a fair wind and plenty of it. 

For the last three or four days we have been running before a heavy gale and under water about half the time, the ship behaving beautifully. 

Of course a very heavy sea got up and, as the wind was fair and the ship scudding right before it, she went like a bird, but a very wet bird, every sea that struck her sending more or less water on board, drenching everybody and everything. "The cabin was all afloat for two days and as doleful a place as you can imagine. The gale has now settled down into a fine westerly wind which is sweeping us along towards "Merrie England" at a famous rate and we are dry and comfortable again. If we hold this wind, four more days will see us safely at anchor at Spithead (the man-of-war anchorage near Portsmouth) but I am prepared to lose the wind before that, although as westerly winds prevail we may be lucky enough to carry it right to the anchorage. We have had such rough weather during the last week that everybody on board is anxious to get into port when the ship will be quiet again. I am very much pleased with the Sabine, she sails and steers finely and scuds before a gale like a bird. I couldn't have had a finer ship......I suppose you will write once a week while I remain in Europe and perhaps more frequently......Steamers for Europe leave New York almost every day.....To England the postage is twelve cents the half ounce and to France 15 cents the quarter ounce. I miss my loved ones at home very much and I am constantly consulting my watch

which is still running on Boston time and fancying what each is doing …………………….I wish, dear Betty, that I could look forward to settling down on shore at the end of this cruise for the remainder of my life, leaving the service to engage in some business that would give me enough to do and to keep me from being discontented. It seems such a waste of life to leave all I hold most de ar for such a length of time and knock about the world for a bare living. It disgusts me every time I think of it. "23rd. We are still rushing along on our course with a fine fair wind.......Up to this time we have outsailed every vessel we have fallen in with and I suppose it is fair to pronounce the Sabine a fast ship........It has this moment struck ten o'clock while by my watch (Boston time) it is half past six. I suppose little Fan will soon be going off to bed and, I hope, for a good night's sleep. I wish I could see the little sprite and kiss her goodnight. "24th........Today we are two weeks out from Boston and we are still running along with a fair wind. If the wind holds as fair and strong as now, I shall expect to make the Scilly Islands by eleven o'clock tomorrow night......... The run up the Channel is very pretty with a fair wind and clear weather and I hope we may be favored and have a good look at everything as we pass. I am dragging this letter out to a terrible length but I have enjoyed sitting with my sheet before me and thinking of my dear ones at home...... I trust our next separation may be a long way off. "26th. Yesterday we had another heavy blow and I was obliged to reduce sail until we had less on than at any time since leaving Boston. 

It was very thick and rainy also, so we could get no observations and most of the time couldn't see more than half a mile and, as I knew we were close to the Scilly Islands, I was a little anxious to find out exactly where we were. About ten in the evening it cleared away with a nice fair wind, I got an observation of the north star for latitude and of the moon for longitude, soon sighted the Scilly lights and he aded the ship up the channel. This morning we are running along the south coast of England with a light fair wind and a prospect of anchoring at Spithead tomorrow morning. 

The day is lovely, the water smooth and everybody in the best of spirits. 

I have never gone to sea, after being on shore any time, and been as well as I have been this time. I have felt well and strong without a particle of seasickness or trouble of any kind. I suppose it is owing to my general health being so much better, for I am a very different man from what 

I was a few years ago........What would you have thought if I had been pointed out to you as your coming husband the first time you saw me on board the Susquehanna? I am afraid my lean figure and sallow face would not have prepossessed you in my favor. I can see you now as I first saw you, and I remember my first impression perfectly.......... "27th. Are now snugly at anchor at Spithead." "U. S. S. Frigate Sabine, Spithead, July 30, 1869. Last evening I dined with Vice Admiral Sir James Hope, the Port Admiral of this station. There were twenty-two at table and it went off very well, nearly all ladies and gentlemen having titles. I took in Mrs. Rice,

wife of Capt. Rice of the English navy and found myself between Mrs. R. and Lady Allison, wife of an army officer and son of the historian. As they were the handsomest ladies present and very talkative and pleasant I quite enjoyed myself. After the ladies left the table, I chatted with 

Sir George Gray, a bright old gentleman who is quite prominent in political life I believe. After the dinner was over I started for the ship against a strong head wind and tide and a considerable sea and was fully two hours getting off. A very nasty pull." "Spithead, August 5, 1869........Tomorrow I shall be very busy making my parting visits and clearing up bills etc. Amongst other things 

I must go over to the Isle of Wight to call upon Sir Thomas and Lady Cochran who invited me to dine and sleep at Quarr Abbey House, offering to send their carriage to Ryde to meet me etc., and I want to go to

Shankland to see Mr. Whitehouse's sisters who are passing the summer there with two brothers. The brothers and sisters have been knocking about Europe for about four years and seem to have no desire to go home." "Cherbourg, August 8, 1869......It has been rather nasty in the Channel, thick and rainy, with a strong wind and I was very glad to get in and anchor in this smooth excellent harbor, where we are as snug as that famous bug that inhabited the rug.........I shall take tomorrow to call upon the French authorities and draw French gold for the use of the ship, and Tuesday morning I shall go off to Paris to see Uncle. I shall probably return to the ship Saturday and confine myself to her for sometime. She needs a good deal of looking after and I find that none of the officers have had experience enough in large ships to get things into the condition that I wish... "Goodnight dear........I so wish I could be with you tonight, I miss and long for you, dear, all the time. It is like being separated from a part of myself...... "Division Navale Cuirassée 

Monsieur le Commandat

Fregate La Savoie Rade de Cherbourg le 13 Aout 1869 

J'ai L'honneur de vous informer que Dimanche prochain, 15 Aout, jour de la fête de Sa Majeste l'Empereur Napoleon III, les batiments de guerre français présent sur rade hisseront le grand pavois à 8h du matin en même temps que les couleurs, et le conserveront jusqu'au coucher du soleil. Les Fregates cuirassées feront 3 salves de 

21 coups de canon, la première à 8h du matin, la seconde vers midi, la troisième au coucher du soleil.

J'ai pensé qu'il vous serait agreable de savoir ce que les batiments francais font pour célébrer cette solemnite.

Receves, Monsieur le Commandant, l'assurance de ma haute considération.

Le C. Admiral Commandant en Chef 

(signed) Hornoy"

The above letter explains itself; the Sabine joined in firing salutes and flew the French colors as all the other vessels in the harbor did. "Cherbourg, August 16, 1869.......I went to Paris by the night train last Tuesday, arriving Wednesday morning and returned today (Monday).

I saw but little of Paris as I passed most of my time with Uncle who was delighted to see me and loath to have me leave him even to go to my hotel to go to bed. He looks pretty well but is in constant dread of another attack and suffers about the head a good deal at times.....Uncle forwarded his resignation as a senator while I was with him........ "Yesterday was the Fete Napoleon and also the one hundredth anniversary of the birth of Napoleon the 1st. The illumination and fireworks in Paris were superb, almost beyond description, and the crowd so great that it was difficult getting about the streets. All vehicles were stopped and the streets packed with people on foot. I intended to have been here to take part in the affair but remained in Paris to keep Uncle company. Before leaving here I entered our barge in a regatta for yesterday and she took the first prize beating the boats of the whole 

French ironclad fleet badly. Prize, a silver medal given by the Prince 

Imperial and 225 francs in money.....I am thinking of you constantly my dearest and longing for the end of the cruise that I may again be with you. I would not have believed that I could feel the separation so much. 

You are a part of my very life." "Cherbourg, August 17, 1869.....My dear wife. I finished and sent you a letter a few hours ago but I am alone in my cabin with no one to talk to and my thoughts at once turn towards my dear girl at home and I feel a desire to write as the best thing I can do. It seems to bring you nearer, darling, and I know you will be glad of the letter, poor as it will be, for it comes from the great love I bear you....... "One thing that I have enjoyed very much in passing through both England and France is the beautiful country, like an immense garden, so thoroughly cultivated and so charming, I was constantly wishing you were with me, you would have enjoyed it so much. "18th......I am to have a visit within the next half hour from the general commanding the troops here and after he has gone I think I shall go on shore for a look about the town. It is a queer old place but rather a pleasant, though dull, one I judge...... The visit is over, the salute of 13 guns fired and returned from the shore and I am once more scribbling away to you.......By the way, while I think of it, I will tell you about the band. We have picked up four or five more musicians and the band begins to be quite respectable. While at Spit-

head several men deserted from the English military bands there with a view of enlisting in this ship but of course I couldn't allow anything of that kind to go on and I sent them on shore to shift for themselves. I should have liked the men but I couldn't wink at or encourage desertion. The men were crazy to go to America and were willing to risk anything to accomplish that. "Left Cherbourg the afternoon of August 20, 1869." (It was then that the mutiny canard was started.) "August 22nd......It is Sunday evening, dear Betty, and we are drifting about the channel without wind enough to move a feather. While 

I was writing to you yesterday it fell calm and has continued so ever since... "25th.....At noon today we were about 180 miles to the northward of Cape Finisterre, the northwest point of Spain, and I expected to pass it tomorrow but it has fallen calm and we are doing nothing now.... 

The knocking about at sea is good for the midshipmen and crew, and the officers are rather glad to stay out in order to have money due them when they arrive. London and Paris having pretty nearly cleaned out their pockets....... "Saturday 28th......We have had very bad luck with winds and have got on very slowly. For the last three days we have had calms and very light head winds but just at dark tonight the wind allowed us to lay our course and it has been gradually freshening until now, half past nine, we are going six knots and are feeling quite encouraged in consequence. I fear it will not hold however. Yesterday afternoon I spoke a brig, a few miles to the northward of where we are now, in distress for provisions, which I gave her. She had been out 29 days from St. Ubes (only a few miles from Lisbon) and bound to Bergen, Norway. So you see we are not the only ones troubled by bad winds. She had been 29 days going a distance that she should have run in three with a fair fresh breeze. "Sept. 4, 1869....I reached Lisbon on the evening of the 2nd... and found myself famous!! An article is going the rounds of the European papers to the effect that we discovered a conspiracy to blow up the Sabine (a lighted match being applied to the magazine) and that twenty-two men were arrested, at once court martialed and seven sentenced to be hung (all this in Cherbourg) that I applied to the French authorities for permission to execute the sentence in the harbor, and upon a refusal being given, immediately weighed anchor, stood out of the harbor, and heaving ho, hung the wretches in plain sight of the French fleet, the officers and men of which, with their glasses could see the whole thing distinctly. That several fishing boats were quite close and saw the awful spectacle etc. etc. Everybody in Lisbon was agog to see the captain of the Sabine who hung seven men...... Of course the whole thing was untrue, we not having had the slightest trouble aboard." 

The canard had caused much worry and distress among the relatives of the officers and men of the Sabine and Mrs. Walker as wife of the commanding officer received many inquiries as to the truth or falsehood of the report but of course she was as much in the dark as any one.

It seemed a needlessly cruel piece of newspaper work; the originator of the story was never discovered but it was apparently an attempt to discredit the line officers of the Navy. "Lisbon, Sept. 13, 1869... ...Things go along pretty well on board the Sabine and the cruise is pleasant for everyone so far, I believe. Thursday I dined on shore at a hotel (table d'hote) and at the same table with me was a man whom I knew more than seventeen years ago. 

In June or July, 1852 I was a midshipman on board the Portsmouth at 

Guayaquil, South America (Ecuador) and a revolution was in full blast, the government holding the city and General Flores at the head of the revolutionists trying to get possession with a small army of fellows picked up along the coast. The inside party found a young man holding a commission as Colonel under Flores, in the town, trying to buy over some of the officers and men, and tried, convicted and sentenced him to be shot as a spy. We promised if they would give him up to us, to retain him on board ship and take him out of the country. In that way his life was saved and Thursday I went in to dinner and found him at the table. When on board the Portsmouth he was quite young and very handsome, two things that probably had much to do with saving his life, and he is now a middle aged man although still fine looking, but I knew him at once and we embraced according to the custom of the country. 

Knowing that I had returned from Cintra he came on board to see me today but I was on shore and missed him. His card has for a crest the 

Mexican eagle upon a branch of cactus strangling a serpent and under it - Montezuma XV He claims to be the legitimate emperor of Mexico, one of the Monte zumas... "14th.... I have been busy today flying about the English ironclad squadron (which arrived yesterday) visiting the dignataries and examining the ships and I must soon dress for dinner, being invited to dine with the first Lord of the Admirality who is here in the Agincourt, a large ironclad. He has been cruising with the squadron for a couple of months past, seeing for himself. This ironclad squadron consists of 12 heavy ships and two for dispatch vessels, and is the strongest squadron ever afloat. I am very glad to have seen it. of the ships are quite wonderful as regards size, strength and ingenuity of detail. They are the latest and best productions of 

English skill in the way of fighting machines." 

English Ironclad Fleet at Lisbon, Some

Cruiser 

Swine burn "15th.......The dinner passed off very well last evening, 20 at table. I was glad to go in order to have the opportunity of seeing and conversing with some of the best officers of the English service. They were very civil and I was quite pleased with some of them.......The King visited the English ironclad fleet today and I had to man yards and fire two salutes (21 guns each) in his honor, which was a bore." "Sept. 18, 1869.....I must tell you something of my trip to

Cintra and Mafra.......at ten o'clock (P.M.) we got into our carriage and started for Cintra where we arrived at half past twelve, very tired and sleepy. The next morning after breakfast we got donkies and started for the Pena Castle or convent for it is sometimes called castle and sometimes convent. It is built upon the highest point of one of the highest peaks of the Sierra de Centra about 3000 feet above the sea which almost washes the base of the mountain. The castle is very beautiful and the view not to be surpassed, being miles upon miles of country dotted with farms and villages upon two sides, and upon the other two the boundless ocean. It was originally built by Don Manoil for the Jeronymites of Belem; when convents were suppressed in Portugal it was bought by a private gentleman who afterwards sold it to Don Fernando, the present owner who is a cousin of the late Prince Albert and the father of the present king. He has spent immense sums in restoring the castle and in making and keeping up the beautiful grounds by which it is surrounded, the whole top of the mountain immediately below the castle being a magnificent garden where originally there was nothing but masses of rock... 

From the Pena Castle we went across to one of the neighboring peaks to an old Moorish castle of which nothing remains but the walls, towers and an immense bath, and back to the hotel to dine. "The next morning we drove about 18 miles to Mafra.....The sight at Mafra is the enormous palace, convent and church in one and it is certainly something wonderful. The history, according to the guide books, is this: Don John V, anxious for an heir to succeed him, made a vow that on the birth of a son he would change the poorest into the most magnificent monastery in his kingdom, and upon the birth of an heir he caused enquiries to be made and Mafra was selected. It was built in imitation of but is larger than the Escurial, being a church, a convent, a palace and barracks for a battalion of soldiers. Thirteen years were spent in its erection and at times, when the work was hurried on, 45000 men were employed but the usual number was 14700......The chimes have 

114 bells.....The church is the most wonderful I have ever seen, the inside being entirely of colored marbles, exquisitely carved, the whole inside is one mass of carving. The monastery is broken up and the palace is going to ruin. A few rooms are fitted up for the king who sometimes goes there to hunt.

The King's palace at Centra was the Alhambra of the Moorish kings and is a curious jumble of buildings Moorish and Christian.. 

But the greatest attraction of Centra is its natural beauty which can scarcely be surpassed. one as was ever seen. "19th 8 P.M. ....It is Sunday evening and as beautiful an

The moon is full and scarcely a cloud in the sky, while a soft west wind makes the temperature all that can be desired. 

Cadiz is in plain sight and about an hour ago I hove the ship to, to await a pilot or daylight. I am afraid to attempt to take the ship in myself at night and I presume a pilot would not take her in, either, until after daylight. If we have a breeze in the morning we will soon run in but I think it very likely that morning will find us becalmed or with a light land breeze, and waiting for the sea breeze we will not reach our berth until sometime in the afternoon. I hope to receive a letter from you then giving me the home news. "Monday morning 20th. I picked up a pilot and came in last night after all, anchoring near the Richmond which had preceeded us a few days. Capt. Mullaney sent our mail aboard at once, containing any number of papers from home with accounts of the conspiracy or hanging on board the ship and letters for nearly everybody but not a line for me.....My letters have come all out of time ever since leaving Cherbourg.

I see the papers are filled with abuse of naval officers all springing from the story of the mutiny aboard this ship. I am sorry now I did not telegraph the legation in Paris as soon as I reached Lisbon and heard of the report, stating it to be entirely without foundation, but the story was so absurd that I did not think it worthy of a denial. 

People will believe anything if they only see it in a newspaper! Since writing the above I have received your Nos. 10 and 11.....I was very glad to get them as I always am, but very sorry to find that you had been so distressed by the reported mutiny etc. We sailed from Cherbourg, as you know ere this, on the evening of the 20th August and the dispatch from Paris reporting the mutiny was dated the 29th when we were more than half way to Lisbon. 

The men are quiet and well behaved, we have had no trouble of any kind, all like the ship and are enjoying the cruise, and the report was so preposterous that officers and men joined in a general laugh when we first heard of it. Such an affair would be impossible in such a ship as this. I have no idea of the cause or manner of starting the story, but it seems to have taken everybody in, at home. Mr. 

Rich sent a denial of it to the Army and Navy Journal but I think it is hardly worth while now to do more. In our Navy the men are better treated than in any military service in the world and there is never the slightest probability or possibility of mutinies.....I am well and contented and happy as I can be away from you, my dear wife. The cruise is passing away and I am looking forward to the end as a thing devoutly to be desired....... As I write the band, now consisting of 17 pieces, is practicing on the gun deck. It is now quite a good band. I sent home from Lisbon for discharge, three musicians who were good for nothing and enlisted four others. I hope before the 1st of August next you will hear and judge for yourself.

"Gibraltar, Sept. 30, 1869. We left Cadiz on the 27th, had very light and unfavorable winds and at noon the following day were farther from Gibraltar than when we started but about 9 P.M. took a fine fair breeze and came in early yesterday morning. A sailing ship tries one's patience a good deal some times, but perhaps that does no harm....I was pretty well used up yesterday having been up all night before getting in here. It was a wet nasty night, very dark, and I was obliged to heave to in the narrowest part of the straits to wait for daylight. As the safety of the ship and crew is confided to me, I feel bound to look after matters myself and sometimes, as in this case, it is hard work. It is not comfortable to get wet through and through, to go without sleep, and to have a large ship and five hundred men upon one's shoulders. "Uneasy lies the head that wears a crown." "Oct. 1st......I was obliged to pay my official visits yesterday and so was unable to write as much as I wished. I called upon 

Vice Admiral Sir Alex. Milne who is here with his squadron, Rear Admiral

Prevost, Supt.of the dock yard, and Major General Bisset, Acting Governor. The General invited the officers to witness a review of the troops comprising the garrison and I went, and in the evening I dined with Admiral Milne so that my day was a busy one. The review was quite interesting although not large, there being about three thousand men in line, but the troops looked finely......Tomorrow I am going to lunch at the Artillery Mess on shore and then through the fortifications with Col. Maberly, commanding the artillery here. I quite like the old fellow's appearance, very intelligent looking and he is extremely polite and kind. I met quite a number of army officers at dinner last night and suppose I shall see a good deal of them before leaving here. You know the fortifications here are wonderful, and I could not possibly see them to better advantage than with Col. Maberly who knows every inch of them and can give me any information I want about them. Admiral 

Milne sails with his fleet for Malta this evening and unless I go to Malta during the winter I shall probably never see the old gentleman again. He is a pleasant man and I rather like him. The "Sunday Oct. 3, 1869.....Tomorrow I am to dine with Mr. Sprague, our consul, and on Tuesday with General Besset, the acting governor. 

English are great for dinners and, as my digestion is good, I rather like it. They seem to think they are doing the civil thing in the proper way and I am not sure but they are right. I have found them very civil and attentive wherever I have been so far on this cruise....My men are beginning to look respectable. I have hard work to get them into their proper uniform as many of the green men cannot sew and have to wait until other men can get time to make their clothes for them, but at muster this morning they looked pretty well.....It is a great deal of work to get a large ship's company into good order, many of the men requiring looking after like children.....Wednesday 6th.....The dinners went off pleasantly and today I have been calling on Col. Maberly and family......Nineteen years ago yesterday I entered the Navy. Nineteen years seems a long time, but it seems but a very short time since I reported for examination at Annapolis. Time flies!....... "Gibraltar, Oct. 8, 1869.....I have been having a row with the governor about two or three deserters (English subjects) from this ship,

and have succeeded in making the old fellow decidedly uncomfortable but of course I shall never get my men. He does not dare to give them up to me.

He fears it would injure him at home, as it undoubtedly would. "Gilbralta, Oct. 12..... ..Admiral Prevost commanding the dock yard has been away all the time that I have been here, only returning day before yesterday. He is a good old fellow and very anxious to smooth away all hard feelings about our treatment here.........He came aboard to see me yesterday and again today and I am to dine with him tomorrow evening, taking Mr. Train and Mr. Birney with me." "At sea, Oct. 17, 1869.....when I sent my last letter I expected to leave Gibralta that day (15th) but it was calm all day and I did not get under way until yesterday morning and then with so little wind that I was four hours making two, but I at last took a breeze and a fair one. At noon yesterday we were due south of Europe Point, the southern end of the Rock of Gibraltar, and one half mile distant, and at noon today we were off Cape de Gatte having made one hundred and sixty miles. The breeze still holds fair and I have headed up along the Spanish coast intending to pass between the coast and the island of Iciza, and along to the northward of Majorca and Minorca. I do this that, if I get northwest winds in the Gulf of Lyons, as I may, I can make a fair wind of them and run my course for Nice, or rather for Villa Franca, a mile and a half from Nice, where we anchor. We are getting on famously now,.........What a horrid blot! I brought out of the inkstand, on the point of my pen, a dead fly and off he dropped onto my letter just as I noticed that I had him transfixed....19th....

We lost our fine wind about one o'clock on the morning of the 18th and had very light airs until three o'clock in the afternoon when we got a severe squall lasting two or three hours which made us shorten sail in a hurry. Since that time we have had pretty good winds and are getting on very well. When the squall struck us we were in company with fifty sail of vessels and it was laughable to see the scattering, every fellow taking in sail as fast as possible and running in the direction he thought best to avoid the full strength of the wind. It blew pretty sharply for a time but I held onto my course and, with all hands on deck, reduced sail as necessary, gaining several miles on the vessels in company with us....26th...I had written this much when, for some reason or other, I laid down my pen (and I can't tell why) and went on deck to find a heavy squall coming down upon us and the officer of the deck, fully believing that it was made of rain and not wind, making no preparation for it. I ordered him to reduce sail as fast as possible and before he was half through we had it upon us, the heaviest squall I have seen for years. We came through it all right but if I had not chanced to go on deck as I did, we would probably have lost our light spars at least. 

The squall settled down into a heavy northwest gale which lasted us four days, driving us over on the coast of Sardinia and due east of Minorca defore it broke.....Just here a row on deck and a midshipman comes down to report the lee leach rope of the main topsail parted. The sail must come in and we shall lose its services for a couple of hours while it is being repaired. I split the fore and mails ails all to pieces during the gale and if this leach rope had gone while it was blowing hard the topsail would have gone into a thous and pieces. The suit of sails we have been using are old and the gale tried them severely. For a couple of days it

blew very hard with a very heavy sea but the ship behaved beautifully and we were as comfortable as men can be under such circumstances. 

It has been rather a hard passage for me but I trust not to have another until I start for Gibraltar bound out of the Mediterranean.... 

27th...Again a heavy blow. It began to breeze up in the evening, steadily increasing in force until, at about half past two, it was blowing great guns. We split the main topsail and had a great row all night but lived through it as you see.....I have lost a good deal of sleep this passage, slept only very little last night and shall not sleep much tonight but I shall soon make it up after we are safely moored in the harbor of Villa Franca. "Villa franche, Nov. 2, 1869.....Just now we are scraping, scrubbing, painting and putting everything to rights for port. It is a grand overhaul, the most extensive one that will be made during the cruise and was much needed. When we are through I think the ship will look well. After this is over I shall settle down into a regular routine of drill, drilling a certain number of hours every day and making the ship's company as perfect at that kind of thing as I can. "Sunday Nov. 14.....We fared comparatively well in the gale on our passage from Gibraltar. The Juniata lost her fore topmast, leaked badly from strain and was generally used up. A North German frigate that left Gibraltar just after us lost all three topmasts, we hear, and vessels exposed to the gale generally suffered very much. 

The Sabine behaved very well and I flatter myself that she was well handled. "Villefranche, Sunday Nov. 28.....Remey, Whitehouse and myself landed on the eastern side of the harbor this afternoon and walked about two miles to an old Martello tower and back. " "Genoa, January 14, 1870.....We have an invitation to another dancing party tonight and I must begin to dress. We gave quite a pretty affair on board yesterday afternoon dancing until night. 

We had quite as many guests as we could attend to and they seemed to enjoy it very much. Saturday 15th I left the party about twelve o'clock but most of the officers remained until 5 this morning and are to bring a lot of girls off here this afternoon to finish it up. "Genoa Sunday Jan. 16, 1870.......This morning after breakfast, inspection, etc., I went ashore taking Mr. Rich with me and climbed to the top of the great light house here, three hundred and seventy feet high. The view from the top was very fine and I was much interested in the tower, which is a magnificent one, and in the machinery for revolving the light. At three o'clock the English clergyman came on board by my invitation to perform service, bringing his wife and daughter and our consul and wife, so that my day has been rather a busy one. He gave us a very good sermon from the text "Quit thee like men" and I am glad I had him off.....Tuesday 18th.....I had to make a number of calls and attend to several things on shore and then come on board and dress for a dinner party at the English consul's.... 

The dinner was a very pleasant one and quite a party of people were invited for the evening to meet us so it was eleven o'clock before I

left the house. At 11 this morning I tripped my anchor and am now (3 P.M.) about half way to Specia and with a fair wind to take us to the mouth of the bay. I expect to work up to the anchorage by ten or eleven tonight.....Wednesday 19th.....We lost a good deal of our wind when we got in under the high land near the entrance of the bay of Spezia and when we fairly opened the bay the wind came out dead ahead so that I did not get up to the anchorage until one o'clock in the night. It was very squally and baffling but bright moonlight and I managed it very well, passing through the Italian fleet and anchoring in shore of them all. "At sea, Sunday Feb. 6, 1870.......I waited in Spezia harbor for a fair wind and pleasant weather until I was tired, and finally, reflecting that as a practice ship a little extra experience at sea would do no harm, on Wednesday last I got under way and started for sea. 

Having worked down the harbor about two miles it fell flat calm and I was obliged to anchor, remaining there all night. The next day (Thursday) I got under way again and, after beating about all day with light airs and calms, got out of the harbor just before dark and into a southeast storm. Wind dead ahead, plenty of rain and frequent squalls. As 

I determined to pass to the eastward of Elba it was necessary to keep a sharp lookout for the rocks and shoals in the way, and the weather being thick and bad I did not feel like trusting to anyone the ship and the lives of the five hundred people on board. Consequently I have scarcely slept at all and today feel pretty much used up. I feel it more while writing than at any other time. I feel tremulous and weak. I passed through Piombino channel this morning (between Elba and the main) and am now running off to the southward with a light wind and delightful weather. "Wednesday 9th.......We are this morning in plain sight of

Ischia and Capri, with Vesuvius in the distance, but with a very light head wind so that we are doing next to nothing.....Thursday evening 10th...

A little before four last evening when close in to Ischia the weather began to look badly, the wind to get up, and before five it was blowing a furious gale right in our teeth. All that could be done was to take care of the ship, so I stripped off the canvas and let it blow, keeping the ship head to the southward and letting her drift to the southward and westward. Between half past eight and nine we were struck aback by a heavy squall but as I had expected that, I had everything ready and no harm was done. In five minutes after the squall it was almost calm and we anchored in Naples at 2 PM.......Last night was as nasty as one cares

While the wind lasted it blew furiously and all night the rain came down in torrents.....We have been a week coming from Spezia during which time I have had little sleep and that little very broken, while my normal condition has been, wet. "Naples, Feb. 23, 1870.......The King arrived here from Florence on Sunday and tonight there is a grand court ball to which I am going. I suppose it will be very crowded and I shall probably not stay long. This morning I received a present from the King of a wild boar killed during his yesterday's hunt. The flesh is considered very fine and the present is quite a compliment.

"Thursday 24th. I went to the ball last night and remained until half past twelve when I came off on board. The palace is very fine and the ball went off well. Very crowded, many fine looking men in fine uniforms and covered with decorations, but not many pretty women. The dresses and diamonds were magnificent. "Naples Feb. 26.....I have passed most of the day on shore, coming off at half past five to dine on wild boar, which was delicious. 

I think I have never tasted finer meat. It is not a particle like pork, is dark colored, without fat, and has the flavor of game. I gave half of him to the wardroom. "At sea. 

Saturday, March 19......We are seven days out (from Naples) this afternoon and to me the week passed very quickly. 

On a long passage at sea the days slip away one after the other and a month rolls around seemingly in a very short time......Sunday 20th.

Another Sunday has come and our meeting is one week nearer. I wonder if you remember today, that this is my birthday. Thirty-five years old today! I am becoming a middle aged man, am I not? and I feel no older than I did years ago.....I can't realize that I am more than twenty-five...

Monday 21st. 9 P.M. The breeze has freshened and the old ship is rushing along from nine to ten knots with studding sails both sides, dead before the wind. Judging from present appearances there is little doubt but we will get in (Gibraltar) tomorrow in time to get our mail on board before the gates close and of course we are all feeling pretty jolly. 

Everybody is anxious for letters and we expect to find a large mail for us, the amount collected in twelve or fourteen days.....Tuesday 22nd. 

11 P.M. We are safely anchored in the harbor of Gilbraltar....The consul, Mr. Sprague, was thoughtful enough to send our letters out in the harbor to us so that we have them as soon as we anchored, which was about six o'clock......Wednesday 23rd...I have made my official visits and if the wind holds to the eastward expect to sail tomorrow for Madeira. "Gibraltar, March 25, 1870....The wind is blowing strong from the westward through the straits and of course I cannot go to sea until it stops......Yesterday I went to a steeplechase gotten up by the officers of the garrison here and enjoyed it very much. There were a good many falls but no one badly hurt. In the evening I dined at the Artillery Mess... "Monday 26th.....Wind still strong and steady from the westward with no sign of change. It is impossible to be at the ship out against the wind because of the strong current always running into the Mediterrane an and there is no tow boat here powerful enough to take us out against so strong a breeze. "Funchal, April 5, 1870......The people are so civil here that 

I really have no time to write to my dear old wife. Between lunches, dinners, dancing parties, etc., and my duties aboard, my time is pretty well taken up and one can't well write when full of dinner and old madeira. "Funchal, Madeira, April 7, 1870.....I went to a very pretty dancing party last evening, given expressly for our sakes by the Baron de

Conceicao, a plesant old gentleman who has been very kind and civil to us. There was another given by an Englishman to which a few of the officers went, also a very pretty party. It was rather unfortunate having both on the same night.....I mean to get under way in the morning as soon as the stewards are back from market. I am not sure whether 

I shall anchor at Porto Grande or go direct to Rio. If I stop it will only be for a few hours to send letters and to try to get some papers to learn what is going on in the world. Porto Grande is a great coaling station and a very fine harbor but one of the most barren, desolate places I have ever visited. "At sea, April 14, 1870. At noon today we were two hundred and eighty-five miles from Porto Grande, Island of St. Vincent, for which I am steering with the intention of sending mail ashore.....if we arrive off the port in the daytime.....I shall pass right by the mouth of the harbor anyway and sending a boat in with mail will detain me but an hour or two if we have daylight to see what we are about. Since leaving Madeira we have had most magnificent weather with fair winds although very light so that we have made but slow progress.....Everything has fallen into the sea routine on board and we are jogging along in a half torpid way, one day being very like another. Time usually passes quickly at sea with good weather and I trust it may do so with me this spring and summer. I find it difficult to take hold of anything in the way of reading on board. I am always thinking and planning about my arrival home and can't fix my attention upon my work..... 

I shaved a week ago today for the last time until I reach home and this morning I had my hair cut "Fighting Fashion", close to my head, and my appearance is now somewhat rough. It is very comfortable having the hair so short in warm weather and there is time for it to grow before reaching home, while shaving is a great nuisance. You see that being at sea and away from the fair sex, I am making myself comfortable as circumstances will admit......Sunday, April 24th, 1870. We are sixteen days out from Madeira and until night before last have had perfect weather but we are now in the "Doldrums", the belt of qualls, calms and rains between the northeast and southeast trades, and shall get no more good weather until we get through into the southeast trades. We are about one degree north of the equator and, according to old Coyle, the signal master, it is hot enough in the rooms below to bake bread. 

As long as the northeast trade lasted it was quite comfortable but it is sweltering now. We are doing very well and I hope to get across in a day or two, but sailing vessels are sometimes becalmed for weeks. 

As soon as we get the steady trade we shall have fine weather again and make rapid progress towards Rio. I am wondering what you are doing today and hoping that everything is going well with the children and yourself.....I am not going to the Cape. I suppose you will be glad to know that I am not going farther away than to Rio as it makes it pretty certain that I shall be with you very soon after the 1st of August if not on that day. I am very well indeed. I have lost flesh since leaving home but I think I am losing little if any now and I shall soon recover what I have lost when I have my darling to pet and look after me. "Tuesday 26th...We took a breeze yesterday afternoon, crossed the equator a little after seven in the evening....We got across the doldrums very nicely and I suppose in two weeks time I shall reach Rio.

"Friday 29th..... Three weeks today since I sailed from Madeira and just one year since we left Annapolis. One year ago tomorrow I reported in New York for the command of this ship. As I look back it seems a short year but it was long enough when viewed from the other end....We have been rather unfortunate with the southeast trade as it is blowing so much from the southward that it is not a fair wind for us and consequently here where I expected to be getting on rapidly I am making but indifferent progress.....We are having fine clear trade weather but very warm. To be comfortable one must keep in a draught and in the shade..... "Sunday May 8th.....I wish most earnestly that things were so that I felt at liberty to say that I would never make another cruise. 

It is such a waste of life to be separated from everything most dear to me in the world........Wednesday morning, May 11th.....We arrived and anchored here (Rio) about 10 o'clock last evening, 32 days from Funchal... "Rio de Janeiro, May 14, 1870......I suppose from my 50 you have learned......that Porto Grande is a harbor in the island of St. 

Vincent, one of the Cape de Verde Islands. It is a very fine harbor, the best in the islands and is a great coaling station for steamers.... 

Water is becoming very scarce in the city for lack of rain to fill the springs in the hills which supply the waterworks, and I may be detained a day or two before I am able to fill up.....I begin to feel as if the end of the cruise was really drawing near and I am rejoicing accordingly .......Tuesday 17th.....Yesterday I bought a bag of fine old coffee to take home, paying eleven cents a pound. It is the old plantation coffee, is very hard to get because very scarce, and is said to be equal to any coffee in the world. Mr. Wright, a shipper of coffee, has been in chase of it for me ever since I arrived here. "At sea, May 19, 1870....I waited yesterday to get what fresh water I could....and am now working along to the eastward towards Cape 

Frio, with the wind so light that we are only going about one mile an hour. If I can get in without too much trouble, I am intending to go into Cape Frio harbor for three or four days. It is a good little harbor with very few people about and a capital place to exercise my men. With a sailing ship it is not always easy to go just where I want to and if the wind is very unfavorable I shall not insist upon going in.....I am now bound nowhere, my object being to use up the time until the 14th or 

15th of June when I shall go into Bahia for my letters. "Friday, 20th. I have given up the idea of going into Cape 

Frio harbor on account of one of my men who is so sick that exercising and firing guns would probably kill him. He is very sick although I still hope we may be able to save him. I shall run along towards Bahia and get into the trade wind as soon as I can as the best chance for him .....Yesterday I trimmed off my whiskers and now have a closely cropped full beard. My whiskers had grown very long, longer, I think, than you have ever seen them and trimming them off makes a great change in my appearance. I think whiskers are more becoming to me than any other style of beard but as I can't shave myself and shall not have a barber at my beck and call after I get home I thought I had better start my full beard and get over the worst looking part of it (the two first months) while at sea...Evening....My poor man is dead, and the ship is running

before a strong wouthwest blow, going about 9 knots an hour. I suppose we have the tail of a pampero and that farther southward it is a hard gale. It is helping us along towards the trade finely. I am very sorry for the death of my man. I was very much afraid of it this morning but hoped he might take a favorable turn and had no idea he would die so soon. I was sitting at the table reading about half past two when Remy reported him dead. I have been hoping that we could finish the cruise without a death on board but with so large a crew that was hardly to be expected. "I always feel sad over the death of anyone on board ship, away from home and friends, with only rough men about them and I have no doubt that many die who would recover if they had the comforts of home and the care of loving friends. We do all we can, but are poor substitutes for mothers, sisters, or wives. "Sat. 21st......I have just come down from reading the burial service over the remains of poor Flood. I have seen many men buried at sea and am perhaps hardened to it, but it must be very impressive when seen for the first time. The body is prepared for burial and sewed up in a hammock with 2 thirty-two pound shot sewed in at the foot, the ship is hove to, colors are hoisted half mast, the body placed in the lee gangway on a plank projecting over the side, covered with the Union Jack and at the call of "All hands to bury the dead" every body on board comes on deck, remaining uncovered while the service is being read. At the proper time in the service the inboard end of the plank is raised and the body slides overboard, the heavy shot taking it down out of sight instantly. When the service is over the officers and men are "piped down", the ship fills away on her course and everything goes on as usual......We shall receive a large amount of mail matter in Bahia, being a month's mail via Europe and the mail direct from New York by the South America. You will not have a long interval as I shall follow 

No. 54 pretty closely. I suppose it will reach you about the 25th of

July and I mean to be with you a week or so later. Don't forget that 

I have written about opening the door without being sure who is outside, and to be careful how you rush down stairs. I write because I think it very likely I may not be able to get home before night, as I shall have to hunt up the port admiral to report my arrival, and fuss around more or less before I can start for Cambridge.....Wednesday 25th....I expect to reach home in first rate condition, a little brown and decidedly thin, but well and strong....Friday 27th.....Counting from the time

I left Boston to day after tomorrow, when I expect to anchor, the ship will have been actually at sea one hundred and seventeen (117) days, to which by the end of the cruise we shall add about fifty more. Many of the officers are anxious to stop at Barbadoes on our way home, I should like to do so myself...... Perhaps if have good luck from Bahia to the northeast trade I may make up my mind to call. I know you will be glad to have a dozen or two of bayrum and that is the only place I can call to get it. "Satruday evening 28th.....It is a dark nasty night, with rain squalls in every direction, a very light air, just giving the ship steerage way, and nothing for us to do but keep quiet and take it easily... ....The weather is very warm.....Drawing in with the land give us rain. 

The pleasantest time of the twenty-four hours is during my bath in the

morning.

Then I am cool and comfortable. "Sunday 29th....I dreamed of you last night and of getting home.

I thought when I got home you were away, the house was the old house in Hillsborough in which I lived when a boy. I found the children in the kitchen with two little girl visitors, all four sitting and standing at the kitchen table eating, with a pretty young servant girl looking after them. Fannie knew me and kissed me but Jamie did not and was only anxious to know if there was any omlette left. "Bahia, June 13, 1870.....I wish I could take you some of the oranges we get here. They are very large, as large as small melons and of very fine flavor. I have never seen as good in any other port and the people here claim that they are the finest in the world. This particular kind is at its best here and does not grow about Rio at all. "Tuesday 14th.....We are filled up with water having stopped pumping a little while ago......Since I was here before a new Commodore has taken command of this division of the Erazilian Navy and this morning I have been to call upon him,finding him cordial and pleasant. He served as a midshipman in our Navy several years, being one of a number sent by the Brazilian government to learn the profession.....in the latest papers received, find very long passages reported from this part of the world to New York, fifty and sixty days. "I am rather amused by my old Madeira friend's letter to your father and at the same time annoyed because I suppose he may have written. to other people, whom I don't know, in the same way. He is a native of 

Madeira named Fequeira who went to Philadelphia when a young man, accumulated a large fortune and married there. A few years ago when people were dying of starvation in Madeira, the crops having failed, he gave largely for their relief from his own means and was very active in raising money and sending supplit, so much so that the King of Portugal, by way of acknowleding his services, created him a baron. He is now getting on in years and not very strong and passes most of his winters in Madeira going back to the United States in the spring. Within the last year or two he has gone a little into the wine business, as he says to give himself something to do and think of during his winters in Madeira. He is a very cordial, hospitable old gentleman, with a pleasant wife and two nice daughters, one married to the Visconde d'Andaluz, the governor of the island, the other single.....I hope your mother will be benefitted by the lifting cure as it is called. I always think of sixty as pretty old and of your father and mother as rather young.
