Your mother seems to me about forty-five.....One week from today at the latest I shall sail for home....You must not expect me before the first of August and not too surely then as I may get light airs and calms and make a long passage .....I don't think it likely that I shall wait for night to go home if ready to go before, but if I anchor during the afternoon I shall probably be detained by reporting to the port admiral and attending to details about the ship some two or three hours and consequently it will be late in the day, if not evening, before I can reach Shepard Street. afraid your all night's talk will be a failure. I am pretty certain to be up nearly all night the night before getting in and to reach home very tired and needing sleep. I suspect the first thing you know you will find

your listener fast asleep. I hope I may get home early enough to see a little of the children before they go to bed and then I shall not want to see anyone else, my darling old wife excepted, until the next day........I wish it might be possible to get something to do that would allow me to resign and live at home but I fear I shall not find that an easy matter."

After a very successful cruise the Sabine was put out of commission and Commander Walker detached on July 28, 1870, and on the first of October, at the end of two months leave, he reported as Inspector of the second Lighthouse District. This district took in the lights, buoys, etc. on the coast of Massachusetts and Rhode Island, including Narraganset 

Bay, headquarters being in Boston, and the work was both important and interesting; but from the time of his cruise on the Sabine in 1869-70 he had been seriously considering resigning from the Navy and going into business if he could find a suitable opening. He dreaded the long separations from his family, necessitated by cruises of two or three years which must come in rotation with land duty, and in April, 1872, he asked to be detached from this duty and given twelve months' leave. In 

February of that year his uncle and adopted father, Senator James Wilson Grimes, died at his home in Burlington and Mrs. Grimes wanted Walker to help her about her affairs and she also thought he would have a better chance of finding a good business opening in that comparatively young town, and hoped to have him settle near her. 

Promotion was very slow after the war and the navy life in times of peace did not look attractive to an able and energetic man with wife and children to consider. He did not succeed, however, in finding the opportunity he was looking for and after some months in the employ of the B. & M. Railroad he returned to duty in the navy and was ordered to the Boston Navy Yard in January, 1873.

Perhaps an unacknowledged interest in and liking for the service in which he had been so successful may have had some thing to do with his lack of success in finding a suitable niche in civil life. This possibility had been in Senator Grimes' mind when in 1870 he wrote to Mrs. 

Walker:- "There is no doubt in my mind about John's success in business were he to leave the Navy and embark on some other employment. He has the requisite energy when he has an object before him to be attained, he is economical in his habits and he has, I think, a remarkable share of good common-sense, which is, after all, more essential to success in any pursuit than all else combined. But this is to be considered. His ambition has all been in the way of the Navy. How will he feel in a few years when he sees the young officers now below him, pass into the higher grades where he would precede them were he to remain in the service? 

But this after all is a matter of sentiment and feeling." 

Commander Walker's duty at the Boston Navy Yard lasted only about three months, for on April 16, 1873 he was detached and ordered to Washington as Naval Secretary of the Lighthouse Board. This Board was under the control of the Secretary of the Treasury; Professor Henry, a distinguished physicist, who had been a member of the Board since its organization in 1852, was at this time the chairman, but he was seventy-

six years old and the exacting duties of his office of Director of the Smithsonian Institute, as well as the scientific investigations he carried on, occupied most of his time, so that while he was always ready with advice and presided at meetings of the Board, the Naval Secretary was the real executive head.

The first year of Commander Walker's incumbency was a trying one as friction developed in the Board and just a year after his appointment he wrote to a friend:- "Efforts have been made with the President and through members of Congress to remove me from the Board. We are not running smoothly and are liable to an explosion at any time." At last affairs reached a climax over what perhaps would seem to an outsider a trifling matter but which Commander Walker felt was only the opening wedge in an attempt to take the control of the lighthouses, big signals, etc., away from the Navy which had such a deep interest in their efficient management, and he decided to bring the matter to the attention of Hon. W. A. Richardson, Secretary of the Treasury, who was evidently convinced of the correctness of his stand for Commander Walker held this office until May, 1878.

His letter to Secretary Richardson gives such a clear statement of the reasons for objecting to the contention of the Engineer Secretary of the Board that his signature on official papers should precede that of the Naval Secretary that it is given here with only slight omissions.

Letter to Hon. W. A. Richardson, Secretary of the Treasury. 

(from the Lighthouse Board) November 8, 1873......This Board was first organized through the exertions of a few Naval Officers, who, alive to the great importance of a proper organization for administrating the light-house system of this country, possessing so large an extent of sea and lake coast, made a careful study of the Lighthouse systems of Europe and were instrumental in preparing a bill which became a law August 31, 1852. 

In this, the organic law of the Board, precedence is given to the Naval members rather than to the engineer members and to the Naval Secretary rather than to the Engineer Secretary, and such is the case without exception in all laws governing the Board and in all regulations of the Board itself.

Sections 14 and 15 of the organic law were evidently intended to define and limit the duties of the Engineer Secretary, and heretofore, by the regulations of the Board, his duties have been so limited. No limit was set for the duties of the Naval Secretary and in the regulations of the Board, the Naval Secretary was placed in charge of the office and of all business except that especially assigned to the Engineer 

Secretary, viz.: engineering operations, the building and repair of light stations and the preparation of plans, drawings, estimates, etc., for the same.

It was evidently thought in the early days that educated seamen

were the proper persons to be entrusted with the charge of Light Stations (after completion), so necessary to them in the pursuit of their calling, often essential to the preservation of their very lives and erected for their sole benefit.

During the entire existence of the Board, more than twentyone years, an unbroken line of official reports and papers has been signed by, lst, the Chairman; 2nd, the Naval Secretary; 3rd, the

Engineer Secretary, and I have been unable to find a single exception to this rule.

There is no law or regulation saying in what order the signatures shall be attached, but in the absence of special law or regulation custom becomes law, not to be broken without good reason. 

In this case Major Elliot cannot base his claim upon military rank, as I am by many years his senior in rank, nor would this be good were it otherwise, as this is a civil organization where military tank has no place, all the members standing upon a footing af equality. He surely cannot claim that signing after the Naval Secretary can in any case affect the dignity of his position, as he has the example of a long line of engineer officers who have preceded him, some of them men of no ordinary ability, above fear and above reproach, who have made no objection to the order of official signatures, and would undoubtedly be the first to repel the suggestion that in allowing the Naval Secretary precedence, they were guilty of any lack of personal or professional dignity or pride........

I regret exceedingly that a difficulty of this kind should be presented to you for settlement in the midst of your many engrossing cares, but while not responsible for raising the question, I feel it my duty to respectfully protest against any change in the established order of precedence in the matter of signatures or duty, all the laws and regulations and the custom of the office being opposed thereto. 

Precedence in the order of official signatures is of itself a small matter, but in this case it is the first step toward a claim to precedence in far more important things. 

Hon. W. A. Richardson Respectfully, J. G. Walker

Commander U. S. Navy 

Naval Secretary. 

Secretary of the Treasury"

During the five years in the Lighthouse Board, Captain Walker

had much ill health, the result of worry, overwork and strain, as well as few and short vacations and hot summers spent in the trying climate of Washington. He seriously thought, in 1874, of asking to be ordered to command the Portsmouth Navy Yard and considered buying a house in Portsmouth, N. H. where he could establish his family and make it his permanent home; but his services were too valuable on the Light house 

Board and he was kept there till May, 1878, when he asked for a year's leave and once more tried railroad work, this time in Chicago, as Secretary of the Chicago, Burlington and Quincy Railroad. There was much interesting work for him to do during this year in Chicago besides the routine work of the office, but only a suggestion of the variety of matters passing through his hands can be given here. In October, 1879, the Iowa post of the Grand Army of the Rupublic arranged an escort for General Grant across the state and asked the C. B. & Q. railroad for a special train and accommodations for thirty besides his party, and the whole matter, accommodations, feeding, etc., was placed in the hands of 

Captain Walker, who would meet the party at Omaha and escort them to Chicago.

He wrote to Mr. Potter:- "after reaching Omaha we can take care of Grant and should not have too many people belonging to the road. 

Carrying ex-Presidents is an everyday business with the C. B. & Q. and requires no extra crowd of officers (3). I enclose a note from Fred 

Grant. I am a little doubtful about the Old Man's feeling particularly pleased but one can't well judge for others and I suppose it is safer to do too much than too little. I have been thinking over the matter and I think if the Grand Army people don't number over twenty-five or thirty that we could manage to give them a dinner in the dining car by taking a few of the prominent men in when Grant and his party dine and feeding the others later with a good but less elaborate dinner. How does it strike you? They will be a nuisance any way we can handle them... 

Easrlier in this year the officers of the road had been considering the best way of caring for their sick and injured employees and Captain Walker wrote to Surgeon General P. S. Wales of the Navy: "My dear Wales: This company has now more than 1800 miles of road and has just voted at a stockholder' meeting to consolidate in a railroad of 850 miles, which with what we are building will, by December 31st next give us 3000 miles. "We now employ about 15000 men and after the consolidation is consummated, will have nearly 18,000. The question has been raised as to the policy of building a hospital at some central point for sick and injured employees of the company who are worthy of such care. "We now spend a considerable amount of money every year for medical attendance, nurses, etc., etc., and it is claimed that the establishment of a proper hospital will in a series of years add little or nothing to the expenses of the company while in many instances it will be the means of saving limbs and life in some cases which now have to be cared for as best we can in country taverns, farm houses, or wherever we can get the sufferers taken in. "I have taken an interest in the matter and our Vice President

has turned the whole thing over to me to be worked up and reported upon. "I have no doubt that from your zeal in your profession you will be interested in the matter and write to ask if you will give me any and all information you may have at hand with regard to hospitals, etc. etc. which may be of use to me and aid us in coming to a just conclusion. 

Any printed matter which you may not like to part with permanently I will return to you."

Although Captain Walker found his work with the C. B. & Q. interesting and the officers of the road were anxious to keep him as Treasurer of the railroad he did not wish to move his family to Chicago and probably found it harder to give up the Navy than he had expected and at the expiration of his leave he left railroad work. 

In 1880 some Boston men, Mr. Henry L. Higginson, Mr. Charles 

Fairchild and others wanted to associate Captain Walker with them in the management of some paper mills, but he decided that although the report of Mr. Fairchild was very favorable, one hundred thousand dollars was too much for him to put into any one thing. 

In December of that year he received notice from the Navy Department to hold himself in readiness for the command of the Shenandoah, the flagship of the South Atlantic Squadron. In the fall of 1878 there had been rumours that he was to command this vessel when she was ready for sea and was put into commission and he received many letters from men who wished to serve with him. Captain Walker did not care for this command and he knew that his friend, Captain Kirkland, would be very glad to have it as his wife was a native of South America, so after a trip to Washington at this time and a talk with the powers that were, these orders were not issued, for Captain Walker remained with the C. B. & Q. until March 

9th, 1881, when a telegram from the Chief of the Bureau of Navigation offered him the command of the Powhatan, and he accepted by telegram. 

He sent in his resignation to the railroad to take effect on the twelfth and on the eleventh wrote to Commander Dewey: "I skip to Boston tomorrow night to get my brass bound clothes and kiss my children before joining the Powhatan. Our people here are very grumpy and disgusted at my leaving them and I am half inclined to think myself that I am making an ass of myself." He had never liked Chicago and did not care to make his home there and his interest in the Navy to which he had given up so many years of his life was very strong, strong enough to draw him back into the service.

Captain Walker's term of duty on the Powhatan was very short, only about five months; in August, 1881 he was detached and on waiting orders. On the 8th of that month Captain Walker wrote to the Secretary of the Navy, Hon. William H. Hunt - "Sir: I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of the 6th inst. informing me of your purpose to appoint me to the position of Chief of the Bureau of Navigation in the Navy Department. I desire to most heartily thank you for this evidence of your opinion of my professional attainments and for the very kind way in which it is conveyed." 

There was a long delay about the actual issuing of his orders

for President Garfield had been shot in July and was so seriously wounded that it was impossible for him to sign Captain Walker's commission, and it was only in October after President Garfield's death in September and when President Arthur had had time to catch up with the mass of waiting business that he was commissioned Chief of the Bureau of Navigation from October 22, 1881. In November he took his family to Washington and from this time that city became their home. 

As Chief of the Bureau of Navigation, Captain Walker had the temporary tank of Commodore, and he was also head of the Office of Detail attached to that bureau in 1865, which meant that he had charge, of course under the Secretary, of issuing all orders to the officers and men of the Navy and directing the movements of all naval vessels, also receiving reports from all stations.

On March 23, 1882 an Office of Intelligence was established in the Bureau of Navigation and "to facilitate this work the Department Library will be combined with the Office of Intelligence and placed under the direction of the Chief of the Bureau of Navigation." A week later the duties of the Bureau were defined as comprising "all that relates to the Naval Observatory, Nautical Almanac, Hydrographic Office, Department 

Library and Office of Intelligence". The latter office was founded largely through the efforts of Captain Walker who had long felt that there should be a systematized method of collecting and filing matters of naval interest from all over the world, and officers especially appointed to watch the development of Scientific discoveries and inventions in times of peace and in case of war the office should have charge of securing information of military value.

The office of Assistant Secretary of the Navy had not been created at that time but Captain Walker filled the place later assigned to the Assistant Secretary and was Acting Secretary during the absence of Secretary Chandler. It will be seen that the duties of his office were many and varied and entailed heavy responsibilities and much overwork and strain, while great tact was needed to resist improper political interference with the department, and pressure to secure desirable berths for friends or contracts for favored firms; such requests could not be summarily refused even when unwise or impossible of fulfillment for it was very necessary to retain the goodwill of Congress in order to secure the funds necessary for the maintenance and building up of the Navy; the necessary diplomacy and compromises were often trying and made more difficult by newspapers. Articles appeared referring to 

Secretary Walker and Commodore Chandler, evidently intended to arouse the Secretary's jealousy and diminish Captain Walker's influence. 

The situation become difficult and relations with the Secretary of the Navy, William E. Chandler, became so strained that in 1883 Captain 

Walker wrote to Senator Allison, who had married Miss Neally, Captain

Walker's adopted sister, "I am just now undergoing the process of being frozen out. Chandler, who I think likes me personally well enough, has evidently made up his mind to take from me one duty after another until he forces me to resign as a chief of the bureau. I judge that his jealousy has been aroused by squibs in the papers, written for the purpose, and by the talk of certain officers in the line who dislike to

to have a captain, comparatively young, in a position of some consequence and power in Naval matters. The old fogy element rebels and their ranks are swelled by all the dead beats, and the staff as a body. 

Impression enough has been made upon Chandler to start the ball and as he is active and vicious when started upon anything, I feel quite sure that he will go forward and that within a few days, perhaps, my connection with the Navy Department will have ceased. When I cannot remain here and retain my self-respect I shall of course tender my resignation as Chief of the Bureau...

Evidently matters reached a climax quickly for on the same day he wrote the following letter to the Secretary of the Navy. "Letter to William E. Chandler, Secretary of the Navy.

Sir: 

October 27, 1883.

On the 18th inst. there was placed upon my desk a printed order, No. 309, taking from me the duty of receiving reports as to the movements of the ships of the Navy and preparing, under your direction, orders for their guidance. Of your intention to make this important change in my duties I had no intimation until I saw it in print, although I had been in daily confidential intercourse with you. This was followed by the receipt at my house on the evening of the 24th inst. after a day spent with you at the Navy Department, of your letter of that date informing me that you had decided to take from the Chief of the Bureau of Navigation the Office of Detail, which has been in his charge for nearly nineteen years. "My principal duties in the Navy Department having been thus summarily taken from me, self respect requires me to resign my commission as a Chief of the Bureau, and I have the honour to enclose herewith my resignation as Chief of the Bureau of Navigation and to request you to forward it to the President. "This appointment came to me unsought, it found me in command of the Powhatan, where I had desired to remain until I should have completed my service afloat as a captain.....I have the honour to ask to be restored to the command from which I was taken, that I may complete the usual service at sea." 

Secretary Chandler evidently did not really wish to dispense with the services of his able assistant for in spite of a certain amount of jealousy he relied greatly on Captain Walker's judgment and knowledge of men, so that after this little flurry matters settled down with Captain Walker still Chief of the Bureau of Navigation, where he gathered under him a corps of able and energetic young officers who were really interested in helping their chief to build up the Navy. His choice of assistants was almost always wise; after his death Admiral S. A. Staunton, who had served on his staff during the cruise of the White Squadron and later on the Pacific station as

well as taking a civil position under him on the Isthmian Canal Commission, wrote of him, "He was a remarkable man....the ablest and biggest man in his knowledge of men and his grasp of large questions that I have ever known."

Since Walker was not to go to sea at this time but to retain his place in the department, he planned to take a vacation in the summer of 1886 to visit his wife and children who were then in Germany, but before this was arranged he was taken sick, his illness proving a severe one. When he was better, indeed well but far from strong, Secretary 

Whitney ordered him a special duty to England, Germany and France, with which he could combine a visit to his family. He was still so weak that two friends went with him to New York and did not feel easy until they saw him safely on the steamer. The ocean trip and the complete rest did him good and he was quite ready upon reaching Liverpool for a busy week, talking to ship and engine builders, visiting plants, etc. 

After a holiday with his family in Heidelberg, Commodore Walker returned to work with renewed strength and vigor. About this time he was much interested in plans for the new Naval Observatory to be built at Washington, as well as the construction of new modern vessels for the Navy; that he himself was deeply interested in the work of the 

Office of Naval Intelligence is evident from the following letter: "July 22, 1887

Commander Charles V. Gridley, U.S.N. 

Office of Lighthouse Inspector, Buffalo, N. Y. 

My dear Gridley:

I want to get certain information for the office of Naval Intelligence with regard to canals in Canada, the best points to strike at for blowing up locks and obstructing the canals in case of necessity, the fortifications of Kingston, Toronto and other points, etc. etc. In fact information that would be needed both for the protection of our own interests and to injure the enemy in case of a war with England. 

I have been thinking somewhat of sending one or two officers from the office of Naval Intelligence to obtain all the information possible, and have thought a little of going with them myself. I suppose if we could have a few days in your tender for passing through the Welland Canal and for visiting a few points on Lake Ontario it would assist us much and would enable us to see a good deal without attracting any attention to ourselves.

Will it be possible for you to help us out in this way for five or six days, say from about the 16th of August to about the 20th?

It is possible that we may not go and I recognize the fact that the work of your district may be such that you cannot help us, but I will be glad to have you let me know as soon as you can what you think of the chances. 

Of course if we go up there we want nothing said about it and we desire to get on without attracting attention in any way. We should simply be two

or three gentlemen travelling for pleasure and while on board the Haze simply friends of yours taking a little run in the steamer with you; therefor please do not speak of the matter to anybody. Please let me hear from you......."

While Chief of the Bureau Captain Walker was very earnest in his efforts to build up the Navy; new vessels were urgently needed, many of those in commission being obsolete and quite useless in case of war with a European power. He also took steps to have able young men who did well in their studies at Annapolis given an opportunity for further study along some line in which they were interested. Men who ranked high upon graduation were sent to military schools in England and France to perfect themselves in some branch of their profession ordnance, naval construction or some technical naval matters; others who were interested in a scientific subject were sent to the Smithsonian Institute to carry in their studies, Captain Walker feeling that a wider education tended to make better officers and provide them with an interest during cruises in out of the way places. His own ability was becoming recognized by this time outside of the Navy an in 1882 Charles Scribner's Sons asked him to prepare the third part of the Naval History of the War of the Rebellion which they were planning to publish, the part concerned with the operations in the Gulf and inland waters, but he felt obliged, reluctantly, to decline as his work in the department was so exacting, taking all his time and strength. 

The civilian world has very little realization of the amount of diplomatic work done by the officers of the Navy; the routine work of their profession takes them all over the world and their appearance in any country causes no comment so that a tactful and discreet officer may be of great use in bringing about cordial relations between nations where there has been some misunderstanding of the acts of his government or can secure information about conditions in a country where a regular envoy would be looked upon with suspicion and be unable to get reliable information.

Such a situation arose in 1885 when a revolutionary movement in Columbia spread through several departments including Panama. When 

President Nunez in that year, disregarded the constitution of 1863 which made the component states sovereign he was strongly opposed by the people of Panama, who resented the loss of their local government. In order to protect the railroad and secure safe passage of traffic across the Isthmus in these disturbed times, it became necessary to land Marines at Panama and Colon in accordance with the terms of a concession under which the railway had been built. Captain McCalla, U.S.N. was sent in command of this expedition, consisting of the second battalion of Marines and two battalions of bluejackets, and in addition to his official instructions from the Navy Department directing him to "open the transit and to protect the lives and property of American citizens" he received the following letter from Captain Walker: "April 6, 1885

My dear McCalla: It is of considerable importance in sending off such an expedition as that which you are to command to the Isthmus that we

should keep the country with us in the matter and for that reason you, with such officers with you as you think most judicious, should keep the department informed of what occurs, how things are progressing and how they look to you, explain the political situation, go into the whole subject of Isthmus troubles as far as you can, and do not spare the telegraph in sending us information. We want to have everything of any importance in the way of information that it may be given out to the press, and the people kept in accord with the department. Of course we must also avoid any appearance of overdoing the thing or trying to make capital. "While you are on the Isthmus you must also go and examine the islands in the Bay of Panama belonging to the Pacific Mail Steamship Company, as well as Tobago and the islands in its immediate neighborhood upon which the English Pacific Mail Company have some works. The islands belonging to the Pacific Mail Company are reported to be perfectly healthy. Give them careful examination and report what you think about them, how completely they would control the Bay of Panama if we were to occupy them with a permanent force, whether good fresh water can be had on them, or whether it would be necessary to collect cistern water; the depth of water and how near heavy ships can approach- in fact everything necessary to the thorough understanding of the value of those islands to us should we desire to occupy and hold them with an armed force. I write you all this confidentially. 

There will be no record of it in the Navy Department, but you may consider it an order to be carefully carried out. I will send with this an order for Captain Norton of the Shenandoah to put his steam cutter, or other boats at your disposal while in Panama. 

Very truly yours. #1 Commodore Walker expected to go to sea upon the expiration of his four year term of service in the Bureau of Navigation in the fall of 1885, but to his surprise in October he was reappointed by the President for another four years. This was a most unusual thing and it was very gratifying to find that his work in the office had been more widely appreciated than he had realized. His confirmation by the Senate did not come until January, the Senate meeting in that month, and he wrote to "Jan. 14, 1886 ......Chandler's attacks upon me and the attempts to prevent my being confirmed by the Senate made me think it better to remain here until that matter was settled. I have had no fear as to the result but did not know what these fellows might say or do. I was confirmed yesterday and so far as I know, without opposition."

Mr. Chandler was no longer in the Cabinet but from his retirement amused himself by publishing anonymous articles, whose authorship was, however, well known, in newspapers and magazines evidently intended to stir up jealousy of Commodore Walker's standing in the Navy Department and to rouse opposition to his continuance in office. 

Disturbances on the Isthmus continued; the large sums of money

spent in Panama by the French Canal Company made that country the happy hunting ground of corrupt officials of the central government which had deprived the department of Panama of all local self government and it suffered greatly at their hands.

In November, 1886, Secretary of State Bayard wrote to Mr. W. C. 

Whitney, Secretary of the Navy, that he had received information from the Panama Railroad Company of the existence on the Isthmus, of a feverish state of things, unfavorable to American interests and suggesting the desirability of sending one or more men-of-war to Colon to report the condition of affairs. 

"To Rear Admiral Luce, U.S.S. Tennessee. 

November 6, 1886. 

My dear Luce: 

I send you herewith a letter from the Secretary directing you to send one of your ships to Aspinwall. I have just been reading a lot of documents received from the State Department with regard to Isthmus matters.......Mr. Bayard attaches a good deal of importance to getting a ship to Aspinwall as soon as possible and I think you had better send the Yantic with all practicable despatch. I shall advise the Secretary to also send one of the Pacific Squadron to Panama. There seems to be a good deal of ill feeling towards Americans on the Isthmus, and trouble is liable to break out at any time. I am somewhat of a believer in the ounce of prevention theory and shall therefore try to get one of the 

Pacific Squadron ships to Panama as soon as possible........" 

My dear Sir: 

"CONFIDENTIAL Department of State 

Washington 

December 9, 1887

On the 13th of January last I brought up in a meeting of the Cabinet the subject of the application of the Hawaiian Minister to me in relation to Midway Island.

The subject of the proposition was that as the United States had by virtue of alleged discovery and occupation obtained the island of 

Midway in the Pacific Ocean (Latitude 28° 12' north and Longitude 177° 

12' west from Greenwich) many years ago - yet that as no use had been made of the island the United States might not object to cede it to the 

Hawaiian Government.

When I mooted the subject you promised to look into it and obtain a report from the proper bureau of the Navy Department.

In view of late occurances in the Sandwich Islands and other movements by Germany in the Samoan Islands, it has occurred to me that it might be as well for the United States to make some expressive movement by way of a visit of one of our national vessels to Midway Island, the re-erection of a flag and mast and possibly landing some coal there which being made publicly known would serve to answer any suggestion of nonuse, or abandonment of title by the United States. 

The Hydrographic Bureau has charts of the harbor at Midway, and in the Executive Document No. 79, 40th Congress, 2nd Session will be found a report by the Secretary of the Navy (Mr. Welles) on the subject. 

Yours very truly, Hon. W. C. Whitney, Secretary of the Navy.

Commodore Walker 

Personal " (signed) T. F. Bayard"

In September, 1889, after eight years of arduous and unremitting work, Commodore Walker tendered his resignation as Chief of the Bureau of Navigation on the 30th and was ordered to command the newly formed Squadron of Evolution, made up of the newest vessels of the Navy, which it was felt would compare favorably with those of the same type of any other nation. 

This squadron of cruisers was ordered to the European station and the whole country was enthusiastic about the new vessels and proud to show the European nations that at last we had some vessels worthy of the country. 

With his orders Commodore Walker received the following letter: "Sir:

You are hereby appointed an Acting Rear Admiral in the Navy of the United States, such appointment to hold good while in command of the Squadron of Evolution. 

You are authorized to hoist the Flag and wear the uniform of Rear Admiral, and to affix the title of Rear Admiral to your official signature. Respectfully, B. F. Tracy, Secretary of the Navy."

He reported for duty on October 1st, the squadron then lying

at the New York Navy Yard preparing for sea. On October 30th he was given a Farewell Banquet in Washington, an unheard of attention to a retiring Chief of Bureau. Rear Admiral C. R. P. Rogers, as Toastmaster, made the following graceful little speech: "Gentlemen:

We are here tonight, to offer homage of our best wishes and most cordial esteem to an eminent officer, who, after long and distinguished service among us, has been promoted to a still higher trust, and is about to leave us for service afloat, that indicates the confidence of the Government he has served so well. 

I should do better justice to this occasion, if I should put into words what we all feel, as to his high professional merit and great personal worth, but am advised by the Committee of Arrangements that speech making is not in order tonight, and I shall only beg you to fill your glasses and join me, when, in your name, I wish with unstinted measure health, happiness, and the most brilliant success to our friend, Rear Admiral Walker, the Commander-in-Chief of the new 

Squadron of Evolution."

This was followed by a letter from the Secretary of the Navy which showed most gratifying appreciation of what Admiral Walker had done during his long term in the Bureau of Navigation and quite overcame, as well as surprised, the Admiral. It was such an unusual tribute to the work and accomplishments of a Chief of Bureau that it seems worth while to give it here in its entirety. "Navy Department, October 30, 1889.

Sir: 

Upon the occasion of your withdrawal from the Bureau of Navigation, of which you have been for eight years the Chief, I take pleasure in expressing to you my strong sense of the lasting service which you have rendered to the Navy Department, and of its loss at your departure. Your large experience and ripe judgment in all matters of 

Naval organization and administration have made your counsel of peculiar value, at a time when the material development of the Navy presented many new and difficult problems. It will be a satisfaction to you to know that, in this critical period, your good sense, your breadth of view on all professional questions, and your devotion to the best interests of the service, have left a permanent mark upon the labors of the Department. Your wide personal knowledge of the officers on the 

Navy list, your sound discrimination of character, and the spirit of fairness with which you have performed the arduous and thankless task of assignment to duty, have done much to check the abuses of favoritism, and to regulate employment upon wholesome and normal principles. The 

Bureau of Navigation, as the only branch of departmental administration directly concerned in the management of the fleet, has a most important influence in raising or lowering the character of the navy personnel,

and to its well-directed efforts under your guidance are due, in large measure, the good order, discipline and harmony which now prevail in the service. Such a result could only be accomplished by untiring industry and watchfulness, an unswerving tenacity of purpose, and a resolute stand on the side of right and duty. 

It is peculiarly appropriate that at the close of your responsible duties here you should be entrusted with the command of the first squadron of the reconstructed navy. It is not in the power of the department to confer upon you any higher distinction, and your selection for the command is made not only from a knowledge of your special fitness for the post, but as a recognition of the services you have hitherto performed. There is much still to be considered in determining the direction of future naval development, and in building up a system of fleet organization adapted to modern conditions; and the naval administration will be largely aided in arriving at its conclusions by the experience of your squadron. Therefore, while I regret your departure, I look with satisfaction upon your present assignment; and in giving you my best wishes for the future, I can only express the hope and the conviction that the successful efforts which have marked your long career of usefulness in the Bureau will continue, with equal advantage to the service, in your new field of action. 

Very respectfully, B. F. Tracy 

Secretary of the Navy. 

Rear Admiral 

John G. Walker, U. S. Navy, Chief of the Bureau of Navigation"

Admiral Walker was much perturbed when the time for speeches came to find that he had left his carefully prepared "impromptu" reply at home; he hurried his messenger, John Smith, off to get it, but alas! it did not arrive in time to be used. However, he was so moved by the entirely unexpected tokens of appreciation and regard that he had no need of prompting and his few answering remarks of thanks were heartfelt and warmly received.

He had often been discouraged and felt that those in authority did not appreciate the needs of the Navy or the difficulties of his task but his resignation from the Navy Department called forth many expressions of esteem and recognition of what he had done for the Navy which more than compensated for his years of hard work. It is impossible to give them all but one letter will give an idea of many received at this time.

Personal 

Navy Yard 

Norfolk, Va. 

My dear Admiral:

I regret to know that at last the direction of active affairs in the Navy has passed out of your hands. As I am on the eve of sailing on the cruise that you have so kindly arranged for me, I cannot refrain from writing to thank you most cordially and sincerely for your great and uniform courtesy and kindness to me personally, not only while you have been at the head of affairs in the Service, but ever since I have had the pleasure of being known to you. I am convinced that the whole service will regret your absence from the part which your distinguished talents and great ability have enabled you to fill so well, and that added to this regret, you will be followed by a measure of affectionate esteem and friendship, not awarded to any other officer in the Service, I trust to live to see you yet, Vice Admiral and Admiral of the Navy, and shall feel highly honored if in any way I can contribute to such an end, and more so still, if I can serve under your command in both these capacities. 

Very sincerely and truly yours,

C. H. Rockwell, U.S.N. 

Rear Admiral J. G. Walker, U. S. Navy"

Great enthusiasm was felt throughout the country about the "new Navy", the first representatives of which were the vessels comprising the Squadron of Evolution, which were admittedly the last word in vessels of their class, and being painted white caused the squadron to be popularly known as the "White Squadron". The vessels were cruisers and were named for cities of the United States; the flagship, commanded by Captain Robeson, was the Chicago and the three other vessels were the 

Boston, Captain O'Kane, the Atlanta, Captain Howell, and the Yorktown,

Captain Chadwick. This squadron of fine modern vessels, the nucleus of the new navy authorized by Congress, left the North River, New York on 

November 18th, amid enthusiastic demonstrations of interest. Secretary

Tracy of the Navy was present on the despatch boat, Dolphin, to wish them God Speed and Ex-Secretaries of the Navy, Robeson and Whitney, and 

Rear Admiral Gherardi, commander of the North Atlantic Squadron and staff,

General Howard commanding the Department of the Atlantic and his staff, several members of Congress and other dignataries, and the guns of the vessels saluted the Secretary, after which the band of the flagship played. At eleven o'clock the squadron got under way, amid the shouts of the crowd on shore, steamed a short distance upstream, turned and came down in line of squadron to be reviewed by the Secretary. As the 

Chicago came up to the reviewing line, the receiving ship anchored at 

Twenty-third Street, fired a Rear-Admiral's salute of thirteen guns; the marines on the Chicago, drawn up by the rail, presented arms as she

passed the Dolphin and the reviewing party. When the review was over the Dolphin followed the squadron down river, salutes were fired from Governor's Island and Forts Hamilton and Wadsworth; all the way down the river and harbor tugs, ferry boats and steamers tooted and flags were run up by sailing craft. The weather outside being bad the squadron dropped anchor just inside Sandy Hook to wait more favorable weather for the trip to Boston, where it was eagerly awaited and many festivities planned for its stay.

The arrival of the vessels in Boston November twenty-second was witnessed by enthusiastic thousands on wharves and house tops and their stay was filled with entertainments, a succession of dinners and receptions, following the exchange of official visits between Admiral Walker and the State and City officials. The vessels were open to the public for three days and thousands visited them, at times crowding them quite uncomfortably, but, to quote the New York Herald: "Every one, however, received the most courteous treatment. All the officers, indeed seem to take their cue from Admiral Walker, who is one of the most approachable and courteous of gentlemen, and whose social qualities are winning him friends every day." One afternoon was given up to a reception for Admiral Walker's personal friends.

The Squadron sailed from Boston on December 7th for Lisbon en route to the Mediterranean, expecting to arrive there on the 23rd of the month. A letter sent by Admiral Walker to the Superintendent of the Navy Yard at that city, expressing his appreciation of courtesies shown him is written in the formal official style of Europe an diplomacy. 

Lisbon, Portugal 

Dec. 31st, 1889

The undersigned Rear Admiral Commanding the United States Squadron of Evolution presents his compliments to Captain the Visconde de Paco Darcos Superintendent of His Majesty's Naval Yard at Lisbon and wishes to thank him for his courtesy in supplying fresh water for the use of the United States Squadron at Lisbon, and for his efforts to obtain the anchor which was lost in the river Tagus by the flagship Chicago.

He will have the pleasure of reporting the same to the honorable Secretary of the United States Navy and can assure him that the naval authorities of the United States will reciprocate with pleasure in case any of His Majesty's ships should need similar services in the United States. 

The undersigned, the Rear Admiral, commanding the United

States Squadron of Evolution begs Captain the Visconde de Paco Darcos will accept the assurance of his highest condideration. 

J. G. Walker."

From Lisbon to Gibraltar for a fortnight, where the British army and navy officers were most hospitable; Colonel Gordon and the officers of the Black Watch invited Admiral Walker to consider himself an honorary member of their mess and Lord Charles Beresford on the H.M.S. Undaunted was host at an entertainment. Next to Cartagena, Spain, for three days and then to Port Mahon, Minorca where he hoped for permission to land his men, under arms, but without ball cartridges, for drill, a very good thing for the men and difficult to accomplish at most ports. At Port Mahon the band of the Chicago took part in a performance given for the benefit of the municipal charities, in fact, wherever the Squadron went every effort was made to bring about cordial good feelings between European countries and the United States. Then to Toulon, where his stay was lengthened beyond his expectation because the harbor of Villefranche was occupied by the entire French squadron, which left no room for his four vessels. By 

February 11th, however, the squadron was at anchor in the harbor of Villefranche, where the officers spent a very pleasant three weeks being delightfully entertained at Nice and Monte Carlo. Admiral Walker in turn lent the band of the flagship for some occasions on shore and of course gave several entertainments, dinners, receptions and so forth on the flagship. Great interest in the vessels was shown everywhere and many well known and distinguished men were the guests of Admiral Walker. 

Duke of Cambridge and party lunched on the Chicago. The After leaving Villefranche the squadron was divided for a time as the Italian government forbade any foreign nation to have more than three naval vessels in a port at ore time and limited their stay to one week. But Admiral Walker received special permission to remain at Naples with the Chicago, Boston and Atlanta ten days beyond this time limit. 

Next Corfu, Greece. The importance felt in maintaining the dignity of the United States even in small matters is shown in the following letter to the U. S. Consular Agent at Corfu "Dear Mr. Woodly,

The American flag is hoisted at the signal station in the same hoist with and under two other national flags. Of course the display of the flag is intended as a compliment, but it is not proper to hoist any national flag under another one and Admiral Walker requests that you will send without delay to the proper person and request that the United States flag be removed from the hoist. 

Very truly yours, S. A. Staunton. 

April 6, 1890."

Then Malta and Algiers where he received cables from the Navy Department which seemed to indicate a change of plan for the squadron, probably orders to proceed to South America; Admiral Walker wired for information and proceeded to Gilbraltar to await his written orders. 

After the arrival of money and supplies which had been shipped from England, as well as the expected orders stating that the President

deemed it important that the squadron under Admiral Walker's command should visit South America and directing him to assume the command of the South American Station, the Squadron, on May 26th, left Gilbraltar and went across to Tangier for a night and then sailed for Madeira on the way to South America. The object of sending Admiral Walker to 

Brazi with his squadron was to extend the good wishes of the United States to the new republic, the United States of Brazil, which had been formed after a military conspiracy in 1889 had deposed the Emperor. 

They visited Bahia, Rio de Janeiro and reached New York July 30th. 

In August boats and marines from the squadron took part in the Ericson ceremonies at New York and October 1st the Chicago went to the New York Navy Yard for repairs and to be put in condition for renewed sea service.

In November, 1890 Brazil sent a squadron to the United States; the officers were received by the President and dined by the Secretaries of State and Navy, and the State Department advanced to Admiral Walker $1,000.00 for entertaining the officers, of which he returned $313.56 unexpended. In January the squadron went to Galveston, Texas and from there with the Chicago, Dolphin and Yorktown to New Orleans, arriving in the midst of the Rex Reception on Mardi Gras. The Admiral and officers of the squadron were warmly received and royally entertained during their stay. During the stay at New Orleans came an invitation from the Mayor of Vicksburg to take the squadron to that city, the scene of so much fighting during the War of the Rebellion, in which Admiral Walker took a prominent part. He was much pleased by the invitation but felt obliged to send the following reply: "15th February, 1891

A. V. Booth, Esq. 

Mayor of Vicksburg 

Dear Sir:

I have the pleasure of acknowledging the receipt of your telegram of yesterday as follows:- 'I send a cordial invitation to you and your gallant fleet to extend your trip to our city. Our generous hearted people will greet you with a warm Southern welcome to the scene of your former triumphs', which I have today answered in these terms: 'Accept my hearty thanks for your kind invitation and my sincere regrets that I cannot visit your city. I should be delighted to meet in friendly intercourse your people whose qualities as opponents commanded my most profound respect. It is a matter of great regret to me that I am unable to make the personal acquaintance of the representative men of their section; but I am too much pressed for time to carry such a plan into effect. The ships were unduly delayed for repairs at the North. I finally sailed with a part of my command and the remaining vessels are now on their way to join me. The orders of the Department require a rendez-vous in the Gulf and an extended series of drills to be completed before the arrival of hot weather, and therefor render a delay impracticable. I explain my reasons thus far at length because I wish you and your people to understand how fully I appreciate your

courteous and chivalrous invitation and how much I regret my inability to accept it' ".

After a stay of ten days the Admiral proceeded to Pensacola, with the Chicago, Boston, Atlanta, Dolphin and Yorktown where the vessels remained several days. At Port Tampa there was target practice of small arms and secondary batteries and the Chicago went outside for practice with her main battery.

In July, 1891 the Squadron took part in manoeuvres with the Massachusetts Naval Militia at Boston which the Governor of Massachusetts watched from the Chicago and later in the month there were drills at 

Fisher's Island with the New York Naval Reserve. The Vesuvius and the torpedo boat, Cushing, were now with the squadron. 

In writing to Lieutenant Commander J. W. Miller, commanding the first battalion of the Naval Reserve Artillery of the State of New York, after praising the work and the spirit of that organization, Admiral Walker says: "The movement for the formation of auxiliary naval forces had had from its inception my interest and support. I consider it a most important factor in a practical and logical scheme of national defense. Elaborate and costly material the best ships and guns will not realize their full value in time of war unless manned from the beginning of hostilities by men of training and experience." He commended the patriotism of the young men of Massachusetts and New York who had joined the pioneer forces in their respective states.

Cities all along the southern coast of New England were eager for visits from the squadron, the people everywhere being anxious to see the new vessels. 

Admiral Walker sailed December, 1891 from Fortress Monroe with the Chicago, Atlanta and Bennington for Montevideo, Uruguay. There was then some threat of trouble between Chile and the United States, Chile fearing the intervention of the United States in the Chile-Argentine boundary dispute, and in January, 1892 Admiral Walker was receiving reports from a naval officer in Montevideo in regard to munititons of war being shipped to Chile from that port, supposed to be torpedoes. On 

February 2nd the Minister of the United States at Buenos Ayres sent him the following extract from a cablegram from the Secretary of State, James G. Blaine: "the pending questions between the United States and

Chile bid fair to yield to pacific treatment," and Walker judged the Chilean war excitement to be about over. 

The United States Minister at Montevideo was anxious to have the vessels visit La Plata but Admiral Walker wished to await the arrival of the Philadelphia and Concord, which were hourly expected. If he visited La Plata he wanted to take five new ships which would make a far greater impression than a smaller number. "I shall be very glad to do anything in my power to impress these people with the fact that the United States is something more than a little South American republic." 

In March, 1892 Admiral Walker took the Chicago, Atlanta and

Bennington to the Ensenada, the port of La Plata, Argentina, and received a very warm and enthusiastic welcome. During their stay of ten days, the officers were almost overwhelmed with the entertainments arranged for them, which they returned to the best of their ability, giving receptions to officials and leading citizens and throwing the vessels open to inspection. The trains between Ensenada and La Plata were so thronged by visitors to the squadron that the President of the railroad announced a five per cent appreciation in the market value of the stock! The visiting officials and officers were much struck by "the robust frames of the seamen and the severe cleanliness of the ships". A visit to the Tigre torpedo station showed the American officers an "amount of war material far in excess of anything of that character which their goverment might pretend to have at hand". The cordiality shown to the American squadron was reported to have disquieted Chile, there being rumors in that country of an alliance between Argentina and the United States. The visit was altogether very successful and the Admiral won golden opinions everywhere. 

Again in the summer of 1892 there were exercises of the First Naval Battalion of New York Naval Reserve in conjunction with the Squadron of Evolution. The squadron was kept busy all summer with drills and visits to various ports on the North Atlantic coast. On September 9th Admiral 

Walker in a letter to the Adjutant General of the State of Rhode Island and Providence Plantations expresses regret that he can not at that time accept the invitation of the Governor to visit Providence with his ships as he was then on his way to New York with the Chicago and feared that the revolution in Venezuela would call the squadron in that direction. 

This did indeed happen, and on September 20th Admiral Walker was at La Guayra, Venezuela, having made the trip from New York in eight days, with the Chicago and Concord, at least, but he hesitated to go to Caracas, the capitol, to confer with the United States Conqul as there were rumors that the railroad might be cut and communication between the two cities interfered with, and while he did not consider any of the rumors reliable, he was unwilling to run the risk of being cut off from his command. Both the government and the revolutionists wished to be on friendly terms with the United States.

At this time there were battleships of five nations in the harbor of La Guayra and Admiral Walker being the ranking officer, the officers of all nationalities considered him as in command and followed his lead in all steps taken to help non-combatants and wounded. 

On the 28th there was something of a scare in La Guayra, there was skirmishing towards Macuto during the day and the people on shore were a good deal excited and frightened. 

Admiral Walker sent an officer to General Quintero at one o'clock on that day to suggest the sending of a car, under a flag of truce and the Geneva cross, to Macuto to bring the wounded to La Guayra. 

General Quintero said that it was impossible, as his instructions from Caracas did not permit him to hold any communication with the revolutionists. 

On October 2nd Admiral Walker heard of comment among the principal residents of La Guayra that medical assistance had been given by

surgeons of the American and other foreign men-of-war to the wounded at Macuto which was in the hands of the revolutionists while the same assistance had not been given at La Guayra; he wrote at once to the 

United States Consul: "It is within your personal knowledge that on the 29th of September I sent my flag-lieutenant, Lieutenant Staunton, on shore to call upon General Quinteros, commanding La Guayra, to inform him that I had heard that, in the engagement of the night before, a considerable number of men had been wounded, and to offer him surgical assistance from my squadron. You accompanied Lieutenant Staunton to the interview with General Quinteros, and Mr. Anderson, the British Vice- 

Consul, interpreted. General Quinteros replied that he had but one wounded and needed no assistance. Lieutenant Staunton then, in my name, made the offer a standing one.......Will you kindly communicate the contents of this letter to your colleagues of the Consular Corps in La Guayra".

Although this offer of assistance was declined, the surgeons had their hands full at Macuto where they established a hospital for the care of the wounded, who were mostly government soldiers who had fallen into the hands of the revolutionists.

At this time England, France, Germany and Spain had each a war vessel at La Guayra and their commanders co-operated most cordially with Admiral Walker who, as being senior in rank, took the lead in measures to protect foreigners and non-combatants. A letter written by Admiral Walker to his wife at this time and dated October 4th gives a good picture of the situation and a portion of it follows: "I have nothing to do here with the Venezuelan boundary question. I am here because of the unsettled state of the country, to look after American commerce and interests. The whole country is up in arms under different chiefs, each contending for power and plunder. 

It is a war of intrigue rather than bloodshed, and every chief fights on the side that seems to promise best for himself personally. 

General Crespo, who is at the head of the revolution, seems to be generally considered by foreigners as one of the best men in the country. He has a large following and is supposed to have many more troops than the Government de facto; but he is probably short of arms and ammunition, and has had, undoubtedly, dissensions in his own camp. 

Since my arrival here, two weeks ago yesterday, all kinds of reports have been flying about as to his where abouts and movements. I believe, however, that he has been at, and in the neighborhood of, Valencia. It is now said that he is marching on Caracus, and there seems to be good grounds for the report. The principal mercantile house in Caracus telephoned its agents here, this evening, that Crespo was at a small village within twelve or thirteen miles of Caracus, which agrees with information received yesterday from the Spanish minister through the Captain of the Spanish cruiser now in port. If Crespo captured Caracus the present government will fall at once, and I think substantial peace will be established for a time. As soon as this matter is over, I expect to sail for home; but, although we have reports by the score, they are so untrustworthy that I shall have no confidence in Crespo's success until success comes.

June now I have a question upon my hands which it is not easy to decide. The Government de facto has decreed a blockade of two ports held by the Crespists; one of them - Puerto Cabello is a port of call for the "Red D" steamers and had no blockading vessels off there until 

Friday morning, the 30th of September, the morning that one of the "Red D" steamers was due. The Kears age was off the port and escorted her in, one of the blockaders steaming up and taking a look at them, but making no signal nor attempting to speak her. The mail steamer came out Saturday evening, finding no blockading vessels, and came directly here, entered at the Custom House, discharged and received cargo, cleared and sailed again this evening for Puerto Cabello. This P.M. I received a letter from our Minister in Caracus enclosing copies of a correspondence between the Minister of Foreign Affairs and himself, and Bolton & Co., agents for the steamers, and himself. The Minister of 

Foreign Affairs complains bitterly of the conduct of the Kearsage, and threatens death and destruction to anything that attempts to pass the blockade. The whole thing is very absurd. Puerto Cabello is only 60 or 70 miles from here and the de facto goverment claims to blockade it. 

The steamer after breaking the blockade there comes directly here, enters at the Custom House, discharges and receives cargo and passengers without any interference by the government officials at this point. 

There has been more or less skirmishing for the last week in and about the little village of Macuto, a couple of miles east to La Guayra. I offered surgical assistance to the officer commanding the government troops in La Guayra, but he professes to have no occasion for it. Learning that a considerable number of wounded were lying at 

Macuto, mostly government soldiers, prisoners in the hands of the revolutionists, absolutely without medical or surgical aid of any kind, I sent the Chicago's surgeons to look after them. They have had the assistance of surgeons from the German, Spanish and English ships, and have had a good deal of work to do, including a number of amputations and different operations. While they have been at work the village has been captured by the government forces, retaken by the revolutionists, and again captured by the Government troops, who are still in possession at sunset this evening.

Street fighting has been going on at times while our Doctors were operating. Dr. Urie, of this ship, while passing along the street, was fired upon, but as the soldier firing was at a considerable distance he missed. One night the operating room was broken into and robbed by soldiers; they carried off clothes, operating aprons, a stretcher for carrying wounded, some instruments, etc. They are a regular set of brigands and nothing is too bad for them. Some of the things stolen have been recovered, part of them in La Guayra, where they were being publicly sold in the streets. 

Wednesday, Oct. 5th. This is the anniversary of my entering the Navy. 

Forty-two years ago! ...I am now going into the fourth year of my cruise, having joined the ship Oct. 1st, 1889. Trains loaded with troops for Caracus were sent from here last evening and another this morning. It looks very much as if reports of fighting in the neighborhood of Caracus were correct.

Wednesday evening. Reports have reached me from various sources, all practically to the same effect, that fighting was going on yesterday and continued today within a few miles of Caracus. Judging from the reports the fighting was severe and favorable to the revolutionists, the Government forces apparently meeting with a severe defeat. It looks as if 

General Crespo would occupy Caracus within the next day or two. Telephone communication is still open with Caracus, and messages from merchants to their agents here, and from Ministers to Consuls and Naval Officers, all substantially agree. The people in Caracus are very fearful that the town will be pillaged by the defeated government troops, who will re-enter it in a very disorganized condition, and probably have a day before General Crespo's army will come in and get fully in charge. 

The people here, where there are many foreigners, largely Spanish, also fear the town will be sacked, as has been threatened. Threats have also been made that in case the revolutionists enter La Guayra the town will be fired upon from the fort on a hill overlooking it. I, this evening, called a conference of commanding officers of ships of war now in the harbor, and, following my lead, they all signed a note to the Commanding General informing him that we should not permit the town to be pillaged; that if attempted we should promptly take the necessary steps to stop it, which means landing a force and taking possession of the town. We also informed him that if the fort fired upon the town the ships here would promptly open fire on the fort. Staunton is now on shore for the purpose of delivering the note to the Commanding General. I suppose he may find it a somewhat difficult job, as everybody is frightened and everything shut up, and it may be attended with some danger, as at such a time there may be drunken and irresponsible soldiers about with all their worst passions aroused. I imagine that the note will have the effect of spurring the officers up to do their best to prevent pillage and disorder. I hope we shall have no occasion to use force, but I intend to have order here if I have to fight for it. 

Later: - Staunton has returned on board all right, having found the 

General and delivered the note with a profusion of compliments from me, assuring him that I had every confidence in his desire to restrain his men and preserve order, but that I knew at such times bad men were difficult to control etc. etc., He brings off the report that two different parties in Caracus have telephoned here during the evening that the Government in Caracus has sent a commission to General Crespo offering to surrender the town. If this report is true the revolution is over, and peace will be established at once. I shall probably know much more about it tomorrow, but everything now points one way......... 

Friday, October 7th. The revolution is entirely successful and the 

Government has utterly collapsed. The President, Cabinet, prominent generals, etc., are fugitives on board the French and Spanish ships here, and in little steamers which have sailed for Curacao. Yesterday was a pretty busy day for me, and I was up most of the night. The remnants of the government army came into town during the day and night so that, by midnight, we had three or four thousand troops here. As I am writing the town is reasonably quiet; the troops have thrown away their arms and dispersed. I think we shall have no trouble of consequence. 

We are having bad weather here today, the worst since our arrival..... We have some wind, a good deal of sea, and torrents of rain.

I look now for peace in Venezuela and shall hope to soon get away from here, where it is anything but pleasant. I am feeling pretty seedy today from want of sleep, and from being on the stretch with all these matters.

Saturday evening........ ..The government troops that were brought here, to protect the President, Cabinet, high officers of the government, and the Generals who deserted them at once on arrival, behaved upon the whole really very well. They discharged their arms, many of them threw them away, and they dispersed in every direction like melting snow.........I think the rainstorm was an exceedingly fortunate thing for us; nothing disperses a mob quicker than torrents of rain. 

Last night my friend, General Pinango, whom I met at Macuto, when looking after the wounded, came in with a force of revolutionists and occupied the town. Today about 1500 came in, having marched over the mountains from Caracus; they are about the town everywhere, but are quiet and orderly, and I hear of no disturbances. I saw two men shot this morning on the beach, abreast of the ship, and their bodies carried off; but have not learned the reason for the shooting. 

I received a letter from General Pinango this afternoon asking me to order the commanders of the men-of-war in port to send the fugitives, now on board their ships, ashore, and adding that they would all have individual guarantees as soon as they landed. I replied that the ships of the United States had received no fugitives on board, and that I had no authority over the ships belonging to other nations. Made the letter a very civil one, and sent Capehart, in uniform to deliver it in person. The General seemed pleased with my promptness and with the reply; sent me civil messages, hoping soon to be in a position to receive me on shore in a becoming manner etc. etc. I am very glad to have escaped having anything to do with the refugees first, I don't think we really had any right to receive them on board, and, secondly, they are an awful nuisance to have on board, as we have no room for them and they inconvenience everybody. 

Railroad, te le graphic, and telephone communication with Caracus is cut off temporarily; the only way of communicating is by a man on foot over the mountains. I sent a letter to our Minister by that method today. The railroad was destroyed by the fleeing Government people to impede pursuit, and they cut the telegraph and telephone wires also. One telephone wire escaped in some way, but that gave out finally, perhaps caused by the storm which was tremendous. One of the last persons to use the solitary telephone wire was Staunton, whom I sent on shore Thursday night to telephone to our Minister in Caracus.

He found the telephone occupied by the troops, the clerks under arrest, and the manager, an Englishman had just been marched off to gaol. 

Of course he was not allowed to use the telephone. He went directly to the Custom House, and demanded to see President Pulido. The President was not visible (he was preparing to skp on the French cruiser 

Magon) but he saw General Quinteros and his brother, Mr. Quinteros, Governor of Caracus; he demanded in my name the use of the telephone to communicate with the Minister of Caracus, and with such success that these two gentlemen went to the telephone office with him and he was

allowed to talk with the Minister as much as he pleased. Before he got back to the ship, the President and Cabinet were on board the Magon; and as soon as the main body of troops arrived, about two o'clock in the morning, the commanding Generals (Ignacio Pulido and Josef Monagas) and other principal officers, deserted them and went on board the Spanish cruiser. 

Yesterday morning at 7:20, I sent a cable to the Navy Department, telling of the flight of the President and Cabinet and the entire success of the revolutionists. Capehart was at the office before it opened. My despatch was the first one off, and I suspect the first positive information of the collapse of the de facto government. 

I am curious now to know if I am right in believing that a hurricane passed to the northward of us yesterday......The sea here was very heavy. All the men-of-war in port got up steam, let go second anchors, sent down light spars, and two- the Englishman and Frenchman were obliged to get underway and move out, into the outer roadstead. 

The sea knocked away a portion of the heavy stone parapet of the breakwater, knocked in one of the sides of the sheds on the breakwater, and did considerable injury inside, the water pouring over the breakwater like a young Niagara. This ship had less motion, and was more comfortable than any ship here; but we could not send to Macuto to look after the wounded, and communication with La Guayra was infrequent and difficult. Today the water has been rough and we have had a strong trade wind but on the whole the weather has not been bad. 

I had a strong guard on shore at the Consulate last night, as a precautionary measure, and I shall send a guard tomorrow morning on board the mail steamer. I act in all these matters upon the old adage that "an ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure." It is easier to keep an armed party of Venezuelans from going on board the Philadelphia than to recover people whom they may have arrested and taken on shore. I really anticipate no trouble whatever, but I am not going to take any chances... 

Sunday morning. 

Nothing new here this morning, everything quiet on shore. 

Here is the notice sent to General Quinteros by the commanders of the foreign men-of-war: 

To General Quinteros U.S.S. Chicago, Port of La Guayra, Vene zuela 

October 5th, 1892.

Commanding La Guayra 

The undersigned Naval Commanders of the United States, French, Spanish, British and German war vessels in port present their compliments to General Quinteros commanding La Guayra and inform him that in view of many fears expressed that La Guayra might be pillaged by the troops of the government in the event of their defeat, they have to pre-

sent the following conclusions: 

The town of La Guayra must not be given over to pillage, nor must its innocent non-combatants be subjected to unrestrained disorder and violence. Should proper order not be preserved we shall take such measures as may be necessary to protect foreign lives and property. 

The fort must not fire upon the town. Should this be done the men-of-war will open fire upon the fort in order to protect the town. 

Signed by

Admiral Walker Capitan de Fregata Eulate y Fery Commander James B. Young 

Korvetten Kapitan Draeger 

Capitaine de Vaisseau de Barbeyrac "

The revolution was successful and the President and several of his ministers fled, incidentally carrying with them the funds raised by the ladies of the city for the care of the wounded. President Villegas 

Pulido and several of his cabinet took refuge on the French Cruiser Magon, and later in the night Generals Ignacio Pulido, Joseph Monagas and ten others sought asylum in the Spanish Cruiser Don Jorge Juan. "After being abandoned by their generals the government troops shouted for Crespo, discharged and threw away their arms, and dispersed.

There was no rioting or pillaging and no intentional injury, but a few were killed and wounded by random firing.

Admiral Walker's efforts to protect the town of La Guayra and the care given the wounded by his direction were greatly appreciated by the Venezuelans and the order of the Buste del Libertador was conferred upon him in recognition of what he had done to ameliorate suffering. 

On March 1, 1893 Admiral Walker, commanding the North Atlantic 

Squadron, reported to Admiral Bancroft Gherardi, commanding the Naval

Review Fleet, for duty and enclosed a list of vessels under his command; the Chicago, Miantonomoh, Atlanta, Kears age, Philadelphia, Concord and

Vesuvius. A Naval Review was to be an important part of the celebration of the four hundredth anniversary of the discovery of America by Columbus; it had been postponed one year as the World's Fair in Chicago was not ready to open in 1892. Squadrons from many foreign countries took part in the review and Admiral Walker was in command of the Second Squadron of the American Naval Review Fleet. 

The Second Squadron, with the Atlanta, Baltimore and San Francisco temporarily attached to it, went to Hampton Roads in March and early in April began intensive drill. The exercises lasted for three days, the Naval signals being used for the first two days, while on the

third day the International Signals were used and the Naval Review Programme practised. The ships were in need of practise but improved materially during the three days exercise. 

Three more days of practise in the Manoeuvres to be used at the Naval Review took place on April 11th, 12th and 13th and Admiral

Walker considered them on the whole very satisfactory and felt sure that his vessels would compare creditably with those of other nations. 

The great Review which was held at New York early in May aroused great interest throughout the country. People of the Middle West, far from the sea coast, uninterested in the relations of America with European or Asiatic countries, knew little and cared less about the Navy, its importance as a first line of defense against aggressions from abroad and the diplomatic work done by it in time of peace. The fine stand of their 

Navy in comparison with those of other countries upon this occasion appealed to their national pride and aroused an interest which was of great value to the Navy.

At noon on May 31st, 1893 Admiral Walker hauled down his flag and turned over the command of the North Atlantic Squadron to Rear Admiral Benham, after nearly four years afloat, during which time he had commanded the Squadron of Evolution, the European Station, the South 

Atlantic Station (two different periods), the North Atlantic Station and the Second Squadron of the Naval Review Fleet. 

That his services were greatly appreciated by the Navy Department is shown by the following letter dated May 20th, 1893. "Acting Rear-Admiral

Sir: 

Jno. G. Walker, U. S. R.

Upon issuing the order detaching you from command afloat, the department desires to express its high appreciation of your efficient and valuable services while exercising the command from which you are now relieved, and especially for the manner in which you performed the duties incident to that part of your service during which you were in charge of the Squadron of Evolution. No officer of the United States Navy, with one exception, has, during the last twenty-eight years, commanded a squadron for a period so long as you and the Department congratulates you upon the manner in which you have discharged the duties imposed upon you. 

With best wishes, I am 

Yours respectfully, (signed) H. A. Herbert 

Secretary of the Navy"

The twenty-eight years mentioned covered all the time since the close of the War of the Rebellion.

For the next ten months, or until March 1894, Commodore Walker, his title after leaving his command afloat until his promotion to Rear- Admiral in January, 1894, served on many boards, Naval Examining Board, Board of Inspection and Survey, and so forth, and in March, 1894 appeared before the Joint Sub-Committee on Naval Reorganization to explain the bill which he had suggested for reforming and reorganizing the personnel of the Line of the Navy.
