He knew that any good bill, incorporating substantial reforms was bound to hurt somebody and would therefore meet with opposition from some Naval officers. but he felt that promotion by seniority alone did not always get the best officers to flag rank. He urged strongly a system of grade retirement, this retirment being determined by the attainment of the maximum age fixed for promotion to the next higher grade, combined with promotion, either partially or entirely by selection. 

Any board of officers appointed to select officers for promotion would have the records of their careers from an early age upon which to form an opinion and such a system would be an incentive to industry and effort. 

Grade retirement without selection Admiral Walker felt would work badly, promotion to high rank depending not upon merit or industry but solely upon the age at which an officer enters the Navy, modified slightly by the position taken in his class upon graduation from the Naval Academy. A boy who entered at fourteen or fifteen would be sure of all promotions and of ultimate promotion to flag rank, while a boy entering at nineteen or twenty would be equally sure of retirment in the first grade in which this system was applied. In neither case would ambition find any incentive. He based his opinion upon his years of service, his great interest in the question of the personnel, the attention he had given to its study and the experience of foreign navies, notably that of England. 

Admiral Walker's appointment in March, 1894 to command the

Pacific Station was the result of President Cleveland's wish to have a thorough and author atative report upon conditions in the Sandwich Islands, where there had been much uneasiness since the coming to power of 

King Kalakaua.

The Sandwich Islands, when discovered by Captain Cook in 1778, were divided into three distinct kingdoms, but soon afterwards, upon the death of the ruler of Hawaii, Kamehameha I succeeded to his position and soon proved himself a man of great force of character. He learned from the foreigners who visited the islands to build vessels and trade with the other islands; he also introduced the use of fire arms and finally attacked and conquered the chiefs of the other kingdoms and became King of the whole group, making two American seamen his advisors. Soon after his death the American Board for Foreign Missions sent a party of a dozen men and women to the islands to educate and convert the natives and their educational work was very successful. From time to time there was trouble from the actions of the officers of vessels of foreign powers but in 1840 the King, Kamahameha III, gave the country a constitution and soon the independence of the islands was recognized by England, France and the United States and an American lawyer became the first Minister of 

Foreign Affairs.

Until about 1870 the government ran smoothly, the native kings ruling, but having as their advisors members of the "missionary party", descendants of the early American missionaries. During the more than ninety years that the Kamehameha dynasty ruled the islands American influence predominated, laws and institutions modeled after those of the United States were adopted and the American school system was inaugurated, while the aim of Kamehameha, III, was to be the king not of one race but of all people in his domain; but with the death of Kamehameha V and the end of the dynasty there was a change. 

King Kalakaua, not in any way related to the Kamehameha dynasty, was elected and trouble promptly developed. He treated residents of 

American or European descent, who were responsible for the development of the islands, as alien invaders and not only sought to make himself absolute monarch of his own island but also aspired to the "Primacy of the Pacific". He surrounded himself with flatterers, the gratification of whose expensive and childish extravagances made it necessary to find fresh sources of income, licensing the sale of opium, exemption money from lepers and gross official corruption. The benefits of a treaty of reciprocity with the United States caused for a time a spirit of forbearance towards the King but at last the patience of the respectable foreigners and better class of natives was worn out and a committee of Safety was organized, the members of which elected a committee to demand from the King good government and a new Constitution. Finding that the foreign diplomatic representatives would give him no help but advised him to accept the terms of the mass-meeting, he yielded and signed the Constitution. Upon his death in January, 1891 his sister was proclaimed 

Queen under the name of Liliuokalani. Her aim also was to restore autocratic government and the struggle between the Queen and the reform party culminated in January, 1893 in a revolution when once more a Committee of Safety was appointed at a public meeting and the Committee then formed a Provisional Government and reorganized the volunteer military companies which had been disbanded in 1890. A mass meeting of citizens rectified these actions. On account of the disturbed state of affairs a small force was landed from the U. S. S. Boston on the evening of January 16th, at the request of the United States Minister, Mr. Stevens, and a committee of residents, to protect the lives and property of American citizens in case of riot or incendiarism. On the next day the Committee of 

Safety took possession of the government building, declared the monarchy at an end and that the Provisional Government should rule "until terms of union with the United States shall have been negotiated and agreed upon."

The Queen surrendered under protest and appealed to the government of the United States to reinstate her. When President Cleveland took office in March, 1893 he withdrew the treaty of annexation, which had been negotiated a month before, and sent Mr. Blount to Hawaii as Commissioner paramount to report on the situation. Upon the receipt of his report that the revolution had been accomplished by the aid of the U. S. minister and the landing of troops from the Boston, Mr. Willis was sent out as Minister and, after securing a promise from the Queen to grant an amnesty, made a formal demand on the Provisional Government for her reinstatement. This was promptly refused and President Cleveland,

wishing a clearer understanding of affairs in the islands, Admiral Walker was ordered in March, 1894, to command the Pacific Station and to proceed to Hawaii with the following confidential orders from the Secretary of the Navy. 

"NAVY DEPARTMENT CONFIDENTIAL 

(Copy)

Rear-Admiral John G. Walker, U.S.N. 

Commanding U. S. Naval Forces, Pacific Station. 

Sirs:- 

Washington, March 27, 1894

The following instructions, which are to be considered strictly confidential, are issued for your guidance while in command of the U. S. 

Naval Force on the Pacific Station: 

The attitude and policy which the Government of the United States has hitherto observed towards the people and government of the Hawaiian 

Islands are fully shown in the Executive Documents Nos. 47, 48, 65, 70, 76, 79, and 95 which have been mailed to you in care of the Navy Pay 

Office, San Francisco.

You will freely consult with the Minister of the United States at Honolulu upon all points which may arise in the general course of Hawaiian matters, and you will give due weight to his knowledge and opinion upon the actual employment, in any event, of the military forces under your command, and in deciding the course to be pursued by yourself in any matter not covered by specific instructions. 

Your purpose as Commander of the Naval Forces of the United States will be the protection of the lives and property of American citizens.

In case of any civil war in the islands, whether growing out of an attempt to restore the Queen, or an attempt to establish a permanent government, or out of any election, or arising from any cause whatever, you will extend no aid or support, moral or physical, to any parties engaged therein, but you will keep steadily in view that it is your duty to protect the lives and property of all such citizens of the United States as shall not by their participation in such civil commotions subject themselves to local laws and thus forfeit their right in that regard to the protection of the American flag. An American citizen who during a revolution or insurrection in a foreign country participates in an attempt by force of arms or violence to maintain or otherthrow the existing government, or who aids in setting on foot a revolution or insurrection in such country cannot claim as matter of right that the Government of the United States shall protect him against the consequences of such act.

If you shall observe at any time any effort or attempt on the part of any foreign power to interfere in the political affairs of the Hawaiian Islands, or if you shall perceive any circumstances pointing to such interference you will promptly report the same to this department. 

Very respectfully, (signed) H. A. Herbert 

Secretary of the Navy."

Before leaving Washington Admiral Walker had a talk with the President, who had selected him for the mission as a man of wide knowledge of men and affairs, whose opinion would carry weight and whose discretion could be absolutely relied upon. It will be seen that the situation was a difficult one, some representatives of European nations supporting Liliuokalani in demanding her reinstatement and President Cleveland being inclined to blame the American Minister for the success of the revolution and Admiral Walker wished to be sure of understanding clearly what his position would be.

Everything was done to make his trip across the country and his stay in San Francisco as comfortable and pleasant as possible and letters to his wife show his appreciation of the many courtesies shown him and of his cordial welcome in San Francisco. 

My dear Betty "C. B. & Q. Station, Chicago 

Thursday 5:30 P.M.

Here we are all right, car is ready for us and we go out at 6.32. Howland, Superintendant of Road in Illinois, met me, car is provisioned and they offer to send a car to San Francisco to bring me back when I return. Expect to make a pleasant trip and to arrive in San Francisco at 9.30 Monday morning the second in good shape. After I am fairly settled in my own car I shall straighten things out, unpack my bag and make myself easy and comfortable. 

Give love to the children, tell them I expect letters from them by each steamer. Love and kisses for you, de ar Betty.

Mr. Mende (?), General Manager, has just come to see that all is comfortable and right for me. 

Goodbye, my dear, will write you from Omaha tomorrow, a card 

Kisses and love from if nothing more. 

Your affct. husband."

"Sunday April 1st, 1894. 

My dear Betty

We are at this moment at Winnemucca, Nevada but this note will probably be mailed at Reno which is some hours ahead. Everything has gone smoothly and comfortably and I am really less tired than when I left Washington. It is everything having a private car, you can have no conception of the difference in comfort until you have tried it on a long journey. I have a large room and large bed, go to bed when I please, meals, and very excellent ones, are served when I want them. I can wash, dress and take care of myself in a decent and comfortable way, and instead of finding the trip a very hard one as I had feared, I have really enjoyed it.

The country since leaving Cheyenne has been a desert because of want of water. I have no doubt that irrigation may sometime do much for it but now it is barren beyond words to express.......From Reno we begin to climb the Sierra Nevada, shall pass all the fine parts of the road during the night." "Palace Hotel 

San Francisco, Cal. 

Monday, April 2nd, 1894. 

My dear Betty

I arrived here this morning somewhat behind time but was in my room at quarter before twelve.....have been talking with Commander 

Nelson who arrived yesterday from Honolulu.

He has been there in command of the Adams but was relieved the other day by Brice. I was glad to meet Nelson as he is fairly intelligent and I was able to get a pretty good idea of the present condition of affairs in Honolulu.

Later. I have had several people here to call upon me so that I have not been able to finish a letter and I am beginning to feel pretty tired....

Have been put up at two clubs here, am to dine at the Pacific Union, the swell club, Wednesday evening, and am only afraid of receiving too many attentions. I want to keep myself as quiet as I can.......

After dinner. I am just back from dining at the Cosmos Club where I went with Staunton and Wood because we could get a good dinner cheap and be clear of newspaper men. Both remaining nights I am to dine by invitation at the Pacific Union.

I met at the Cosmos Mr. Bishop, an old resident of Honolulu, whose wife was the last of the old chief blood. She was a fine woman,

refused to be Queen upon the death of the last Kamehameha and allowed the throne to go to an inferior chief, saying that she preferred to spend the remainder of her days with her husband in peace. Mr. B. is rich and is doing much for the welfare of the natives. Has no children and is trying to so dispose his money as to make it of use to the Hawaiian race. He probably stands higher in the estimation of both whites and natives than any other man. He passes much of his time here now and I may not see him in Honolulu at all, which I rather regret. 

Well! I must get ready for bed. I hope to sleep well and feel young tomorrow. Goodnight, my dear! Sound sleep and pleasant dreams for you. 

Tuesday 3rd April. McCalla has just left me after an hour's talk. Have seen Paymaster Bacon, Lt. Stoney, Comdr. Morgan, Pelham Ames and others this morning. Have more invitations to lunch and dine than 

I can attend to possibly....... "U.S.S. Philadelphia 

Honolulu, Friday April 13, '94. 

My dear Betty 1 The passage from San Francisco was very smooth and pleasant, we had some pleasant people on board and I was fairly comfortable. Arrived here at daylight yesterday morning and I came on board at nine o'clock. Admiral Irwin is packing up, I shall relieve him tomorrow and he will then go on shore to a hotel to await the sailing of the first steamer for San Francisco. As soon as he is out of the ship I shall have the cabin cleaned and put in order, my traps arranged and shall settle down for a comfortable stay of perhaps three months.

My quarters are very comfortable, particularly so for this climate, and I expect to enjoy my stay here. I went on shore yesterday afternoon for an informal call on Mr. Willis, our Minister, and talked with him for a considerable time, then went to the Hawaiian Hotel where I met Paul Newman, a royalist and a very bright, agreeable man. Later 

I walked up the valley and met Frank Judd, Mrs. Carter's brother, who is Chief Justice. He offered me a horse to ride while here which I accepted and next week I expect to begin my exercise. As soon as I have taken charge I shall arrange for a call upon the President and Cabinet and get through the official visits as soon as possible......... 

The Honolulu papers of yesterday (evening) and this morning were filled with stuff about my intentions, mostly a rehash from papers at home; some of the personal items I will cut out and send for Fannie's scrap book.

The weather is delightful, just a grain too warm, everybody in white linen clothes. Four men of war here, the Philadelphia and Adams, one Englishman and one Japanese....... 

Everything here seems as quiet and peaceful as you will find it in Dublin but there are various things that may bring about trouble

at any time. I am inclined to think, however, that they will work out finally into good shape without bloodshed.... 

April 14.

I have relieved Irwin and am in full command of the Pacific Station. This morning at ten the fuss began, the Englishman and the Jap giving Irwin a complimentary farewell salute which was returned gun for gun; then his flag was saluted by the Philadelphia and hauled down, my flag was run up and saluted in the same way by the Philadelphia and then promptly by the Englishman and Jap. Altogether one hundred and thirty guns were fired. The Attorney General came on Board yesterday to invite me to visit the leper settlement on the island of Molokai with the Board of Health, leaving here Monday. I was obliged to decline as I must first have my interview with President Dole. A correspondent of a San Francisco paper came on board an hour ago and tells me that it is reported amongst the natives that I have come here to restore the Queen and that I visited her yesterday. 

Mrs. Wilder (Lizzie Judd) has asked me to dinner for Monday evening and I have accepted......I have been offered a special train to visit Pearl Harbor and intend to accept as soon as ready for the trip, but I suppose I shall set a crop of rumors going large enough to fill a newspaper." "Flagship Philadelphia

Pacific Station 

My dear Betty 

Honolulu April 17, 194 .Since arriving here I have been pretty quiet, nursing my cold to get rid of it. 

Last night I dined at Mrs. Wilder's, a very good dinner and a very pleasant one. Party of twelve, President Dole and wife, the Chief 

Justice and wife, the German Consul and wife, Mrs. Carter, Miss Nellie

Judd (an older sister), Mr. Armstrong, (brother of General Armstrong of 

Hampton), Captain Barker and myself. I was the guest of honor, sitting on Mrs. Wilder's right, with Mrs. Carter next me and Mrs. Dole my opposite. When the dining room doors were thrown open and we walked in, we found four pretty young women in pretty servants dresses and caps with Philadelphia ribbons around them, to wait upon the table. They were a daughter, a daughter-in-law, a young unmarried woman and a young divorcee who were staying in the house. They kept their faces and played their parts well. It made the dinner very jolly and later they came into the parlor in their waiting dresses and played and sang for us. I don't know when I have enjoyed a dinner so much. 

Mr. Dole is a very well appearing man. I was much pleased with his appearance and Mrs. Dole was very nice. She warmly invited me to call at her house which I shall of course do. 

Today at noon I called upon the President, in special full dress,

was presented by our Minister and chatted for ten or fifteen minutes. 

I was received by a battalion of troops, and the President had with him the cabinet, Justices of the Supreme Court, the Vice President, Members of the Council, and so forth. (The battalion of Government troops was paraded and the usual honors with arms and band given upon his arrival and departure.) The reception was well managed, dignified and in every way successful. He returned my visit at two P.M. and was received with all the honors due the chief of a sovereign state. It is warm weather for buttoning up in special full dress and I am glad it is over. I got into thin clothes as soon as possible and am now sitting in trousers, shirt and slippers. 

April 20th. Yesterday I went by invitation to visit. a sugar plantation, a few miles out of town. We went by special train over the Oahu railroad and I enjoyed the trip very much. Upon reaching the plantation we were taken by a little plantation locomotive and car all about the estate, to the pumping works, through the cane fields, to the sugar mill, and so forth. All the hauling is done by rail and I saw several trains of cane loaded cars on their way to mill. Artificial irrigation is used which insures a large crop every year. The water is supplied from artesian wells and the pumps force the water up about 140 feet and at a rate estimated to cover four thousand acres a foot deep each month. The mill is now turning out about forty-five tons of high grade sugar per day. 

Today I am having calls from gentlemen on shore, which rather interferes with letter writing. Tomorrow morning the Australia will be due with mail from San Francisco, to the 14th. Of course I am looking forward to letters although I cannot receive anything from you later than the eighth, eleven days only after I left Washington. After this there will be no regular mail of any consequence for nineteen days, until May tenth, the mail by Vancouver not counting for much this time..... 

Saturday April 21st.......I went ashore today and made several calls, one of which was upon President and Mrs. Dole. Met a considerable number of people there and it was very pleasant. I am going down to 

Pearl Harbor on Tuesday to examine the lagoon and see things myself. 

It will be a pleasant trip, will give me a nice outing and I shall learn some thing.

It is now thought that the bar at the entrance, through which a channel must be cut to make the harbor of any value, is composed of coral sand. If this is the case it is easily dredged and the expense will be comparatively small. I am now organizing a party to make a series of borings which will show beyond all doubt of what it is composed. The distance to dredge is small and a thirty foot channel once cut would give us an almost perfect harbor for naval purposes, large enough for a navy of any size.

I have only a few more official calls to make but personal calls are pouring in upon me at a great rate, everybody seems to want to be civil and I expect to find my stay here a pleasant one. Sam 

Parker, a half native, a leading royalist called upon me a few days ago. He was Secretary of State before the Queen was ousted and is a

fine looking man. Thursday is their day and I shall go to call there next week. I mean to know all parties and judge for myself as far as possible.

Sunday. I went ashore this morning and attended service at the First Union Church. I might have thought myself from the appearance of the people and the general air of things, at a Congregational Church in a flourishing country town in New England. A large congregation of nice looking people, attentive and interested. 

The weather for the last two or three days has been cooler, about 75° during the heat of the day, with a fresh trade breeze. It is the trade wind which constantly blows over the islands that makes the climate so delightful. 

Wednesday April 25th. I passed yesterday at Pearl Harbor.

Went down by rail, had a steam cutter meet me and steamed about the place until I had seen all I wanted to. Lunched at the house of a 

Mr. Brown and came back in time for lunch (?) on board. Was tired and went to bed early... 

I am to dine with the Carters Friday evening.....Monday evening I went to a meeting of a literary society and heard a paper read on the Columbian Fair at Chicago, meeting a number of prominent and pleasant men. The whole tone of the place is much like New England. 

Thursday 26th. Yesterday I made a lot of calls and in the evening went on board the English cruiser Champion, for an hour, to dance. When I came away I brought the Minister of Foreign Affairs with me and had a long talk with him in my cabin about island political conditions. I think the probabilities are in favor of things going quietly but there are chances for an outbreak, at any time, that may give serious trouble.

I have only the Philadelphia here now, ought to have one more large ship at least. What I want are men enough to land in case of trouble to enable me to take the lead of the foreign ships here, and in a way, control the situation. It is an odd thing that both political parties look to the United States for care and protection and both would vote for annexation, which I think is sure to come sooner or later. 

Since writing the above I have been on shore making calls and wound up at a lawn party given by Mrs. Prear, wife of a Justice of the 

Supreme Court, upon the twenty-fifth anniversary of her parents' wedding day. Met lots of people but a little shower drove us all indoors and upon the verandas where it was crowded and hot. I remained an hour and then started for the ship.

I me an to get started at my horse-back exercise next week. 

Up to this time I have had so many things to attend to that I have had no time for riding. 

April 28th.......I dined last evening with the Carters and passed a pleasant evening. Mrs. Carter told me that she had braced up

and written to you. The dinner was nice and for eight, two Mrs. Carters, Mrs. VanVliet, Miss McGrew, Captain Barker, Mr. Carter (Charles), a young man whose name I have forgotten and myself. Everything continues quiet here and a stranger would never suspect any trouble existed." "Honolulu, H. I. April 21, 1894

I have the honor to inform the department that on the fourteenth instant, the date of assuming command of this station, the English and Japanese vessels in port saluted my flag and their commanding officers called upon me officially. These calls were returned on the 16th instant. 

On the 17th, accompanied by my personal staff, I called upon the United States Minister and later was presented by him to the President of the Provision Government, when I paid my respects at Government House.

The President was attended at the reception by the Vice President, members of the Executive and of the Advisory Councils and the Justices of the Supreme Court. 

The battalion of Government troops was paraded and the usual honors with arms and band were given upon arrival and departure. 

The President accompanied by members of the Executive Council, Justices of the Supreme Court and two aides returned my call the afternoon of the same day and was received with the honors due his rank. 

Visits have also been exchanged with the representatives of the English, German, Japanese and Portugese Governments. 

Very respectfully

J. G. Walker Rear-Admiral U. 

S. Navy. 

The Honorable Secretary of the Navy"

Admiral Walker had long been interested in Pearl Harbor and the facilities it possessed for the establishment of a secure and commodious coaling and naval station and while the Chief of the Bureau of Navigation he had caused a survey of the harbor and its approaches to be made by officers of the Vandalia. Upon taking command of the Pacific Station his attention was called to the new light which the development of the Honolulu Bar had thrown upon the question of opening Pearl Harbor. The deep- ening of the entrance to Honolulu had been easily accomplished, the material proving to be coral sand which could be easily removed by a suction pump.

Admiral Walker directed the organization of a party under the command of Lieutenant W. M. Wood of the Philadelphia with instructions to male a thorough examination of the entrance to Pearl Harbor by boring at different places to a depth of not less than thirty feet below low water,

to determine the character of the bar and enable a reasonably accurate estimate of the cost of dredging a channel. The work was carried on under considerable difficulties. It was necessary to make the borings from a heavy tripod, weighted to stand upright and withstand the sea, the handling of which was laborious, slow and often dangerous, because of the heavy sea on the bar. It was found that there was a channel through the reef at Pearl Harbor which was filled with coral sand and which could be removed easily and cheaply by a suction pump, leaving the entrance deep enough for large ships and three hundred feet wide between the walls of live coral. 

Every assistance was given to the party by the Hawaiian Government Survey and by the management of the Oahu R. R. Co. and a very complete and careful survey made and a detailed report sent to the Department.

Letter to the Secretary of the Navy: 

"Honolulu, H. I. April 28, 1894

Sir: 

Since my arrival here on April 12 I have endeavored to obtain such information of Hawaiian political affairs as would enable me to form an opinion upon the political situation. I have talked with the 

United States Minister and with men of both parties, and although individual utterances are influenced by political associations and interests, I have received from the whole the following impressions which I think are reasonably accurate and reliable.

The Provisional Government appears to be firmly established and to command to as great a degree the confidence and support of the people as could be expected in any country of a government temporary in its origin and character, bridging over the interval between one permanent form and another. Law and order are observed, life and property are secure and business goes on as usual. 

No one landing in Honolulu today would perceive any indications to the contrary and from all accounts peace and quiet prevail throughout all the islands of the group. 

But there are nevertheless two causes which give uneasiness to those having most at stake in the islands and best qualified to judge: 

1st. The uncertainty of the natives as to the ultimate restoration of the Queen. 

2nd. The possible disaffection of the Japanese.

The registration of votes for the election of delegates to a Constitutional Convention (which election is to be held on the 2nd of May) has closed. About 1500 have registered in Honolulu; the returns from the remainder of Oahu and from the other islands have not yet been

published. Probably the majority of them have not yet been received here. 

Comparatively few natives have registered. Their policy being one of absention from political affairs under existing conditions. They have been informed by their leaders that, in the event of the Queen's restoration, those who had taken the oath and participated in this election would be proscribed, - would perhaps suffer all the penalties of treason and they are afraid to take the risk. They are constantly being told that the Queen is to be restored by the United States and the se stories, which are largely believed by the more ignorant, add to their fears. Foreign influence has undoubtedly encouraged this belief and apprehension. If the native Hawaiians could be fully assured that the monarchy would not be restored by outside interference it is believed that they would readily participate in all the steps for organizing a new form of government. 

The sentiment of loyalty to the Monarchy and to the Queen is, to a considerable extent, a sentiment of self-interest or self-preservation in the event of a restoration. It is said, and probably truthfully, that many prominent Hawaiians would speedily detach themselves and their adherents from the Royalist Party and enter the race for political preferment under the new government if they felt that it was safe to do so. 

I am satisfied that a clearly formulated assurance from the United States of non-interference with the political affairs of Hawaii and of disapproval of such interference on the part of any other nation would rest the situation clearly on its local merits in the estimation of all classes without hopes or fear of outside pressure, and would tend to a speedy settlement of all troubles.

The Japanese have a large interest here from the number of their people who have been brought into the islands under labor contracts. They form now a large percentage of the population, numbering more than 20,000. Of these I am informed that about one half are still under contract and the remainder are free men, those whose contracts have expired and who remain as ordinary laborers. They are inclined to be turbulent they stand together as a solid body and their leaders are said to have political ambitions and to propose to claim for their free men the right to vote under the conditions with which that right is granted to other foreigners. The contract laborers are of the lowest class of Japanese and their alleged political desires may be in a great part simply the talk of political agitators. Many persons here both in and out of the government think the Japanese a possible source of future danger. They are a brave people with military instincts and would fight if aroused or excited to violence. 

Their representative here, Mr. Saburo Fujii, impresses me as a man of considerable intelligence and strength. 

The Portugese are also said to be moving for political recognition and have organized clubs with that end in view. But so far they have shown no disorderly or turbulant tendencies and are regarded as a law-abiding and desirable class of immigrants. 

The Chinese do not enter in to political considerations to any considerable extent.

I have come in contact with all classes of society and if the recent events have left personal animosities they are not readily perceptible. Royalists and supporters of the Provisional Government appear to be on pleasant personal terms. As a rule the English are Royalists but there are many among them who support the Provisional Government and favor annexation.

In my opinion our naval forces here should be strengthened. 

The departure of the Adams to join the Bering Sea Patrol Fleet leaves the flag-ship unsupported and, while I do not anticipate disturbances which will seriously menace the security of life and property, I do not consider it prudent to overlook their possibility. Should they occur we ought to have the controlling force here - stronger than that of any foreign power and equal to any two of them combined. Every one, including foreigners of all classes, look to the United States to take the leading part and holds us responsible for the preservation of life and property. At present we are equalled in strength by the English and exceeded by the Japanese. The Philadelphia's landing force is little if any, larger than the Champion's and not so large as the Takacheho's. 

Should there be disturbances in any of the other islands I have no other ship to send there. I could not guard American interests elsewhere without for the time abandoning Honolulu. I have no knowledge of the 

Charleston's movements but recommend that pending her arrival the 

Monterey be sent here.

I learn from the papers that the Marion is on her way here from Yokohama. Should she arrive here before the 2nd of May I shall hold her until the elections have taken place and longer if deemed advisable. 

I am, Sir, Very respectfully 

J. G. Walker 

Rear Admiral, U. S. Navy, Commanding

U. S. Naval Force on Pacific Station. 

The Honorable 

Secretary of the Navy 

Washington, D. C." 

My dear Betty "Honolulu, May 2, 1894 ...An election is in progress today throughout the islands for delegates to a Constitutional Convention, which is to frame a constitution for a permanent government. I think it will pass off

quietly and that thus one more step will be taken towards establishing a republic. I am going on shore by and by to look about a little and if all is quiet shall make a few calls. Tomorrow evening I am to dine with

Mr. and Mrs. P. C. Jones, a prominent man here, the following evening I am going out again and on Sunday am to breakfast with the Castles at Wiakiki, three or four miles from town, at their seaside house. Night before last I went to a very pleasant party at Dr. McGrew's. Don't sound much like insurrection and war does it? However I have all hands on board today and am ready to land one hundred and fifty men at a moment's notice.

You would eniov the climate here and the outdoor life, It is delightful summer weather with a cool breeze always blowing, the northwest trade, and the luxuriant foliage of the tropics everywhere. 

Evening......I went ashore about three o'clock, looked about a little found everything very quiet and orderly, walked up the valley about a mile, called upon Mrs Wilder, Mrs. Carter and at two or three other places, then walked back to the landing and came aboard to dinner. 

Had pleasant calls upon both Mrs. W and Mrs. C whom I like. I found 

Mrs. W stirred up about the stories, circulating among the natives,that 

I was going to restore the Queen to power at once. The poor natives are kept from declaring for the Government by being told that the United States is going to restore the Queen and that then all who have deserted her cause will have their property confiscated and will be severely punished.

Since I came here there have been almost as many stories about what I was going to do as there have been days. The whole business could easily be brought to final settlement if our government would declare itself in unmistakable terms..

There may be trouble here yet but I hope not and am inclined to think there will not... 

Thursday May 3.

The elections passed off most quietly yesterday, not the slightest trouble of any kind. There are stories of royalists organizing, to be supported by the English, but I doubt if anything comes of them.

The English Minister here and the senior naval officer, are both rank royalists in their sympathies, or perhaps it would be more correct to say anti-American, but I don't believe they will dare to take any steps opening against the Provisional Government and in favor of the Queen. I shall of course try to keep myself informed of what is going on and be ready for anything that may turn up. 

If the weather holds good expect to go to the Palli tomorrow with a party of gentlemen and ladies, lunch and return by three o'clock .........I am very well and comfortable on board ship, have a Japanese steward, cook and two attendants who are going to do pretty well I think. 

Mess by myself, the Captain's cabin being on the deck below mine."

91 "Honolulu, May 3, 1894 

Dear Sir

I have the honor to report that there has been no change in the situation of affairs at this place since my letter No. 6, of the 28th ultimo.

The election of delegates to a Constitutional Convention was held yesterday. In Honolulu and vicinity the utmost quiet and good order prevailed, no reports have yet been received from the other islands. 

I enclose newspaper clippings bearing on the elections. 

The Honor able 

I am, Sir, Secretary of the Navy" "Honolulu Advertiser,

May 3 

Another Scheme 

Royalists Organizing for the Expected Restoration. The latest conviction among the royalists is that the ex-Queen will be restored upon the first day of June. This happy event will not be brought about by their own efforts but will be due to the intervention of their favorite deus ex machina, the President of the United States. In order to maintain the newly restored monarchy it is said that its adherents are busy with the re-organization of the native companies, the King's Own, The Prince's Own, &c. They claim they will have rifles and ammunition enough by the end of the month to arm these companies, as they have a supply now and expect to receive more by the first of June." 

My dear Betty "Honolulu, May 10, 1894 ………..I have seen a good deal of Mrs. Carter and indeed of all the Judds who have been very cordial and kind. ........People here are very hospitable and I am invited to dinners, breakfasts, etc., in every direction. I have been to one breakfast and two dinners this week and have a breakfast and a dinner still to come, while my afternoons are largely taken up returning calls. 

I seem to have made a pleasant impression and am very well received. 

The men of standing have nearly all been aboard and invite me to their homes.

I am careful not to express opinions on the political situa-

tion but keep my ears wide open and try to keep well posted as to what is going on.

There are rumors of a royalist rising but I don't believe there is any probability of its taking place, and certainly none of a successful rising unless with outside assistance. ......Honolulu is an attractive place to me, the fine climate, the pure fresh air, the outdoor life, for people here practically live out of doors, and the indolent lazy existence appeal to me very strongly. 

I wish you could come here and remain a few months. You would enjoy it immensely........ .I find myself in a peculiar position. Our minister, Mr. Willis, goes nowhere, knows very few people, has cut himself off from society and is heartily disliked by the better class of residents. 

He has no influence with anyone. From his position he ought to have great weight in shaping matters here, as the U. S. Minister has always been looked up to, not only by Americans but by others. He has absolutely none. Instead of going to the minister people now come to me for advice. I have even been called upon and my opinion asked as to proposed clauses in the coming constitution by members of the cabinet and others high in authority and in the councils of the government. Of course I must do nothing to slight Mr. W. and I have to go carefully. 

Saturday May 12. One month ago this morning I arrived here and upon the whole I have passed a pleasant month and am not sorry I came 

Monday May 14. Yesterday I went to a very handsome midday breakfast at a Mr. Irwin's, a wealthy business man, given at his seaside house at Waikiki, about four miles out of town. Very handsome and bright wife, breakfast for twelve. After breakfast had a long talk with the Minister for Foreign Affairs, on Hawaiian matters. He sought the talk and conversed very freely. Of course I was interested as it was giving me information, and we sat in a corner away from the others for fully an hour and a half, not very polite perhaps, but businesslike! ......My surveying party at Pearl Harbor is making slow progress in making borings on the bar but there is plenty of time and I hope as experience is had the work will go faster If we had a cable here from California and a regular weekly mail it would not be a bad place at all." 

Letter to the Secretary of the Navy. "U.S.S. Flagship 

May 14, 1894 ..Quiet and order have prevailed since the election, and the coming assemblage of the Constitutional Convention does not appear to excite any unusual feeling or comment.. ........An Executive Proclamation has been issued calling

the recently elected delegates together on May 30th. ..The hope of the royalists for an ultimate restoration of the monarchy is probably becoming less every day......That they receive a certain amount of foreign sympathy is beyond doubt and that sympathy has aided and to some extent will continue to aid and encourage them, but in the absence of active interposition its effect is diminishing.......There are more than 20,000 Japanese in the islands, they are obstinate and clannish and probably influenced by political agitators and have good diplomatic and military backing." "Personal

U. S. Flagship Philadelphia 

Honolulu, H. I. May 14, 1894 

My dear Mr. Herbert:

I send in this mail an official despatch relative to the political condition of affairs here; but as much of the information which enables me to form an opinion upon Hawaiian matters comes in the way of rumors, private conversations and from my observation of the mental attitude of the public and the drift of its sentiment and opinion, I think it better to place before you in a private letter, and in such form that you may use it or not at your discretion, certain statements which have too obvious a bearing upon the final adjustment of Hawaiian politics to be overlooked or passed by in silence. Both the private origin of some of the statements and the fact that some of the others are based upon opinions and rumors individually often vague and intangible, but collectively worth noting, render more appropriate this communication in a private letter than in an official despatch. 

2. In the election of delegates to the Constitutional Convention the vote through the islands of the group was fairly large in proportion to the registration list. 

3. Before the day of election the Provisional Government removed the last of the sand bags which had been employed to place the public buildings in a state of defense. The election itself is a long step towards the establishment of a permanent form of government, and the absence of any attempt on the part of the Royalists to control or nullify it would seem to be an indication that no further trouble need be apprehended. I am sure that since the election the confidence of the 

Royalists in an ultimate restoration of the Queen has materially diminished. 

4. During the month which has elapsed since my arrival here, I have made every effort to inform myself with regard to the political condition of the islands, and, with this end in view, have gone among the people, have accepted invitations and have met all classes, and I have observed the material gain of confidence on the one hand and the loss of hope on the other which have been the result of the quiet and orderly course of affairs attending the choice of delegates to a 

Constitutional Convention.

5. I apprehend no attempt on the part of the Royalists to bring about a counter revolution by force. They may have a few stands of arms concealed in the hands of individuals, but they have no coherent effective organization, and no leaders of ability, and of course any attempt at military organization and drill would be speedily suppressed by the Provisional Government. Their only hope lies in active interposition from outside. Without such interposition they will do nothing, and should they become satisfied that the Queen will not be restored they would soon enter the race for political preferment under any form of government which may be adopted. 

6. The Commanding Officer of H.M.S. Champion, Captain Rooke, is strongly in sympathy with the Royalists and would be glad to see them succeed in a restoration. In this he is in accord with the majority of the English residents here, whose political predilections are in favor of a monarchy, and who would expect under the future reign of Princess Kiulani, a considerable accession of English influence and control. This Princess has been educated in England and brought up under English influences, which would naturally affect the choice of advisers and her attitude toward American interests. Two weeks ago, a few days before the election, Captain Rooke came to me in a most serious frame of mind and told me he was sure there would be an outbreak within a month and that it would be attended with violence and bloodshed and he appeared to be somewhat exercised about his responsibilities as the guardian of British life and property. Day before yesterday he came again to see me and to inform me that he should probably leave here about the 25th of the month for Esquimault. He seemed quite at ease about the future peace and tranquility of Honolulu and his proposal to withdraw the ship under his command had the British Minister's consent. This change of attitude on the part of a senior naval officer who has been in sympathy with the Royalists, and probably to a considerable extent in their confidence, is significant. 

7. In the absence of outside interference I believe that a permanent form of government, republican in its character, will soon be declared in Hawaii and I see no reason to doubt its successful establishment. I have had several conversations with leading members of the Provisional Government and their advisers who are engaged in drafting the articles of a Constitution in preparation for the assembling of the Convention on the 30th instant. The most difficult question and the only source from which possible trouble is now in sight, lies in the demand for Japanese suffrage; i.e. for placing the Japanese upon the same footing as Americans and Europeans in this particular. Their 

Diplomatic Agent, Mr. Fujii, is even at this early day pressing this demand upon the authorities with considerable force and vigor. There are more than twenty thousand Japanese in the Islands, the greater number of whom, those who are free as well as those still under contract, are engaged as laborers upon the sugar plantations, but a certain number live in the towns and villages as servants and engaged in petty occupations. They have not as yet acquired property interests but are purely an importation of foreign labor. 

8. The people at present in power, while not desiring in any way to restrict the suffrage of native Hawaiians, do not wish to grant

to a horde of Asiatics a privilege, the exercise of which might become exceedingly embarrassing and perhaps dangerous to the state. Of these twenty odd thousand Japanese not less than 80% are men. They will not contract for labor unless a certain percentage of women, about one to five men, are included. The voters, therefore would be 80% of the whole number, and they are clannish, obstinate and would vote as a body, following the dictates of their leaders and regarding only their own interests. The situation is abnormal and its difficulties and dangers can readily be perceived. 

9. Mr. Fujii does not object to placing property or other qualifications upon the right of suffrage, but insists that his people shall be accorded precisely the same privileges that other foreigners receive. The attitude which the Japanese Government would take should this demand not be complied with, has not yet been made apparent. It might withdraw its citizens or refuse to sanction further labor immigration, or it might bring pressure to bear upon the Hawaiian Government. 

10. In a conversation with Mr. Armstrong who, although not an official of the Provisional Government, is well known as one of its confidential advisors, Mr. Fujii became somewhat imperative and menacing and hinted at the military and naval strength of Japan and the possible increase of her naval force here. 

11. I do not suppose that Japan would attempt, or that other nations would permit her, to employ coercion in the matter, but should any widespread disaffection of the Japanese on the plantations take place it would be a serious menace to Hawaiian prosperity; and should such disaffection be attended by a general refusal to work many planters would be ruined. The question has its economical as well as its political side and both are important. A further development of the situation must probably await the debates in the Government. 

12. I will keep you informed, either by official despatches or private letters as may best suit the circumstances, of all that happens here, and with the assurance of my best regards 

Honorable H. A. Herbert 

Secretary of the Navy." 

I remain 

Very truly yours,

J. G. Walker Rear Admiral U. S. Navy

On May 24th Mr. Reid, Minister of Public Defense in Victoria, 

Australia, arrived on the Canadian Pacific steamer from a trip to England in the interests of a cable company to connect British Columbia with New Zealand. He stated they would land cable only on British territory. 

In the same steamer was a British engineer who immediately began collecting information about the position of Neckar's Island. On the same day the Commanding Officer of H.M.S. Champion stated he would take his ship to sea for target practice, to be absent a week. As this ship and Captain some two years since took possession of Johnson's Island, it was soon rumored that the Champion was bound for Neckar's Island. This island has always been claimed by Hawaii. On the 25th a small steamer, Iwalani, was sent to formerly take possession, which it accomplished successfully. "Honolulu, H.I. May 31, 1894

Sir: 

I have the honor to make the following report in regard to the condition of affairs here since my No. 13 of the 26th instant.

With the exception of the temporary excitement caused by the rumor that the H.M.S. Champion had gone to sea to take possession of 

Neckar's Island described in my No. 14, nothing worthy of note has occurred until yesterday when the Constitutional Covention met. 

The Minister of Foreign Affairs extended to me and to the officers under my command an invitation to be present at the opening of the Convention by his Excellency the President, and the invitation was accepted.

After consultation with the United States Minister we decided to go together and a little before eleven o'clock Mr. Willis and myself accompanied by my staff, Captain Barker and the officers of the Philadelphia arrived at the Hall where the Delegates to the Convention were to meet. Seats were reserved for us near the front of the Hall, also for the members of the Supreme Court, Foreign Representatives and the Japanese Captain and officers. The public was also admitted to the body and rear of the Hall. 

At eleven o'clock precisely President Dole, accompanied by the Executive Council, entered and took their places in front of the delegates, the President occupying the Chair of the Presiding Officer. 

After prayer the President read an address, a copy of which is herewith enclosed, which was received with applause, the roll of the Delegates was then called, after which a motion was made to adjourn, in commemoration of Memorial Day, until the 31st instant at ten o'clock, the motion was carried and the President declared the Convention adjourned. 

I cannot speak too highly of the order, decorum and simplicity which prevailed at this, the first step in the formation of a new Government. There was no hesitation, all went smoothly and perfect order reigned throughout.

On the afternoon of the same day the Philadelphia's Battalion of 220 men was landed and joined the George W. DeLong Post, Grand Army of the Republic, in the ceremonies at the cemetary in decorating the graves of ex-soldiers and sailors of the United States. A Battalion of 

Hawaiian Militia was also paraded and assisted with the ceremonies. 

These were attended by the United States Minister, myself and staff and Captain Barker and the officers of the Philadelphia. 

I am, Sir 

To the Secretary"

Sir: 

1. I have the honor to report further regarding the political situation in these islands: 

2. As indicated in my No. 15, the Constitutional Convention met on May 30th and after President' Dole's address, adjourned in observance of Decoration Day. Its sessions were resumed on May 31st, and have continued from day to day, having been interrupted only by the observance of a National holiday, "Kamehameha Day", the 11th of June. Its work has been carried on in a business-like and harmonious manner, which promises well for satisfactory results, and the completion of its work in the near future. 

3. The draft of the Constitution submitted by the Executive Council has passed its first reading in Committee of the Whole with numerous amendments and modifications, and it is rumored that it is intended to declare a republic and adopt the Constitution on July 4th next. 

4. The "Australia" arriving on the 16th instant, brought the text of the Senate resolution on Hawaii and the press comments thereupon; immediately afterwards the Honolulu papers published the letter of Mr. Willis to Mr. Hatch, Minister of Foreign Affairs of the Provisional Government, transmitting a copy of Mr. Gresham's letter to himself, which formally announced the action of the Senate and expressed the President's satisfaction therewith. 

5. This definite announcement of policy expressed in terms so clear and unmistakeable, has strengthened the hands of the existing government, and will undoubtedly assist in the solution of certain difficult questions which attend the adoption of a Constitution and the establishment of a permanent form of government. The Royalists are correspondingly depressed and discouraged. It is understood that their leaders have held several informal conferences upon the situation, but there is nothing to indicate any organization or plan of action involving the use of force. In my opinion the peace is not in danger of being disturbed at present. 

6. As is known to the Department, at the instance of the 

Provisional Government, the crews of our war vessels have not been landed for drills some months past, because of the unsettled political conditions. This is a privation more particularly felt in mastless ships where the opportunities for training and exercises are diminished. Believing that matters had so nearly reached a final settlement that no prejudice could attach to landing, and having been privately assured that there was no objection whatever on the part of the Provisional Government to my doing so, I obtained through the United States Minister formal permission to land the battalion once a week,

the permission being promptly and courteously granted. The flagship's men were landed as infantry on the 19th instant and will continue to land every Tuesday. 

7. The Japanese training ship, Kongo, arrived on the 5th instant from Japan, having thirty cadets on board, intending to remain two weeks and then proceed on her cruise. 

8. The unexpected recall of the Takachino which sailed for home on the 19th has caused a change in the "Kon-go's" orders and she will remain here for the present. 

9. I enclose clippings from the Honolulu papers giving proceedings of the Constitutional Convention and comments on the Senate Resolution regarding Hawaii. 

I am, Sir, Very respectfully, Secretary of the Navy 

Navy Department, Washington, D. C. "Honolulu, H.I. June 23, 1894

PERSONAL 

My dear Mr. Herbert 

1. I report in this mail through official despatches upon all matters of public interest; but as upon a previous occasion there is certain information, more or less definite and authentic, coming from private and semi-official sources, which colors the situation and which is better placed at your disposition by means of a private letter. 

2. General permission to land the Naval forces of the United 

States for drills on shore had existed until November 29th, 1893, upon which date it was revoked by the Provisional Government, leaving the situation as it usually exists in the ports of a foreign country; viz: that permission must be applied for and obtained in each case before armed men can be landed for any purpose. 

3. But it was lately intimated to me that the Government would now have no objection to drills being renewed, and that an application for permission to send my forces ashore would receive prompt and satisfactory attention. In mastless ships it is difficult to give the men sufficient exercise on board and I was desirous for sanitary reasons, of getting them on shore for drills which are of great advantage to their health and physical vigor, and I felt that the political matters had arrived at a point where such action could not be attended with any prejudice to public feeling and security. I therefore obtained from the Government, through Mr. Willis, United States Minister, the requi-

site authority. This was done before the arrival of the "Australia" on the 16th instant with the text of the Senate resolution and its official promulgation. 

4. The flagship's battalion landed on Tuesday the 19th unattended by any unusual excitement or interest, and will be landed each Tuesday here after. 5. 

Immediately after the landing of the force under my command, the British Minister, Major Wodehouse, requested permission to land the Champion's men for a similar purpose. This request was taken under advisement by the Executive Council, has not yet been granted and I understand that there is a probability of its being refused. The ground taken by the Provisional Government is this: that Major Wodehouse and 

Captain Rooke, commanding the Champion, instead of remaining aloof from politics, have been active and offensive partisans of the Royalist cause, that Major Wodehouse's residence has been a centre of Royalist conferences, and that he has constantly gone out of his way to embarrass the Provisional Government and encourage its opponents, and that in view of this well known attitude of the British Minister and the Senior British Naval Officer, the landing of the Champion's force might have upon the public mind an influence inimical to the present government and injurious to the prompt establishment of confidence and order. All the information which I have had from various sources. bearing upon this point confirms the truth of the Government's accusations against the British Minister. Indeed from what I hear it would be perfectly justified in refusing him further recognition, and would probably have done so but for the fact that he has reached the retiring age and notice has been received that he will soon be replaced by a new man. The refusal to land an armed force is an act of sovereignty which may be exercised at discretion; and although it would be under ordinary circumstances offensively invidious to grant the privilege to one foreign power and refuse it to another, it is my opinion that under the existing circumstances the Provisional Government would be entirely justified in making this distinction between the British and other nations, and that such a decision would command respect here from men of all opinions. 

7. Since the full significance and effect of the Senate resolution and its endorsement by the President have become generally digested and understood, a number of conferences have been held, I am informed, by the Royalist leaders. I am sure I am safe in saying that no attempt to restore the Queen by force of arms is seriously entertained at these conferences. They recognize the fact that without a substantial military force, organized and equipped, such an attempt would be futile. 

The Provisional Government is firmly established and prepared to maintain its title. It could not be ousted without serious struggle. The 

Royalists will probably cast about for the best terms they can make and will then give their adhesion to the republic. 8.

A native, one of the ex-Queen's confidential advisors, and of substance and standing, sought an interview with me on the 21st for the purpose of talking over the situation. He recognized that a restoration was impossible, and said that, while as a Hawaiian he had a natural senti-

ment of loyalty to his race and his Queen, he had nothing to say against the men at the head of the present government; that they were good citizens and good men and universally respected. As a man of large property he is interested in the prosperity of the islands and desirous of seeing matters settled upon the best practicable terms and a condition of permanent peace and quiet assured. He seemed inclined to influence the ex-Queen to surrender all claims to the throne in exchange for a suitable pension. This I think could be easily arranged if the proper initiative were taken by her authority, and such settlement would be generally acceptable. Certain business men not personally engaged in politics, but desirous for business reasons, of seeing everything once more upon a secure basis, are exerting their good offices to bring about an understanding and arrangement of this or similar character. 

9. The Constituion in the shape in which it has passed the first reading in Committee of the Whole is now in the hands of the printer. 

I shall send a copy to the Department as soon as I can obtain one, certainly by the next mail. 

I am, Sir, Very respectfully, J. G. Walker 

Rear Admiral, U. S. Navy

Commanding U. S. Naval Force on Pacific Station.

The Secretary of the Navy 

Navy Department, Washington, D. C." "Flagship Philadelphia 

Honolulu, H.I. June 23, 1894

Honolulu, H. I. June 20, 1894

Referring to our conversation this morning, when I called upon you to state that the Government considered the present occasion (you having called upon us to protect you in respect to a newspaper attack) a fitting one to urge you to express more clearly and definitely the nature of your relations with this government and your attitude toward the same; and in which you stated that you had recognized the present government as the defacto government of the country, but were unable to make any further statement in regard to your position, and referring to the fact that you had been accredited to the former government, that you desired to remain in a strictly neutral position; and that you had refrained from attending any of the functions held by this government, especially the opening of the Constitutional Convention, for the reason that you could not do so without taking sides and departing from your position of neutrality; and, in reply to my question, positively denying that you have maintained any official or diplomatic relations with Liliuokalani since her dethronement;

I beg that you will inform me whether I have correctly understood you, and am to take the foregoing as the correct expression of your position and views on this subject.

"Department of Foreign Affairs 

Honolulu, H.I. June 23, 1894

I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt of your Excellency's letter of the 20th instant requesting permission for Captain Rooke of H.B.M.'s S. "Champion" to land his men for the purpose of drill; and also your letter of the 21st inst. in which you state that you cannot see any reason for further or public declaration of your position toward the Hawaiian Provisional Government, nor can you discover any cause for the dissatisfaction with the "attitude of reserve" which you have maintained and which has the approval of your Government. 

As you do not question the accuracy of my statement of your remarks at our last interview and do not modify them I am compelled to refer to what you then said for explanation of what you name "your attitude of reserve".

This attitude as explained by you, is so extraordinary that it cannot be lost sight of on an occasion like the present, when the matter of granting an extreme favor is under consideration; the carrying out of which on your part might be subject to grave misconstruction in the public mind, in case there should be the slightest doubt as to the nature of your relations to the government.

I regret extremely, therefore, that your inability, at this time, to make such full and satisfactory statement of your attitude as would put all doubt at rest, compels the Government to refrain from entertaining your request for permission to land troops from the "Champion" for drill.

I avail myself of this opportunity to renew the assurances of high consideration and esteem with which 

I have the honor to be, Sir,

Your Excellency's 

Most obedient humble servant (signed) Francis M. Hatch

Minister of Foreign Affairs." 

On July 4th, 1894 the Republic of Hawaii was formally proclaimed from the steps of the Iolani Palace, at that time used as the Executive Building, and Admiral Walker and his staff attended as spectators together with hundreds of others. When the hour for the proclamation arrived President Dole, followed by members of the Executive

Council, the Chief Justice and other officers of the Government, descended half-way the steps of the Government Building. The steps and the balcony of the building were filled with people and hundreds more were assembled on the grounds in front. President Dole made a brief address and proclaimed the Republic as the future government of the islands and the Constitution recently adopted as its fundamental law, the Hawaiian flag (in which no change had been made) was displayed over the building, the band played the Hawaiian National Air and a salute of twenty-one guns was fired. The proceedings were orderly and impressive, there was no parade of troops nor display of military force and hearty cheers were given for the new republic and its President, after which the assemblage dispersed.

The fourth of July as a National Holiday of the United States was observed by the foreign men-of-war in port by dressing ship with the American Ensign at the main and by firing a national salute at noon. 

The shore battery fired a salute sunrise, noon and sunset and there was a profuse disply of American flags on shore. The Fourth of July has always been regarded by the residents both native and foreign as the principal holiday of the year and celebrated as such and on this occasion all the men-of-war joined with Honolulu associates in boat races and various aquatic sports in the forenoon, while in the afternoon Minister and Mrs. Willis gave a general reception at the Hawaiian 

Hotel and the festivities were closed by a very fine disply of fireworks in the evening.

On July 2nd, immediately before the proclamation of the Republic there took place two political meetings, one a Royalist meeting in Palance Square which Admiral Walker attended himself, the other at the "Drill Shed" held by supporters of the Government was witnessed by an officer of the Admiral's staff.

In a letter of July 12th to the Secretary of the Navy he gives a brief account of these meetings and his opinion of the political situ ation at that time, which seems worth giving here in his own words. 

"I entered Palace Square at 5.10, and remained until 5.25, at which time the meeting had not been organized. A good many people were present, perhaps twelve hundred of these several hundred were Chinese (who take no interest whatever in politics) and a large number were native women. There were also many bystanders, the hour being one when the business houses are closing and the employees returning to their homes, and when people drive. Everything was quiet and orderly and the meeting was held without let or hindrance on the part of the Provisional Government. 

The protest was read and greeted with cheers, but no plan of action was adopted or even suggested. The ratification meeting was witnessed by an officer of my staff. It was composed entirely of men, of whom the majority were white, and numbered probably a thousand. It was held under the auspices of the annexation club and every mention of annexation and of President Dole was greeted with cheers..

A permanent form of government has been quietly and peacefully inaugurated, an organic law has been adopted and the officers of that government have assumed their duties under the provisons of that organic law. The Provisional Government is a thing of the past, and the chapter of Hawaiian political history which treats of the transition from monarchy to a republic is distinctly closed. There are no new phases to anticipate, there are no events to wait for with hope that they may modify the situation. The Royalists must accept the Republic or attempt a counter revolution by physical force, and of such an attempt there is, in my opinion, no probability whatever. I anticipate the speedy recognition of the

Republic by foreign powers, the gradual extinction of hostile domestic sentiment and the ultimate conversion of the Royalist into an "opposition" under the Constituion.

Under these circumstances I do not consider the constant presence of an American flag officer at Honolulu as longer necessary. One of our ships should still be kept here, and care should be taken that her Commanding Officer is one in whose judgment and discretion the Department has confidence; but the critical period during which it was all-important that American naval influence should be paramount has been passed; and 

Honolulu might now be safely regarded as having reverted to the former status a port of this station especially important because of its 

American interests and affiliations... 

Admiral Walker's personal letter to Secretary Herbert of the same date repeats that he no longer considered it necessary keep an American flag officer constantly at Honolulu; he felt that the peaceful proclamation of the Republic had resolved speculation and conjecture into fact and that the future would bring the gradual changes in feeling and

opinion which are the result of time. "There is no probability of any disturbance inimical to American life or property, hardly a possibility of such an event.......The Republic is too strong in physical force to be attacked with any prospect of success." 

In his opinion a ship should, however, be kept there to guard against the unexpected with a commanding officer of sound judgment. "Royalty is traditional in these islands and confidence in republican institutions is not manufactured in a day. The moral protectorate which the United States has long exercised over them should not at this juncture be relinquished or interrupted. 

I have heard rumors during the past few months from various sources, and I am inclined to think they may have some foundation in fact, that the native and other Royalist leaders are preparing a petition, to be signed by their followers, praying for annexation to the United States, and that their object is, by transferring the country to the United States, to deprive the Missionary Party of their present power and influence. Such a course would simply strengthen the present government as annexation is still its cherished object. " 

The following personal letter to Secretary Herbert bears the same date, July 12th, 1894: "My dear Mr. Herbert:

I was privately informed on the 10th instant by Mr. Hatch, the Minister of Foreign Affairs, that he had been told by Mr. Samuel 

Parker, the late premier of Liliuokalani's Cabinet, that he (Parker) and Judge H. A. Widemann were going to Washington to ascertain why the President had made no direct reply to the ex-Queen's protest and to obtain from him a final statement of his attitude towards her restoration. From every point of view here, this proceeding seems to be entirely futile, and it doubtless emanates from the ex-Queen herself. 

Mr. Parker was the Minister of Foreign Affairs of the last 

Cabinet of the Queen. He is one quarter white, and is a typical 

Hawaiian in all his mental qualities, - amiable, generous, heedless and irresponsible, more like a boy of twelve than a grown up man. He has had a very large property, but has squandered a considerable part of it by reckless extravagance and is now substantially a bankrupt. 

Everybody calls him "Sam Parker" and everybody likes him, but he has none of the qualities of leadership and has no substantial following. 

Judge Widemann is a German who came here fifty years ago and has acquired a considerable fortune. He married a native woman and has brought up a large family that is universally respected. His daughters were educated in Germany and have married well. He has been a Judge of the Supreme Court and a Cabinet Minister. He is honest and upright but is a man of narrow views, strong likes and dislikes and bitter prejudices. He has backed the ex-Queen financially since her de thronement, supplying the money to send Mr. Paul Newman to Washington in February 1893, securing himself by mortgage upon her property. He is an ardent Royalist, influenced doubtless to some extent by financial

transactions and still further by his personal dislike to certain men prominent in the Provisional Government and Republic. His long residence here, his native marriage and family and his wealth give his opinion and attitude considerable weight amongst Hawaiians. 

I have been approached in confidence on the subject of his mission by three prominent men, representing three different points of view, - Mr. Hatch, the Minister of Foreign Affairs, Mr. Parker himself, and a prominent and wealthy planter and sugar factor who takes no part in politics, but who is desirous for business reasons to see matters finally settled. They all agree that restoration is hopeless and that the mission in this respect must necessarily be without results. Mr. 

Parker apparently has no heart in the work, but goes solely at the request of Liliuokalani, who insists that she is entitled to a final answer and that, having failed to obtain it through the usual channels, this method must be adopted. He advised against it but has been overruled. He thinks, however, that nothing but the President's positive statement made to her or to her representatives will satisfy the ex- Queen, and that upon receiving his assurance that the United States intend to do nothing more, an arrangement can be made by which she will surrender her claims and accept a pension, and that in this respect something of value may be accomplished. 

The move is an unexpected one but as far as order and security are concerned it does not modify in the least the situation as reported to the Department in my official depatches. It is rather an additional demonstration of the fact that the Royalist Party has no hope of success unless aided from outside, and that it has no intention of attempting a counter revolution by force. I have been told by a prominent Royalist that at a meeting of their leaders, held since the proclamation of the Republic, he suggested that they subscribe money to buy arms and place themselves in a position to fight and that the suggestion fell flat, receiving absolutely no support whatever. This suggestion was made as an alternative to end the policy of inaction, either to fight or accept the Republic and the Constitution and retain political power by means of votes.

I think it proper that the President should be as fully informed as possible of the condition of affairs and the character of the gentlemen going to Washington but, as my information came to me in confidence, I must beg that this communication shall otherwise be considered and treated as strictly confidential. " 

Everything remained quiet during July and August and Admiral

Walker reported to the Secretary of the Navy that the Government was working smoothly and that there was no probability of any move upon the part of the Royalists until after the return of Messrs. Parker, Widemann and 

Cummings from Washington, and in his opinion there was none that there would be any trouble of a serious nature after their return, the Republic being firmly established. 

In his letter to Mr. Herbert of July 12th Admiral Walker had asked to be relieved of his command and ordered home as he felt there was no longer any danger of a Royalist rising and the presence of a flag

officer at Honolulu was unnecessary. He had left home at brief notice after a long cruise in command of a squadron, and while he had no desire to give up his command while there was any danger of a political crisis, he felt that his own personal affairs needed attention and he would be glad to return to Washington. On August 2nd he received a telegram from the Secretary of the Navy ordering him to proceed to Mare Island with the Philadelphia and turn over the command of the 

Pacific Station to his successor. 

The following portions of a letter to Secretary Herbert written at sea en route to San Francisco on August 17th, explains the delay in sailing. After acknowledging the receipt of his orders, Admiral Walker writes: 

"I gave orders to coal the ship and fixed the 8th as the day of sailing. The Commanding Officer of H.M.S. Champion had announced the receipt of orders to take certain deep sea soundings in the neighborhood of the Hawaiian Islands to aid in determing the feasibility of a cable route, and fixed the 6th as his day of departure. This work was to occupy the Champion nearly three weeks, before the expiration of which time her relief, the Hyacinth, was expected to arrive at Honolulu.

I felt that the departure of both the Philadelphia and the Champion (the only men-of-war at Honolulu) would not be, under existing political conditions, unfavorable to order and security in the islands. 

It would leave both parties entirely free and dependent upon their own resources, without any support, nominal or actual, from an outside source. 

In my opinion the Republic has ample strength to maintain itself and to preserve peace, and would suppress riot or a counter revolution with a firm hand; but as long as foreign vessels lay in the harbor of Honolulu ready at a moment's notice, in the event of disturbance, to land forces to secure the safety of life and property, it was open to the enemies of the government to say that tranquility was maintained only by the presence of those ships.
