I found leading members of the government and others strongly of this opinion, and expressing satisfaction with the prospect of being left for a time entirely alone. 

But as soon as it was known the the Philadelphia was to leave on the 8th, a movement was set on foot among the English residents to retain the Champion, and the British Minister, Major Wodehouse, willingly consented to order her to remain in port. 

This change of plan, and the circumstances under which it was made, put the situation in a new and somewhat serious light, to thoroughly appreciate which it is necessary to understand the attitude and ambitions of Major Wodehouse.

He has been at Honolulu nearly thirty years, and his constant struggle has been to set English influence against American influence and make it predominate, and as an essential part of this policy to maintain the monarchy. The successful revolution and the establishment of a republic during the last year of his diplomatic service, upon the eve of

his retirement, was an evidence of failure which has made him bitter and hostile. Although holding the position of British Minister, and transacting diplomatic business with the government de facto, he had been a pronounced and partisan Royalist. It is currently believed that he had been in constant secret communication with Liliuokalani, and entirely in the confidence of the Royalist leaders, and he had certainly lost no opportunity to embarrass the present government, disregarding the courtesies and amenities due from one of his position. But for his impending retirement, matters would probably, some time ago, have come to an issue which would have resulted in his recall or dismissal. The commanding officer of the Champion had the same interests and view and was quite ready to support his minister. 

These unusual circumstances presented the situation in the following light:

The chances for any disturbance taking place through the action of the Royalists after the departure of the Philadelphia were considerably increased by this significant detention of the Champion. Should any such disturbance take place I believed the British Minister would seize upon any pretext, however slight, to land the Champion's forces, and that once on shore their influence would be to encourage the royalists to increase or prolong the difficulty. Should such action result in the restoration of the monarchy, English influence would henceforth control with Liliuokalani.

There is another consideration which bears upon this point. 

The British are very much in the habit of retaining any foothold once obtained, and there is much talk at present of a cable between the 

Pacific Colonies "to land only on British soil". Should an English military force be established on shore at Honolulu to preserve order, there might not readily be found the opportune moment for its withdrawal. Whatever might be the willingness of the Home Government to yield to the claims of the United States, strong colonial pressure would be brought upon it to oppose these claims. It should not be forgotten in this connection that Pearl Harbor offers strategically and otherwise, the finest site for a naval and coaling station to be found in the whole Pacific.

Although I had full confidence in the Republic's ability to preserve order and regarding the chance of an uprising of the royalists, even under the favorable circumstances mentioned above, as very remote, I did not feel that even such a remote chance should be taken, and I decided to postpone the Philadelphia's departure at least until the arrival of the Australia's mail on the 11th instant. Before reaching this conclusion, I consulted with the United States Minister, whose views coincided entirely with my own, and I was further confirmed in my opinion by the receipt of a petition to keep the Philadelphia at Honolulu, signed by several prominent merchants and business men representing large American moneyed interests. This petition is enclosed. 

Before the arrival of the Australia Major Wodehouse had been relieved by Captain A.G.S. Hawes, who came in the steamer Arawa from his late post at Tahiti and who has the title of British Commissioner

and Consul General. The advent of a new man, who had no other policy than the policy of his government, and was not involved in local politics, changed the situation again, and feeling that the risk involved was then considerably diminished, I left Honolulu on the 12th. 

It is a fact which should not be lost sight of that English influence is always opposed to American influence in the Hawaiian Islands. It is colonial and local in its character, influenced largely, if not principally by trade jealosies, but it is a very potent factor, and is strong enough to eventually influence the attitude of the British diplomatic representative and to have its effect upon British Naval Officers who remain here for any length of time. The existence of this antagonism and of its influences should always be taken into account. Their expression was noticeable during the last few days of my stay in Honolulu, especially before Major Wodehouse ceased to be Minister. The English and royalists were equally elated at the impending departure of the Philadelphia, leaving the Champion in port, while the supporters of the government regarded the situation with regret. Many women especially were emphatic in their apprehension, fearing a revival of former excitements and anxieties. 

When I left Honolulu the Champion was to remain until the arrival of the Hyacinth, which plan keeps an English ship continually at that port. Under these circumstances an American ship should also be kept there all the time. While I would not hesitate in the least to leave the islands entirely alone, I deem it unsafe to leave them under British protection. It jeopardizes our influence there, and might lead to awkward complications. The position of the United States, as expressed by the late Senate resolution, is sufficiently defined, and in its relation to other powers is clearly understood by both natives and foreigners in Hawaii; but that position involves responsibilities as well as rights, and if we are not at hand to perform the duties of our virtual protectorate, no reproach can attach to another power which, in the event of a disturbance, steps in and takes our place. It would have a strong argument in its favor; and that 

British sentiment in the Pacific would eagerly embrace such an opportunity and would make the most of its consequences, I have little doubt. 

Had the Department's orders given me discretion in the matter I should have left the Philadelphia at Honolulu and returned to San Francisco with my staff by mail steamer, failing the arrival of the Charleston by the 18th; but as they were entirely unqualified I had no choice but to obey them at the earliest moment that such a course was justified by paramount public interests. I trust that another ship will be sent to Honolulu at the earliest practicable moment.' " 

The Philadelphia reached San Francisco on August 22nd, ten days from Honolulu the ship steamed badly on account of the foul condition of her bottom after eleven months in the harbor of Honolulu, during which time the bottom had not been cleaned by divers for fear of removing some of the paint with the barnacles and other marine growth and thus endangering the metal. As soon as the ship was definitely ordered to San Francisco Admiral Walker had divers begin work, working every day from August 1st until putting to sea on the 12th, but they succeeded in

cleaning only the screws, shafts and rudder and a small portion of the ship's bottom. As a result the ship "was exceedingly sluggish in her movements, making at first only seven knots, which gradually increased to nearly ten as she became lighter forward from consumption of coal and fresh water......It would probably be impossible under such circumstances to force the ship with the full power of her machinery at a speed greater than twelve or thirteen knots. A cruiser, therefore, whose bottom becomes fouled to this extent is disabled in her most valuable quality, and can neither maneuver to fight an enemy of equal force, nor escape from one of greater force. " "A valuable article was published in the latest number of the 

Naval Institute Proceedings - No. 69. It was written by Lieutenant 

Commander Sebree and describes the manner of keeping the Baltimore's bottom clean by employing divers, and the discussion of the paper the result of such work upon the Boston's bottom was set forth. 

It appears in both cases that the bottoms of the ships were kept clean without injury to the metal. Had I been in possession of this information when I took command of the station, I should have ordered the Philadelphia's bottom to be cleaned in the same manner at that time. The value of clean bottoms is enormous, and if the evidence before the Department is sufficient to warrant cleaning them by divers without fear of injury to the metal, I would recommend the issuance of general instructions to this effect."

On August 24th, 1894 Admiral Walker hauled down his flag and turned over the command of the Pacific Station to his successor, issuing the following general order. "Mare Island, California, August 24,1894

In obedience to orders from the Navy Department, I this day haul down my flag and turn over the command of the Pacific Station to my successor, Rear Admiral Lester A. Beardslee.

To the officers and men of the Flagship, with whom my brief personal association has been of a most agreeable character, and to those serving in distant parts of the station, I extend, with my goodbye, hearty wishes for their future success and prosperity. 

J. G. Walker 

Rear Admiral, U. S. Navy,

Commanding U. S. Naval Force on Pacific 

Station"

Admiral Walker left a letter for the next commanding officer to be ordered to Hawaii, giving him a brief account of what had been done there during his time in command of the station, as he felt that the new command would be greatly helped upon arrival by some understanding of the situation; he also left for him a copy of the confidential letter of 

March 27, 1894 from the Secretary of the Navy which outlined his duties

and would of course apply also to his successor. In addition to these he left a very confidential memorandum giving his opinion of some of the men with whom the newcomer would be thrown which seems sufficiently interesting and of enough importance to warrant inserting here in spite of the note in red ink "To be destroyed as soon as contents noted". 

CONFIDENTIAL No. 4. 

U.S. Flagship Philadelphia 

Honolulu, H. I. August 11, 1894

Sir: 

1. I enclose for your information and guidance a copy of the confidential instructions given to me upon my taking command of this station relating to the attitude of the Government of the United States toward political parties in these islands. 

2. Since my arrival here in April last the peace of the islands has not been disturbed and the general political tendancy has been toward the consolidation and strengthening of the party in power. Although during the interval there have taken place in due order and sequence all the events incidental to the transformation from a Provisional Government to a permanent government republican in form and name, the royalists have made no attempt to recover by force the direction of public affairs, and the deposed queen has remained quietly in retirement. Many of the individual royalists and several papers devoted to the restoration of the monarchy are noisy and blatant. There have been meetings and resolutions and protests; but no leaders are forth coming to attempt to restore Liliuokalani to the throne by force; nor to the best of my knowledge is money offered to purchase guns or arms. Freedom of speech is absolute and the government interferes in no way with meetings, speeches or papers; and talk has been the sole support of the royalists cause during my stay in Honolulu. 

3. A careful reading of the Constitution will thoroughly inform you as to the manner of government which has been established under the title of the Republic of Hawaii. 

4. The President and Cabinet and the principal subordinate officials of this government are the same as those formerly in power under the Provisional Government. From an executive point of view the government has simply changed its name without any disburbance of men or officers. 

5. The task of framing a permanent government was confronted by grave difficulties, some of which can best be appreciated by understanding what constitutes the population of these islands. There are about forty thousand native Hawaiians, a decadent and rapidly diminishing race, indolent, pleasure-loving and of little account as factors in civilization and progress. They still own a good deal of land, but it is rapidly

slipping into the possession of people of more industry and thrift. 

6. With the development of the sugar industry after the reciprocity treaty of 1876, it was found necessary to import labor and this has brought to the country large numbers of Japanese, Chinese and Portugese. Of the first there are now more than 20,000 in the several islands, somewhat less than half of them being under contract and the remainder are free laborers whose contracts have expired. It is a shifting population as many return to Japan as soon as their contracts are at an end and others come to take their places. The Chinese number ten to twelve thousand and the Portugese about the same. These three elements have almost entirely displaced the native Hawaiians from all manual labor and industrial pursuits. The Chinese even grow the taro and catch the fish which form the staple articles of native food. 

7. The few thousand white people of the better class, of alien parentage or birth who own the greater part of the property and possess intelligence, energy and purpose, are, and always have been, the real rulers of the islands. They ruled them under the monarchy, they rule them now and they will continue to rule them under any form of government. To the outside world they are the Hawaiian Islands. 8.

The majority of these people composed principally of Americans and Germans deposed the Queen, established the Provisional Government and are now the party in power. The minority composed mainly of English are the backbone and mainstay of the royalist party. Politics in Hawaii today is the struggle for supremacy between these two factions of a small minority. 

9. In the formation of a permanent form of government of a popular character, the question of suffrage in its several phases became the leading one, whether it should be universal or restricted, whether it should be extended to Asiatics or not, and whether or not aliens of substance and intelligence who did not wish to surrender their allegiance to the countries of their birth should be allowed under any circumstances to vote. The Japanese presented a decided front against any general exclusion as a race of Asiatics and a Japanese Man-of-War was stationed here. The Portugese had political organizations and their own leaders were in the field for political preferment. The Chinese took no very active part, but were influenced to some degress by the prevalent feeling of political unrest and change. These three races included almost entirely the labor element and each of them was clannish, numerically strong, unused to the responsibilities of suffrage and in danger of becoming a menace to the new institutions. 

10. This problem of suffrage in all its phases was in my opinion solved by the Constitutional Convention with skill and good judgment. 

11. In the formation of the Constitutional Convention the Provisional Government kept the balance of power in its own hands. The President, Executive Council and Advisory Council of the Provisional Government (19 in number) were made members, and eighteen outside delegates, apportioned among the several islands, were elected by those qualified to vote under existing laws, who took a test oath not to support restora-

tion of the monarchy. This Convention elected Mr. Dole, President for six years, embodying this act as an article of the Constitution; and the result of its labors was not referred back to the voters for ratification; but was proclaimed as the supreme law of the land on the 

4th of July. 

12. This procedure gave partial, but not full expression to the voice of the people. It would not have been regarded as sufficiently democratic in one of our own States, but it must be remembered that Hawaii is a country just emerging from monarchial rule and not yet educated to democracy, a similar action marked the establishment of the 

French Republic. I believe the authorities of the Provisional Govern ment to have acted with wisdom and prudence in keeping the control in their own hands while admitting to their councils a certain number of delegates fresh from the people. They have ruled the country for sixteen months and they were responsible for order and security. They could not afford to run any risk of being swamped by an ignorant vote led by demagogues, or a hostile vote influenced by political passion or fear, and they therefore postponed the full expression of the qualified suffrage in its effect upon all branches of the Government until 1900. 

13. In the discussion and votes of the Convention no line was drawn between the nineteen members of the Provisional Government and the other eighteen delegates; but on the contrary there was unusual unanimity upon all important questions, showing that the Provisional Government had the confidence of all classes except the pronounced royalists. 

14. I regard the present republican government as sound and stable and far better for the people of Hawaii than any monarchy with a native head. Its officials are honest capable men strong in their intention and their power to maintain it. It keeps and will keep as long as royalist agitation continues, a sufficient military force to guard against any plots or surprises. It is genuinely republican in spirit and principle and in its dispositions for the future. 

15. The royalist party is mainly led by whites, of whom the 

English are largely in the majority. Some of these are royalists by conviction, others through sympathy or self interest. The natives do not care much about politics. They have little sentiment of loyalty to 

Liliuokalani, who has none of the Kamehameha blood, and is not regarded as holding by inalienable right. They have a natural race feeling and do not like to see themselves wholly supplanted by whites, and they have some apprehension that if they give in their adhesion to the republic they may be exposed to the subsequent vengeance of a restored queen, and they are exceedingly credulous of the representations of their leaders who constantly feed their fears and prejudices with all sorts of stories and rumors but none of these sentiments are strong enough to result in the mainainance of a native element perpetually irreconcilable to the republic 15. English influence in these islands is always opposed to American influences. This fact should be constantly borne in mind. It is colonial and local in its character, influenced mainly by trade jealosies;

but it is a potent factor, strong enough to influence the attitude of the British diplomatic representative here; and to have its effect upon British naval officers who remain here for any length of time. 

The existence of this antagonism and the possibility of its influence upon the senior British Naval Officer should be taken into account in the event of any concerted action due to disturbances on shore. The fact that the whole world regards the United States as responsible for order and security in these islands gives to us greater rights and responsibilities in the event of political trouble than can be claimed by any other power. This is a corollary of the Turpis resolution and should not be overlooked by the officer in command of our naval force. 17.

The best tactical position for placing troops to protect American life and property is the grounds about the Hawaiian Hotel. 

18. The above sketch of Hawaiian affairs should aid in giving you a correct idea of the situation, and may be of value should you be called upon in the event of any political emergency to take decisive action. I also enclose a strictly confidential memorandum which I request you will not permit to pass out of your hands, and which should be destroyed after you have digested its contents. I commit the matter which it contains to writing with reluctance, but it is important that you should upon your arrival here be guided in your attitude towards the several interests and factions by knowledge derived from previous experience. 

Very respectfully

J. G. Walker Rear Admiral, U. S. Navy Commanding U. S. Naval Force, Pacific 

Station. 

To the

Commanding Officer, first U. S. Vessel-of-War arriving at Honolul 

To be destroyed as soon as contents noted. 

MEMORANDUM STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL H.I. 

Mr. Willis, the United States Minister, is an honest, amiable, kindly gentleman, loyal to his duties and to what he believes to be the best interests of the United States, but he is not an experienced diplomat, nor a man of commanding ability. He was set at an impossible task here: viz. that of inducing the Provisional Government to voluntarily surrender its power to the deposed queen:- and he has never receoved from his failure.

The Consul General, Mr. Mills, is a poor appointment. 

He is,

I believe, an Englishman by birth, and was a clerk in the State Depart-

ment; came out here as Mr. Blount's stenographer, and was sent back as Consul General. He probably understands the duties of his office 

I have heard no complaints as to that -- but he lacks proper weight and consideration in the community. I have been told that he is frequently under the influence of liquor. 

Of the Government, Mr. Dole, the President is strong, sincere and courageous. Mr. Hatch, the Minister for Foreign Affairs, is a clear headed, clever man, with an excellent estimate of men and measures. The opinions of either of them upon any important matter may be accepted as well founded, honest and reliable.

They devote more attention to political affairs than the other members of the Government. 

Mr. S. M. Damon, the Minister of Finance, has large private interests, and confines his attention to Government affairs, mainly to the business of his own portfolio. 

Both he and President Dole have a great affection for the natives, lean strongly to native interests, and are probably the two white men in Hawaii for whom the native Hawaiians have the greatest respect.

Mr. W. O. Smith, the Attorney General, is a successful lawyer and Captain King, the Minister of the Interior, is a plain sturdy man whose duties are wholly administrative in a business way. 

The "Missionary party" is a title of widely varying application both here and in the American papers. It sometimes means all those who attend the Central Union Church, and sometimes all those who support the revolution. There is a humorous saying in Honolulu that "any one who keeps sober, does not keep a native mistress and pays his debts" belongs to the "Missionary party". It is not true that the missionaries have accumulated as a class large properties. Some of them are well to do, but the great majority of them are of small means. The rich men of the islands are mainly those who, as in other countries, have devoted their time and attention solely to business and money making, and they do not as a class take an active part in politics. 

Among prominent citizens who actively support the present 

Government, are: Chief Justice Judd, C. L. Carter, Judge A. S. Hartwell, the Castles, C. M. Cooke, B. F. Dillingham, Robert Lewers, W. F. Allen,

J. R. Atherton and P. C. Jones. 

The leading white royalists in Honolulu are J. 0. Carter, Paul Neumann, H. A. Widemann, a wealthy German and A. S. Cleghorn, the titular father of the Princess Kiulani. Mr. Carter is a brother of the late Hawaiian Minister to the United States and is the only royalist in that family. His nephew, C. L. Carter, although quite a young man, has been prominent on the other side. Judge Widemann, is an old resident, has a native wife and half white children, and is a man of wealth and influence. Nomann is a clever Bohemian who came here from San Francisco ten years ago.

; "Billy" Cornwell, lives on the island of Mauii. a member of the ex-Queen's last cabinet, He is not a broad guage man, but is active and intriguing, and has a certain amount of influence. 

The most prominent native royalist leaders in Honolulu are "Sam" Parker, the late Prime Minister, and John Cummins. Sam Parker is one eighth white and John Cummins half white. Both are men who have possessed large wealth, but both have become much reduced in fortune by careless management and reckless extravagance, and Parker is a hopeless bankrupt. Neither of them are bitter, and neither has any hope of the restoration of Liliuokalani; but both have gone to Washington with Judge Widemann to ascertain definitely if the approval of the Turpis resolution is the final expression of the Administration's attitude towards Hawaiian politics; or if any thing further is to be done by the United States to restore the deposed queen. This mission has been undertaken at the ex-queen's urgent request. 

The late British Minister, Major Wodehouse, who has been here nearly thirty years, and Captain Eustace Rooke, Commanding H.M.S. 

Champion, are both pronounced partisans of the royalist party and of the restoration of the queen. They have stimulated and encouraged royalist hopes and would in my opinion have been glad at any time to promote any disorder which might have weakened American influence and increased the chances of a restoration. 

Kiulani who would have succeeded Liliuokalani upon the throne had the monarchy not been overthrown, has been educated in England, and her accession would mean increased English influence. 

Major Wodehouse has just been relieved by Captain Hawes who comes from a distant post (Tahiti) and who is quite free from local influences. He seems to be a good man and probably will follow the policy of the British Cabinet towards these islands, which is that of non-interference. Major Wodehouse has not yet left Honolulu. Captain 

Rooke will soon go away as the Champion is to be relieved by the Hyacinth." 

Before leaving Washington to take command of the Pacific

Station Commander Walker had been told that upon the expiration of his term of duty at Hawaii he would be ordered to the Naval Academy at Annapolis as Commandant and in August while still at the Islands he wrote the following letter to his wife: "My dear Betty

I received orders by the Monowai on the 2nd to take the Philadelphia to Mare Island, turn over the command to Beardslee, proceed to Annapolis and take charge as Superintendant of the Academy. 

An order three days later directs me to proceed from Mare's 

Island to Washington and report in person to the Secretary..

There may be some detention but I now expect to reach Washing-

ton about the 6th. I don't know anything about Academy matters, what changes of officers have been made during my absence, nor whether I shall be willing to accept the men who are there. I can't do satisfactory work without good tools and I don't mean to have my last duty on the active list a failure."

Owing to various delays Admiral Walker did not reach Washington until August 31st when he was surprised to learn that his orders to the Naval Academy had been suspended and receiving no explanation of the move and feeling that some explanation was due him, he wrote a personal letter to Secretary Herbert: "My dear Mr. Secretary

In obedience to your order, I came through from California as rapidly as possible, arriving here on the morning of the 31st ult. I was promptly handed an order signed by the Acting Secretary (Admiral Ramsay) suspending my orders to the Naval Academy. He informed me that it was by direction of the President. I am by this action placed upon waiting orders and in an embarrassing position. I can have no voice in the selection of officers for the Naval Academy, although if I go there I am expected to bring about much needed reforms in that institution. 

The places are being filled by men selected by Admiral Ramsay and Captain Phythian. They may be good men without being the men I want or those who will best work with me. It is clearly right that I should have a reasonable voice in their selection. If I am to do good work I must have good tools to work with, willing tools. to work with, willing tools. I have no intimation of why my orders to the Academy are suspended, the reason may be an excellent one, but without such knowledge the suspension acts as a fine in a monied way and as a mortification and indignity put upon me most unjustly, to which I am unwilling to quietly submit, and it seems to me that whatever the reason for this action may be, courtesy to an officer of my rank should prevent by being left in the dark and in my present unpleasant position.

This is a matter which has occurred during your absence from Washington and I have to request that before any further action is taken with regard to my assignment to duty I may be ordered to report to you in person that the matter may be talked over and a clear understanding arrived at. This is but just to me and I think best in all respects. 

I am with great respect, J. G. Walker 

Rear Admiral

To Hon. H. A. Herbert 

Secretary of the Navy

U. 

S. S. Dolphin"

While awaiting his orders Admiral Walker's attention was called to an interchange of letters between Mr. Gresham, Secretary of 

State, and United States Minister Willis at Honolulu which, after the receipt of Mr. Willis's letter of October 20th, had been sent to the

Senate attached to a message from President Cleveland. In view of the later happenings in Hawaii these letters and the Admiral's letter to the Secretary of the Navy in regard to them seem of interest and are given here in full.

Sir: 

Mr. Gresham to Mr. Willis. "Department of State 

Washington, August 31, 1894

I have received your despatch of the 11th instant, reporting the arrival at Honolulu on the 4th of Mr. A. G. S. Hawes, British Commissioner and Consul-General, succeeding Major Wodehouse, lately Minister 

Resident. You also report the movements of the Philadelphia and Champion. 

A very remarkable dispatch from Rear Admiral Walker, dated "At sea, August 17th" and reporting events at Honolulu, up to the date of his departure on the 12th, has been communicated to me by the Secretary of the Navy for my information. I enclose a copy for your perusal. The apprehensions of the Admiral do not seem to have been shared by you sufficiently to warrant your reporting the petition for the retention of the Philadelphia, which was addressed to you as well as the naval commander, and it is trusted that your reported concurrance in that officer's views will be found due to some mis apprehension. 

Sir: 

I am, etc.

W. Q. Gresham" 

Mr. Willis to Mr. Gresham. "Legation of the United States 

Honolulu, H. I. September 29th, 1894 (Received October 20)

I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt of your instruction of the 31st ultimo, enclosing a dispatch from Rear Admiral Walker of August 17.

After a lengthy statement seriously reflecting upon the late British Minister and the commanding officer of the Champion and expressing

such apprehensions touching the political situation here as necessitated the retention of the Philadelphia here, the Admiral, in the eleventh paragraph, says: "Before reaching this conclusion (to postpone the 

Philadelphia's departure) I consulted with the United States Minister, whose views entirely coincided with my own." 

Your dispatch indicates that these words of the Admiral have been given a wider meaning than could have been intended by him. A brief receital of the facts may therefore be proper. 

On the 5th day of August I was informed by the Admiral that he would leave with the Philadelphia on the 8th. On the 6th the British 

Minister, Major Wodehouse, having been succeeded by Mr. Hawes, made his farewell official visit to this Legation, during which he mentioned the fact that the Champion had intended to leave on that day, but some of the English residents had become nervous when they heard that the Philadelphia also was to leave, and yielding to their request, the Champion would remain until the Hyacinth arrived. Upon the same morning I made a parting call to the officers of the Philadelphia, when the Admiral told me that several citizens had expressed their regret at the departure of the Philadelphia before the arrival of the Charleston, which vessel was daily expected. He also expressed some surprise at the change of plans of the Champion. In view of these facts, he asked my opinion in regard to the detention of the Philadelphia. I replied that as the steamer Australia was due here August 11th at 8 A.M. which would probably bring correct advices as to the Charleston's movements I saw no reason, if not in conflict with his orders (as to the nature of which I was not informed), why the Philadelphia should not wait until then.

When the Australia arrived, I immediately notified the Admiral that no news warranting his further stay had been received. He left the next morning.

It is this conversation and the unimportant result following it with which the Admiral connects me.

The statement as to the conduct and sentiments of the British Minister and Captain of the British cruiser, and especially as to their purpose to land troops for political advantage, and the dangers resulting therefrom to American interest, I cannot confirm either from personal knowledge or from reliable information. If called upon to express an opinion, I should be constrained to say that during the past six months, and especially since the proclamation of the Republic, the presence or absence of the British war vessels here ought to have been regarded with indifference by our Government, as it is now by this Government. 

The "Petition of citizens" referred to by the Admiral I did not receive until the day after our conversation. The conditions here for many months had been extremely orderly and peaceful. As stated by the petition itself, there was "no reason to doubt the ability of the Government of the Republic of Hawaii to support and sustain itself and furnish full protection to the lives and property of all residents in its territory." In this opinion even the enemies of the Government concurred. Furthermore on the day after receiving the petition, Mr. 

Hatch, the Minister of Foreign Affairs, expressed to me his regret that

it had been sent, as those he represented welcomed the opportunity to prove to the world that they were able to take care of themselves. 

These I deemed sufficient reason for not forwarding the petition. 

With renewed assurances, etc., Albert S. Willis" "The Secretary of the Navy

Sir: 

I yesterday obtained Senate Document No. 31 (53rd Congress 

3rd Session) containing a message from the President of the United States upon the subject of a cable to the Hawaiian Islands, to which message is appended certain letters and papers, and among the former a letter from the Secretary of State to the United States Minister at Honolulu, dated August 31st, 1894, and the latter's reply thereto, dated September 29th, 1894. This correspondence refers to my dispatch to you relative to the state of affairs in Hawaii, written at sea 

August 17th, 1894 immediately after my departure from Honolulu.

Mr. Gresham comments upon my dispatch as "very remarkable" and plainly intimates to Mr. Willis what reply upon his part will be acceptable to the Department of State. My conclusions and suggestions were evidently unpalatable to Mr. Gresham, and he lost no time in seeking to discredit their accuracy and value, and in indicating to Mr. 

Willis his expectation of the latter's assistance. 

No action of mine in connection with Hawaiian affairs has met so far as I am aware the slightest disapproval from either the Navy Department or the Department of State. Mr. Gresham's strictures related wholly and solely to my opinions formed from careful observations extending over a period of four months at Honolulu, during which time the Constitution was adopted, and the Republic was inaugurated and deliberately expressed after my departure from that port, in a dispatch which was intended to be a review of the leading features of the situation and to close the series of my letters. 

In view of Mr. Gresham's reflections upon this dispatch, which are not only of official records, but which have also become a matter of wide publicity - I deem it due to myself to set forth in an official communication the opportunities I enjoyed for forming those opinions and my reasons for believing those opportunities were superior in extent and reliability to those possessed by the American Minister. 

Upon my arrival at Honolulu I found Mr. Willis in a peculiar and unfortunate position. He had been accredited to the Provisional 

Government, had at once entered into consultations and negotiations with the dethroned queen, and after a prolonged period during which his attitude had given rise to the most intense anxiety and alarm on the part of the supporters of the Provisional Government and corresponding encouragement and hope to the royalists, he had made a demand upon the

Provisional Government to surrender its functions in favour of Liliuokalani, which demand was refused. These facts are now matters of history and I cite them for the sole purpose of making my statement clear and continuous. 

As a natural result there existed among the adherents of the new regime in Hawaii a strong feeling of suspicion and distrust towards Mr. Willis, because he had attempted to overthrow their government; and among the royalists a corresponding feeling of disappointment and resentment because he had failed to do so; and these influences, especially the first, tended to isolate Mr. Willis socially, to limit his official intercourse to the barest exigencies of the situation and to cut him off in great part from that daily mingling in the life and conversations of people, which especially during a time of rapid political development is a most valuable source of information. It is due to Mr. Willis to say that he bore the discomfort of this position with dignity and patience and that his subsequent attitude of impartiality, and the respect produced by a growing knowledge of his character were slowing having their effect in softening the antagonism caused by his efforts to restore the deposed queen. But his relations during my stay in Honolulu were not those which brought to him accurate and full information from all parties and all interests. 

My situation was entirely different. I went to Honolulu unprejudiced and uncommitted to any side or interest there. It was forty years since I had visited the islands or had any connection or association with them - a fact rather unusual in a long naval experience. As stated in my earlier dispatches I met and talked with all classes of the people, I entertained and was entertained by both republicans and royalists; - met them intimately in their homes and in the homes of others. One of my boyhood's friends was an official of the Provisional 

Government, another was a partisan and pronounced royalist. I spent some days on a plantation as the guest of one of the leading royalists; and I was consulted frequently and confidentially by representatives of both parties.

I also met the best representatives of a third class, the business men who took no leading part in active politics, but were keenly alive to all conditions affecting the financial and industrial prosperity of the islands. I attached great weight to the opinions of these men, uninfluenced by the rancor of political contest, or the ambitions for political preferment. I felt that they were of great value in indicating the policy best adapted for the efficient protection of American monied interests. 833 

Regarding the departure of the Philadelphia, one of these gentlemen, a banker and sugar planter, expressed to me his surprise that our government should leave the interests of Honolulu unprotected by a public vessel for the first time in seven years. He regarded it as most unwise as taking great risks - not only to American commercial interests but to American political influence. He said in substance: "I am a business man. My first duty is to protect my business interests. 

I prefer American protection. My business lies with the United States and American protection is better for me than British protection; but

protection of some kind I must have, and if the Philadelphia leaves I shall sign the petition for the retention of the Champion. I do not propose to risk any loss or confusion growing out of an attempt, successful or unsuccessful, to restore the queen." 

Such in brief were the facilities, often of daily occurrence, afforded me to arrive at opinions and conclusions respecting Hawaii's affairs and the status at Honolulu when the Philadelphia left on August 12th. I communicated them to the Navy Department as a matter of course. 

I should have failed in my duty and left it uncompleted if I had not done so.

I submit that the importance of these opinions and conclusions coupled with my rank and experience entitle them to respect and courtesy and should at least have protected them from prejudice and aspersions on the part of the Department of State. I arrived in Washington on 

August 30th, the day before Mr. Gresham's letter to Mr. Willis was written, I was at hand to give him the reasons for my conclusions, to explain anything that he found "very remarkable", to state at any length required, to the best of my ability, the condition of the Hawaiian Islands as I had found and left them; but no such demand was made, nor such opportunity offered. 

I have already been punished for expressing my opinions to the Navy Department through the usual channels of an official letter the one referred to above, dated August 17th last. The publication of Mr. Gresham's letter of August 31st was an additional injury to me. 

I submit with all respect that if objections were made or exception taken to my official conduct while in command of the Pacific Station, I was entitled to have the charges made in the usual form and to have the opportunity of meeting them in the usual manner. 

I have now to request that this letter may receive the publicity which has been given those of Mr. Gresham and Mr. Willis." 

Mr. Willis's optimistic view of the situation was not as firmly held by members of the new government. Chief Justice Judd wrote to Admiral Walker on October 4th, 1894 - "Our affairs are quiet here and an election takes place on the 29th inst. which will all go one way. I am a little disturbed by the evident sympathy of Captain Hawes, the new British Commissioner, with the royalists. He is doing a good deal in a social way for them. Since the Japanese victories over 

China the Japanese Consul General Fujii wants to insist more emphatically "on no discrimination" against their people. "I should hope that the United States may feel satisfied that the Republic of Hawaii is able to maintain itself against internal enemies, it will not abandon us altogether. The assertion by the 

U. S. Senate that it will consider foreign interference as an act unfriendly to the U. S. would imply that the U. S. should have a force here to back up that assertion."

Although Admiral Walker had no further official connection with Hawaiian affairs he naturally felt a deep interest in all that went on in the Islands and it seems well, before going on to tell of his later duties, to insert here some of the letters he received from

public men there. The supporters of the new government did not feel easy as to the attitude of the British even after the recognition of the Republic of Hawaii by all the powers. Mr. Judd wrote again from 

Honolulu under date of December 6th, 1894: "Since the recognition of our republic by all the powers one would suppose that the royalists would give up their plans. But rumours are still rife and I fear the state of uncertainty will continue. good many hope that a revolution will be started in order that it may be suppressed with bloodshed and thus put a quietus on what is very disturbing to our peace and to our commercial progress. The Hyacinth, which was to leave here for a cruise south yesterday, stays on - they say till Xmas. Some think that this arrangement was in consequence of an understanding between Capt. Folger of the Yorktown and Capt. May. 

I cannot vouch for it. We are, as you know, capable of managing the royalists but an American ship would be very welcome here and we hope the Bennington too may have orders for here. No one here knows what her orders are. I presume that the views of the Senate will determine whether we cede an uninhabited island to Gt. Britain.......We hope the opposition to Great Britain's acquisition of the island will spur Congress to appropriate money for an American cable. But we want a cable."

The outbreak of the rebellion stirred up by the ex-queen's followers came on January 6th and scattered fighting continued for a week. The rebels bought arms and ammunition in San Francisco, with money provided by young Rudolph Spreckels, and their secret landing in the islands was well planned and executed but the rising was doomed to failure from the first, the only question being how long it would take to crush the rebellion. The actual beginning of hostilities was premature, before the leaders were prepared to act, which undoubtedly saved many lives and much property loss. Several of the men holding important posts on the side of the rebels were British. A few more quotations from Chief Justice Judd's letters to Admiral Walker are interesting. He writes under date of January 11, 1895: "Dear Admiral.

The "rebels", royalists, precipitated the uprising as you see by the papers I send and as you already know by telegraph. The rebels, such as are not captured, are now fugitive in our mountains back of Honolulu dense tropical forests and it may be some time before we get or kill them all. Citizen guards nearly every decent white man (except the royalits who are in jail) are armed and we are equal to any emergency. 

But, if the attack, which was premature, and in which my dear nephew

Charlie Carter was killed, had not been made, the chances are that the rebels would have killed whites and burned houses for some time before we could (get?) our forces out to repel them. If they had been successful how would Cleveland and Gresham feel? But the sacrifice of 

Charlie's life saved ours. We are all well. Excuse brevity and haste. 

Yours sincerely, A. F. Judd." 

1 

"We are now glad there is no U. S. ship here but if the ship had been here the rebels might not have attempted the rebellion." 

Again on January 29th, 1895 Mr. Judd writes: "Dear Admiral:

I enclose an account of the royalist uprising printed

for private circulation only......The Australia left S.F. on the 21st inst. at 10 A. M. and reached here Saturday the 26th at 6 P. M. The 

Philadelphia started 24 hours before the Australia and has not reached here as yet. Probably the bad weather encountered by the Australia has detained her. We cannot conjecture what Americans the Philadelphia can protect in Honolulu in accordance with the orders issued to Admiral Beardslee, for about all the Americans here were "in it" - saving the U. S. Minister, wife and son and Ellis Mills. All your prophesies have proven true.

The decisions of the Court Martial have not been made public yet.

One thing I wish to add. If the U. S. Government ever have to account to Great Britain for your strictures on Major Wodehouse, we have complete proof of your statements from Liliuokalani's diary of 1894 which shows Wodehouse's frequent calls upon her and what advice he gave

Both Hatch and Armstrong returned here by the Australia. 

How could they stay away! We look for accounts of the discussion in 

Congress with greatest interest. "

The printed letter of January 24th referred to by Mr. Judd, gives a detailed account of the attempted rising and then continues: "The trial of the rebels by a military court and not by our civil courts is fully justified. It would be impossible to secure convictions by a native jury. The military court as the proper tribunal was universally approved. The trials are now proceeding in the "throne room" of the ex-palace. The conduct of the court is dignified and calm, and the prisoners are ably defended by counsel of their own selection." ex- 

On February 20th the same correspondent writes: "Armstrong has quite taken the wind out of my sails by sending you extensive tracts from Liliuokalani's diaries for 1893-1894. I made the discoveries but I confess to feeling rather mean while reading the private thoughts of Madam never intended for my eyes. I think a gentleman should be cautious how he exposes such memoranda. Still I presume the public exigency may justify the exposure. The diaries have been shown altogether too much to accord with my ideas of propriety. I doubt not you will hesitate before letting newspaper men get hold of the matter. 

P. S. I feel confident that there will be no capital punishment inflicted.

Mr. Armstrong, in a letter enclosing extracts from the exqueen's diaries, says that sentence of death had been passed on six of the ring-leaders; two had turned states evidence and there was strong probability of clemency being shown. The ex-queen was a white elephant on the hands of the government; they could not execute her and if imprisoned it was feared she would always arouse sympathy; 

Mr. Armstrong, however, felt that as she had no money the natives would soon give her up.

It is not worth while to quote many entries from Liliuokalani's diaries but a couple of extracts will show that Major Wodehouse was cer-

tainly not neutral. "June 5th, 1893. 9.30 A. M. Major Wodehouse called to say that he had heard that a republic was to be proclaimed to-day by the Provisional Government, but that it was not true - he had hoisted his flag but not in recognition of the replublic - it could not be except with the voice of the people. He wanted to know what I thought of it that I should not have any fears. I told him that my subjects had told me not to lay myself open to be shot, as Capt. Good said he would be the one to shoot me.......He said I am near by and so are my colleagues should you need assistance send to us and we will attend to it. 1 

September 23rd, 1893. Major Wodehouse came to say that he would advise me to go to Waikiki or some place out of town on Monday morning. He had seen many cases of liquor going to the Palance of the P. G. The soldiers might get drunk and rush over here and get into a fight, and no one knows what might happen to me. He would not advise me to go to his house. January, 1894. This A. M. I wrote a note (privately) to 

Mr. Wodehouse to explain more fully my opinion of R. Hind and Mr. Robinson........At Mr. Carter's (J.O.C.) suggestion I will appoint two men whom I know he will approve of the other will be Newman." 

Mr. Armstrong comments upon this entry that it "shows quite clearly that Wodehouse was taking a hand in the formation of a new government, in the event of restoration." With these notes in the possession of the Republic of Hawaii it could hardly be claimed that Admiral Walker's expressed opinion of Major Wodehouse was unfair. 

If Admiral Walker's expressed opinions were unpalatable to President Cleveland and Mr. Gresham, they were warmly approved by 

Americans who had any connection with or knowledge of the Sandwich Islands; one gentleman having business dealings with them said that to himself and others the attitude of the Administration seemed wholly incomprehensible, that the attempted rebellion appeared due entirely to the withdrawal of American men-of-war from Honolulu at a time which seemed to show that the Government of the United States would give no support moral or physical to the new republic; he was amazed that the opinions and advice of Admiral Walker, sent to Hawaii for the purpose of reporting on the situation as a man on whose ability and discretion the Government could rely had been ignored although events had shown how surprisingly correct and accurate his conclusions had been and how wise his advice. 

Admiral Walker was kept in doubt as to his next duty for nearly three months but at last in November his orders to Annapolis were definitely revoked, Secretary Herbert, a warm friend of the 

Admiral, sending the following letter with the orders. 

"PERSONAL 

Washington 

Nov. 6, 1894.

Rear Admiral J. G. Walker 

Dear Sir :

The same mail that brings you this will bring the revocation

of your orders to the Naval Academy. The President was very anxious to have a young man there and in obedience to this wish of his I detailed Captain Cooper, near the bottom of the list of Captains. 

If you would like to be President of the Examining Board until Admiral Greer retires from the Lighthouse Board I will be glad to put you there. 

Yours very truly 

H. A. Herbert" 

On November 12, 1894 he was ordered to the Naval Examining and Retiring Board and December 1st be became chairman of the Lighthouse Board; after the completion of this duty he served in 1895 and 

1896 on numerous boards, courts of enquiry etc. 

Before receiving his official orders for his next duty the following letter told him what to expect: "Letter of William McAdoo, Assistant Secretary of the Navy to

Admiral John G. Walker Navy Department 

August 14th, 1896 

My dear Admiral

Before the Secretary sailed for Europe he advised me that at his suggestion the President had selected you as the Naval Member of the Santa Monica Commission, but that you were on no account to tell anyone of the fact until after the whole commission had been selected and the public announcement made. Be pleased therefore to consider this matter strictly confidential. The Secretary also desired me to substitute Admiral Ramsay in your place on the Board to Consider Plans for Laying Ships in Reserve of this I will notify you officially. Congratulating you on your appointment and trusting that you are enjoying a pleasant vacation, I am, with best wishes, Very sincerely yours, (signed) W. McAdoo."

On November 4th, 1896 he was duly ordered to represent the Navy on a Board to select the location for a deep water harbor in southern California.

Sir: 

Washington, November 4, 1896

Pursuant to a clause contained in the act making appropriations for the construction, repair and preservation of certain public works on rivers and harbors, and for other purposes, which became a law June 3, 1896, making provision for a Board for the location of a deep harbor for commerce and of refuge in Santa Monica Bay or at San Pedro, California you are hereby detailed as the officer of the Navy to serve upon said Board, and to perform all duties as a member of the Board as required by the law above referred to. 

Very respectfully, W. McAdoo 

Acting Secretary

To Rear-Admiral John G. Walker, U. S. Navy, Chairman Light House Board, 

Washington, D. C."

He was made chairman of the Board, the other members being 

Col. Richard P. Morgan of Illinois; Augustus Rogers of the Coast and

Geodetic Survey; George S. Morrison, a civil engineer of Chicago and and Prof. William H. Burr of the Columbia College School 

New York; of Mines of New York.

On his way to California Admiral Walker went to Portland, Oregon, where he stayed with the Light House Inspector on the Columbine and looked into the matter of a light ship which was under construction, talked the matter over with all the interested parties and prepared a telegraphic report to Washington. From there he also wrote his wife on 

December 7th: "I received a telegram to-day from my friend Henry Scott in San Francisco, asking me to dine with him at the Pacific Union Club of which he is President (the swell club of S.F.) and offering to come up here with a special car to take myself and party to S.F.......It would make the trip (about forty hours) very pleasant but he has evidently planned a big dinner with a lot of prominent men to meet me, and under present conditions I would rather avoid it. I therefore telegraphed him that I hoped to leave here Wednesday evening, he can't reach here before Thursday morning, that I should stop in S.F. one day or two and during that time I wanted to go to Mare Island Yard, that I was alone, should make a special point of seeing him but that the dinner would have to be deferred to a more favorable opportunity. I hope that will stop him but it may not. He is a very good fellow, has a lot of money, would give me a supurb dinner with a lot of the first men in the city to meet me, but I don't know who is who on the harbor business and

I don't want to give the anti-Huntington newspapers - they are nearly all against H an opportunity to say that I was entertained by Mr. 

Huntington's friends etc......."

The Board made its headquarters in Los Angeles, holding public hearings in a room supplied by the Chamber of Commerce, and making trips to Santa Monica and San Pedro to study these two rival locations during which expenditions they worked from 10 A.M. to 6 P.M......From 

San Francisco he writes, "Mr. Hearst's San Francisco paper is already abusing us as creatures of Mr. Huntington. We are sure to catch it, no matter what we do, but I think the board will not be frightened by that sort of thing and will do exactly what it thinks right. The members seem to me strong men and absolutely honest.

There is a great deal of feeling in California over the question and of course much that is unreasonable" "We have had two days of public hearings and everything has gone smoothly and pleasantly.....The room is crowded with interested spectators. We sit as a court of five judges and, with the shorthand reporters in front of our platform at tables of their own, the display of plans and maps upon the walls and the speakers, we are quite imposing." "The San Francisco Examiner and 

Los Angeles Times are bitterly opposed to Huntington and the Southern Pacific R.R.- violent partisans of San Pedro as the site for the harbor, and inclined to abuse the members of the Board because of their fear that they may see something good in Santa Monica. I have never seen more indecent journalism in papers which stand, as they do, amongst the leading papers of the state. The Examiner belongs to Mr. Hearst." "Dec. 27. This morning Mr. Morrison and I went to the morning service at the Unitarian Church. Found a very large congregation, excellent music and a preacher rather worth hearing. I should say an 

Irishman or Scotchman, not highly educated but has read and studied a good deal and a man of original thought, a little sensational in his delivery and with a badly trained voice. He delivered a sermon an hour long and held his audience to the last word." 

Admiral Walker's duty as President of the Board to locate a deep water harbor on the Pacific Coast was completed in July, 1897. 

But in the meantime on the 20th of March, 1897, having reached the age of sixty-two Admiral Walker was placed on the retired list and looked forward to a life of leisure with his family, from which he had been absent so much. His friends hoped that he might write some account of his naval life which had been very full of interest and covered the period of change from the old wooden sailing ships of his youth to the modern steel men-of-war; but if the years before retirement had been full of work and interest those that followed were quite as full of interesting and important work. It would require too much space to include in this sketch the many letters of appreciation received by Admiral Walker at this time of his retirement but two or three will give an idea of the place he held in the minds and the hearts of those who had held positions under him or had worked with him and knew him well.

"Navy Yard, New York 21 March, 1897 

My dear Admiral

Your retirement is a matter of profound regret to me for my own sake and for the service. My own career has not been particularly short and there is no portion of it which gives me more satisfaction than my service with you in the Chicago. The winter we lay in Montevideo when for weeks war hung in the balance I knew the feeling in the fleet of profound conviction that if it were war we had an Admiral on whom we could contentedly stake our own honor and that of the flag. 

I cannot believe that in the full vigor of mind and body you will cease to take an active and important part in the affairs of the service and I hope that for many long years you will continue to do so. 

With greatest respect and regards 

Yours very sincerely 

C. S. Sherry."

From Captain Raymond Rogers, our Naval Attache at Paris and a distinguished officer of outstanding ability, whose father and uncle had also served the Navy with ability, came the following: 

"U. S. Embassy Paris 

11 March, 1897 

My dear Admiral

This will reach you about the 20th of March when, I regret to say, you will have reached your 62nd year and retire from active service. It would be superfluous for me to tell you how much I regret that the Navy is to lose you at its head for you know well how much I feel this. My service with you for seven continuous years attached me very warmly to you and was particularly agreeable and useful to me. 

When I was under you as Chief Intelligence Officer you always gave me much scope in the administration of my office and showed me the greatest consideration, and as Navigator of your Flagship I had the great advantage of gaining an experience and of serving in a capacity of more than ordinary value. 

And in your retirement I feel that I have lost my best friend, perhaps my only friend, among the Superior Officers of the Navy. 

I hope you will find occupation which will be of interest and that your years of retirement may be as agreeable to yourself as your

active service has been of value to the service and to the country 

I am, Admiral, always 

Yours very sincerely, Raymond Rogers"

The next letter is from an old friend and contemporary in the service who had known Admiral Walker from the beginning of his naval career. 

"U. S. Naval Observatory 

Georgetown Heights 

Washington, D. C. 

March 11, 1897 

My dear Walker

Let me express my regrets that, by the operation of law, you are to be removed from your present field of usefulness, and also the hope that by your retirement, the service may not altogether lose your assistance in promoting its welfare. 

The feeling among the officers of the Navy in favor of your selection for the position of Assistant Secretary of the Navy is wider and stronger thant you may believe it to be 

Very sincerely yours

R. L. Phythian" 

From the Naval War College came a letter from the Commandant which with one from a man "of other ranks" will end this selection of letters. 

My dear Admiral 

"Naval War College 

Newport, R. I. 

March 20, 1897

It is no exaggeration of style that I state the solemn fact that in your retirement today the Navy loses the best man it has at, or anywhere near, the head of the list. If there are as good men out-

side that limit they are as yet unproved and unknown. 

I don't deny that I am glad to get an additional step up the ladder but I am sincerely sorry that it should be at so great a cost. 

There are so many others who "Never will be missed" it seems a shame that they should hang on superfluous.

My object in writing you this morning is to tell you of my regrets as well as to wish you a long life and an unabated interest in the service to which you have been such an honor. 

May all good things go with you. 

Yours very sincerely 

C. F. Goodrich." 

"U.S.S. Monadnock, 2nd Rate 

San Diego, California 

Rear Admiral John G. Walker, U.S.N. Sir: 

Washington, D. C. 

March 14th, 1897

I saw by the Army and Navy Journal that you are about to be retired and thought I would write and tell you that I am very sorry, that I had rather far you were going to sea in command of the finest fleet this country could bring together and I once more on board the flagship as one of your quartermasters. The most sorrowful day I ever put in in the service was the one on the Chicago when I hauled down the flag which you carried with you as a memento of the finest ship then in service. Hoping you will pardon the liberty I take and assuring you that you will always be gratefully remembered by all your old shipmates before the mast, I am 

Very respectfully 

Antonio Mason 

Quartermaster 1st Class, U.S.N."

Even before the actual date of Admiral Walker's retirement, as soon as it was widely known that he would soon be free from the constant work which had been his portion for so long, various attempts were made to secure his well known ability in other spheres. Gen. Francis A. 

Walker, the president of the Massachusetts Institute of Technology, died

January 5th, 1897 and at once Admiral Walker's name was suggested for the position as it was desired "to get a prominent man of national reputation.......and not a teacher or pedagogue"; this scheme came to nothing, perhaps on account of Admiral Walker's age, which would probably not allow a long incumbency. More in line with his life long interests was a request from Captain Goodrich in command of the Naval War College at Newport, portions of whose letter follow: "Naval War College, Newport, R.I. 

March 4, 1897. 

My dear Admiral:

You have been so intimately associated with the Naval War College from the first inception of its underlying idea; with its foundation, due to your official efforts, and its varied and ofttimes precarious existence; to the present day when, thanks to the persistent energy of Capt. Taylor, it seems to be an assured fact; that I am anxious to maintain your deep personal interest as well as to enlist your support in widening its sphere of usefulness. 

It will not be long, so I understand, before the natural operations of law will put you in possession of an unaccustomed leisure. I may say for myself, and for a large body of officers, that no one ever went on the retired list leaving so large a gap behind and with the regrets of so many of the Navy's best members as yourself. 

Will you not take into prayerful consideration this proposition namely, that you prepare a paper, as long or as short as you like, to be read by you here, before the class of officers next summer? I leave the choice of subject entirely to yourself, but as, in cases of this kind, a suggestion is sometimes helpful, I mention as a topic which I know your study and experience will enable you to treat with authority, that of battle tactics, including for example, such minor considerations as the proper composition of the fleet, the best tactical unit, the position of the flag officer in action, how close can and should ships manoeuver in battle, what formations appear to be most advantageous for attack and defense, how fast can signals be made and how far seen in battle, &, &. A paper dealing with one or more of these topics or kindred themes coming from your pen would be of great value in itself as well as of great weight in ridding the college of the reproach that its lecturers were born yesterday........

The proposed paper was never read for on June 4th of that year Congress authorized the President to appoint a Nicaragua Canal Commission "to continue the surveys and examinations authorized by the Act approved March 2, 1895......into the proper route, the feasibility and cost of construction of the Nicaragua Canal" and on July 29th, 1897

the President appointed Admiral John G. Walker, U.S.N., Captain Oberlin M. Carter, Corps. of Engineers, U.S.A., and Prof. Lewis M. Haupt, Civil 

Engineer, as members of that Commission, Admiral Walker to be President and Capt. Carter its disbursing officer; the last named, however, was relieved from this duty on Oct. 18th and was succeeded by Col. Peter C. 

Hains. Both as a sailor and as a military man Admiral Walker had long been deeply interested in the question of an inter-oceanic canal across the Central American isthmus, feeling that in times of peace it would be very valuable commercially while in case of war its importance in enabling the government to transport ships and troops rapidly from one coast of the United States to the other could hardly be exaggerated. 

It also became increasingly evident that if the United States neglected to build and control such an important waterway some other nation would certainly do so.

The idea of a canal across Central America to connect the Atlantic and Pacific oceans was not a new one, the Nicaragua route had been reported upon favorably in the days of Spanish rule while from 1815 to 1865 there were various plans to carry out such a project by private companies under government sanction, in one of which Cornelius Vanderbilt was interested. At last, after the close of the Civil War, the government awoke to the great desirability of such a waterway. 

This is not the place to speak in detail of the various expeditions sent out by this country to investigate and report upon the various trans-isthmian routes but it is interesting to note that all these investigations were carried out by naval officers, Capt. Shufeldt, Commander Selfridge, Commander Lull and Civil Engineer Menocal, U.S.N. 

leading expeditions to survey and study various proposed routes. 

Few people realize the work done by the Navy in times of peace; they look upon it as a fighting machine and would be greatly surprised to learn of a naval officer's varied peace-time duties, which have, in the past at least, made the career an interesting and broadening one.

The law under which the Nicaragua Canal Commission was appointed empowered it to make all surveys and examinations into the proper route for a Nicaraguan canal, its feasibility and cost of construction "with a view to making a complete plan for the entire work of construction". This meant a great deal of preliminary study and work, besides the organization of the expedition itself, with its engineering and commissary departments; all the reports of government surveys as well as those of private parties must be carefully studied and information obtained from members of former expeditions as to personal as well as government matters. For instance the following outfit was recommended for the members of the commission: "Wear in town the same kind of underclothing as in Washington in summer, with light flannel or serge clothing. In the woods wear light weight woolen drawers and navy blue flannel shirt with no undershirt. I prefer duck shooting pants to woolen as they do not catch the bush, do not absorb so much water and dry sooner.....We ar Thompson's 

Hunting Shoes. Do not attempt to wear light shoes in the woods. They soon become so soft that thorns will go through them and pierce the

feet. In the woods always wear leggings. Canvas are the best. For a hat wear something light which can be wrung out when wet. Canvas or light woolen with a cloth sweat band. Sleep in light flannel pajamas.

Get all flannel goods large as they will shrink. 

The bedding of each person should consist of: 

2 woolen blankets 

2 rubber blankets, 42" by 84" & 42" by 72" 

1 rubber pillow with 3 pillow cases 1 mosquito net, 7' by 3' by high, with cod line sewed around the top and loops at the corners. Also 6' of line for each corner. Have no opening in side. The net should be made of cheesecloth or light lawn. 1 rubber coat 

2 umbrellas 

1 pair light rubber boots 

Needles, thread, buttons, safety pins, etc. 

Water tight match safe for pocket 

1 piece of oil silk for protecting paper money, etc. 

1 soap box

Always have dry clothes and a pair of light shoes to put on in camp.

When the preliminary studies were ended and the or anization completed the expedition sailed from New York on Dec. 5th, 1897, nearly one hundred strong and reached Greytown on the 17th. 

A letter from Admiral Walker to Senator Allison gives some idea of the first difficulties encountered. "San Juan del Norte, Nicaragua 

December 19, 1897 

My dear Mr. Allison: 

The Nicaragua Canal Commission, with its staff, instruments and outfit, arrived off this port Friday noon. The landing has to be

made through the surf, is frequently difficult and dangerous, sometimes impossible; but we succeeded in getting ashore yesterday without any accident. Shall now push our parties into the woods as fast as possible. I shall write to the Secretary of State recommending that an additional appropriation be made for the work of the Commission. We have fitted ourselves very thoroughly and completely with the idea of making this examination a complete and final one; but work of this nature in this country is carried on under many difficulties and at great expense. All our white men have to be brought from the United States and have to be fed and supplied from New York. 

Transportation is expensive and, in fact, everything in this country is expensive. Now that we are here with our outfit of instruments, tools and supplies of all kinds, it would be very bad administration to have to suspend work because of the lack of money, and it is impossible for anyone to tell in advance what our expenses may be. I shall recommend an additional appropriation of probably one hundred thousand dollars. No more money will be spent than is necessary, but while we are down here and ready to do this work it should be done in the most careful and thorough manner, that the whole question of the building of the Nicaraguan Canal may be definitely settled. I hope you will advocate an appropriation and put it in our power to close up this whole subject at once and for all time. 

Very sincerely yours, J. G. Walker "

On January 2nd, 1898 he writes to a friend "The Commission landed here two weeks ago yesterday. It has rained most of the time and at times very heavily. We are now in the midst of our second "norther", but hope it will let up on us in a day or two so that we can get 37 laborers ashore from the Newport. She arrived with them from Port Limon yesterday morning. I have found a good deal of difficulty in getting laborers as few were to be had here, and without the services of the Newport I should have been helpless. She has made two trips to Limon, bringing the first time seventy laborers and this time thirty-seven. As soon as we can get these men ashore and distributed the Commission will go up the river and go on to Managua to pay its respects to the President of the Republic. 

I have put all our parties, eleven in number, into the field and although they are somewhat shorthanded in the matter of laborers, still they are beginning their work and making progress. 

I hope we shall begin getting results soon. We are completely in the hands of the Atlas Line from New York and the Navigation Company here, for all our transportation and they stick it to us pretty severely, so that everything is extremely expensive.... ..

The Commission and the men we have brought with us from the United States are all in earnest, willing to work and desirous of making a complete, successful and final examination of the canal route .......This is a manana country where nothing is done today that can be put off until tomorrow, but we are getting on and I hope for the best."

On the 25th of that month he writes from Rivas on Lake Nicaragua to the U. S. Minister in Costa Rica, "The members of this Commission are now here for an examination of the Canal line from the lake to Brito, the visit to Managua having been completed. We expect to complete our work on this side of the lake and to leave for the San Juan River February 5th. We shall walk from Ochoa to Greytown where we expect to arrive between the 20th and 25th."

The Commission planned to go from there to Port Limon and hoped to pay their respects to the President of Costa Rica and then on to Colon to go over the Panama Route. 

One result of the careful study of this route was the discovery that the maps of the coast line of Lake Nicaragua, a body of water 100 miles long, were quite incorrect, the difference in some places being as much as five miles, and that many of the charted positions of the islands at the eastern end were very much in error. 

The return to the eastern coast was delayed a week by an attempted revolution and at the end of that time the Commission only got across the lake with its personal baggage, by special order of the President, on a steamer full of troops. The work of the field parties was hampered and delayed. For instance, the chief of the party on the west side of the lake sent in a requisition early in January for provisions, stores and some small articles of outfit and on February 24th these supplies were lying at San Carlos at the eastern end of the lake with no prospect of their being sent on to their destination. The government had seized all the steamers for military purposes and no freight was forwarded. Col. Hains and Professor Haupt of the Commission left for the United States on March 20th and Admiral Walker followed on the 25th.

War with Spain was threatening and upon Admiral Walker's arrival in Washington he received many letters from men wanting to serve in the Navy in case of war; although he was now on the retired list his influence in Naval affairs had been so well known that it was natural for men to ask his help in obtaining appointments. 

Early in 1898 friction between Spain and the United States caused by Cuban affairs became acute but long before that it had been felt that there was great danger of war with that country over her treatment of Cuba, and Commodore Dewey, an old friend who had asked for sea service, was disturbed by hearing that he was to be ordered to command the Asiatic Station; he wanted to do his share if war came and thought that these orders would take him far from the field of action and that he would have no chance to use his training and knowledge in the service of his country. In talking with Admiral Walker one day he asked him to use his influence to have his orders changed and was quite surprised to find that the Admiral thought that the Asiatic post was the best possible one for an able man in case war Came Admiral Walker pointed out that the squadron in the water of the West Indies was too near home for independence of action, the commanding officer would constantly receive orders from Washington and might find it hard to carry out his own plans. He told Dewey that if

he were in command of the Squadron in Asia, as soon as war was declared he would at once sail for the Phillipines, a Spanish possession, and cut the cable. Commander Dewey saw the point and the world knows the result. 

War was declared in April and during this short struggle Admiral Walker was kept in Washington where he met with the War Board to consider plans for the campaign and was held in readiness for active duty in case Admiral Sampson's health, never very robust, gave out.
