There had been a strong movement to have Admiral Walker recalled to active service and placed in command of the Naval forces but he felt, as the Department did, that Admiral Sampson should have his chance first.

When Admiral Walker began to look into the questions of plans for carrying on the war Admiral Cervera's fleet had sailed from Spain and its whereabouts in the Atlantic Ocean was unknown and there was much uneasiness along the Atlantic seaboard as to its goal. Many people moved their valuables from the cities near the coast into the interior, fearing the sudden appearance of the powerful Spanish battleships and the destruction of the large cities by gun fire. The first question that Admiral 

Walker asked was what agents the Navy or the Government had in the West Indies to secure and send to Washington the earliest possible information in regard to the missing fleet and any discoverable news of the plans of Spain. The Government seemed quite satisfied to rely upon information sent by its accredited agents, consular and diplomatic, and officials seemed surprised when it was pointed out to them that Spain would certainly make every effort to prevent important news from reaching the known agents of her opponents and that some less conspicuous and quite innocent appearing correspondents must be arranged for if early information in regard to military matters was wanted. 

In spite of the terror in parts of the United States the war proved to be an opera bouffe affair and at the end of July, 1898 Spain sued for peace.

There was one very unfortunate result of the war, the socalled Sampson-Schley affair, arising from the claim that Admiral Schley was the victor of the battle of Santiago when the Spanish Admiral Cervera was defeated, Admiral Sampson being, at the time the Spanish ships came out of the harbor, on his way to a conference with General Shafter and his flagship, the New York, four miles east of the blockading squadron. 

Admiral Walker received some very interesting letters from an officer on the New York written at the time, parts of which it seems worthwhile to include here as they undoubtedly helped to form his opinion often expressed to friends during the long controversy which broke Admiral Sampson's heart and shortened his life. The letters, which follow, were of course strictly confidential. 

STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL U.S.S. New York Off Santiago de Cuba 

June 26, 1898 

My dear Admiral:

I am sure you will be glad to hear some thing of the war, out-

side of press reports, which are mainly inventions and lies of an utterly baseless character. It has been on my mind a dozen times to write you, but I really have not found the time. I hardly know how it goes, but I seem to get rid of about fourteen hours a day, and the rest I need for eating and sleeping. 

You made no mistake in your estimate of Schley. He has proved himself totally wanting, and is today a discredited man in this fleet. 

I think he is ignorant, incapable, and that he was disgruntled at being placed under Sampson's command. He seemed to have no conception of the importance of keeping Cervera shut up here, and it took every despatch boat and cable to get him here; and then he did his best to abandon the place. Cervera made Santiago on May 19 and he was free to get out any time during the next eight days. I think the real reason that he did not will be found to have been that his ships were not in a condition to go to sea and meet the enemy. They are rated, you know, as 20 knot ships.

They made, from Cape Verdi to Curacoa, only 200 miles a day. To that fact alone I did not attribute much importance, as their bottoms were undoubtedly foul and economy of coal was an object; but from Curacoa to this place they made no better speed; and that was significant, as every strategic consideration demanded that, having decided upon their 

Cuban port, they should make a dash for it.

To return to Schley: We learned definitely on our way back from San Juan that Cervera had left Curacoa on the 15th of May. We also heard from the Department that he had important munitions of war for Havana and that he was ordered, at all hazards, to reach Havana or some port in rail communication with Havana; and for deep draft ships 

Cienfuegos is the only port on the south coast that meets this condition. 

We left the Squadron, hurried forward, and reached Key West on the 18th. Everything that had been east of San Juan was short of coal. Found Schley at Key West coaling with the Brooklyn, Massachusetts, Texas and Scorpion. Sampson's Squadron followed the New York rapidly in and all began to coal. Everything had been ordered in from the Cienfuegos blockade to Key West. Watson was holding on at Havana with a few light vessels expecting to be "eaten up" if Cervera came his way.

On the 19th Schley, practically full of coal, was sent with the Brooklyn, Massachusetts, Texas and Scorpion, as powerful auxiliary, to blockade Cienfuegos, and if Cervera had arrived to keep him in there. 

On the same day we heard from an unofficial source, to which at first not much credence was given, but which afterwards proved to be very reliable, that Cervera had reached Santiago. On the 20th the Iowa was sent to reinforce Schley, the Castine conveying the Merrimac with 4500 tons of coal was also sent and on the 21st the Marblehead, Eagle and Vixen were despatched to him. Orders were sent by the Marblehead to 

Schley directing him to ascertain if the Spanish squadron was at Cienfuegos and, if not, to push on to Santiago. Early on the morning of the 21st we went in the New York to the Havana blockade. We had heard again from our underground friend and with a growing conviction that the Spanish squadron was at Santiago, Sampson sent the Hawk, a converted yacht, to Schley with a duplicate of the despatch sent on board the Marblehead, and with an additional memorandum urging upon Schley the

importance of getting speedily to that point. Schley arrived off 

Cienfuegos on the morning of the 21st, was joined by the Iowa and Gastine and collier on the 22nd and by the Marblehead, Vixen and Eagle on the 23rd. He sent her back at once with an airy despatch saying that he had "seen smoke" and "heard guns" and was satisfied the Spanish vessels were in Cienfuegos, and should remain there. 

This of course did not reach us for several days. At the same time 

Schley sent fool telegrams to the Navy Department about the proper equipment of colliers "fool" I mean at such a time. RBO In the meantime Sampson found out definitely that Cervera was at Santiago, and fearing an attempt to come to Havana by the north coast of Cuba he collected the monitors and everything that could fight and cruised as far east as the old Bahama Channel for several days, and sent another despatch vessel to Schley. 

Schley did finally make up his mind that he had better move and did start east on the evening of the 24th, but sent another despatch to say that he could not blockade Cervera at Santiago as he must go somewhere to coal, he having certainly not less than ten or twelve days coal in his battle ship at the time. Sampson sent Folger in the New Orleans to order him peremptorily to hold the Spanish Admiral blockaded at Santiago at all hazards, and the Department which had "caught on" began to go for him. 

Well the outcome was this, Schley arrived off Santiago on the 26th of May, ascertained that the enemy was there either then or on the following day, and on the 27th started west, and signalled to the squadron that he was going to Key West to coal, and telegraphed the Department to the same effect. This you will think incredible, but these are the simple official facts. Fortunately the Harvard caught him with a peremptory telegram and he turned back; but even after that he had his whole squadron at one time 25 miles from port. 

Sampson had given Schley his best battleship and best cruiser and as soon as it became clear that he could not be depended upon in any way, Sampson came around here with the New York, Oregon and Mayflower at a speed of 13 knots and took charge. On the 31st the day before we came, after much urging by his officers, he (Schley) went in "to destroy" the Christobel Colon and Maria Teresa which were in plain sight in the upper part of the channel 1000 yards above the entrance. He bombarded at ranges of from 9,000 to 11,000 yards, steaming rapidly past the mouth of the harbor as he did so; and as soon as a shot fell in his vicinity, hauled out.

That is Schley's record in this war, and the people under his command have used language about him that I can't repeat. 

We are holding Cervera tightly in Santiago and the army is here and landed. We keep an absolute blockade and keep the searchlight of a battle ship on the narrow entrance all night long with launches and light vessels close up to act as pickets. Although we are constantly at night within range of their batteries, they never fire on the large ships, and only occasionally with musketry on the picket boats. We have bombarded them twice and have always steamed

deliberately into position and opened the ball.......Admiral Sampson is a strong man and doesn't worry......I think he has the full confidence of this squadron. I think he likes to fight and he is quite indifferent to his personal safety. I actually took hold of him during the last bombardment of this place to keep him behind the conning tower. He wants to stay up on the flying bridge. Of course we aren't hit once a month but no one knows when the monthly shell will arrive. 

The Army began landing on the 22nd - a part landed at Daiquiri, 12 miles east of Santiago and the remainder (by far the largest part) at Siboney, about 7 miles east......That whole army with its animals, food, ammunition, and impedimenta, has been put ashore under unfavorable circumstances without any opposition. The Spaniards beat the devil they do indeed. The small detachment in the block house fled towards Santiago with no delay. And they are not cowards, not by a d--d sight - the man who thinks they won't fight has surprises in store, but they are as unprogressive as the Chinese.....Goodrich had charge of the disembarkation and managed it well. Shafter thanked the Admiral and said he was much indebted to the Navy ......I hoped you would be in this business. I often think of you, and of the clear grasp of all problems here presented which you would have 

On July 29th the same correspondent wrote: 

"My dear Admiral: The outrageous newspaper lies about Sampson and

Schley seemed to me to have passed the limit of patience the other day and I wrote a personal letter to Henry Nelson telling him some truths about the Santiago Campaign for his own knowledge and that he might write intelligently upon the subject if he wrote at all. As I took some pains with the letter, I had a number of copies made and send one to you.......I do not doubt that you understood the situation pretty well, but will be glad to get facts and incidents. Schley thought he saw his chance and he at once began to claim that he and the Brooklyn had done it all. He has a little snipe of an Associated Press Correspondent in the Brooklyn whom he has made his man and evidently he had other alliances in the various press boats which are always with the fleet and as soon as they could get their telegrams in the newspaper crusade began. The New York Journal led in the business, Sampson having reported some time since one of its men to the Navy Department for telegraphing the secret movements of the fleet. 

At Santiago Schley brought over a day or two after the fight a "preliminary report" in which he made the assumption evident that he was in command at the time. Chadwick and I urged the Admiral to nip that sort of thing off at the beginning; we said it was unjust to him and unjust to history, and that it should not be allowed to go on file, and he sent for Schley and made him withdraw it. This interview with the C in C and the growing indignation in the fleet, which he was quick enough to see caused Schley to completely change his demeanor........ 

Schley exercised no control of the fleet during the action. Each vessel was handled at her captain's discretion. It was in fact a Captain's fight on lines which had been previously thoroughly considered. 

There was no room for tactics, and no demand for them. The Brooklyn simply took the part of a single ship. Nor was she well handled in this role. She was lying four or five miles west and south of the port.

When the Spanish vessels appeared, she, in common with the other ships headed directly in for the harbor entrance: i.e. for the escaping ships, for her a N. E. course. But when they turned west and their formation and direction became defined instead of starboarding her helm, turning to the north and then to the west towards them and closing - which a real fighter would have done - Schley ported his helm and made a complete circle to the east and southeast away from the enemy and away from the direction of the chase, and thereby risking collision with the other U. S. vessels all steaming as hard as they could to the N'd and W'd towards the Spaniards. She remained throughout the chase at a very respectable distance from the enemy's ships, and her 8" shot were falling short of the Christobal Colon when the latter surrendered to the Oregon."

The letter to Henry Nelson is much too long to include here but a portion of it should be quoted to complete the record. "In the fight itself the New York was, much to our regret, left in the rear. 

The Admiral in the execution of his duties had gone towards Siboney, directing all the other vessels to hold their positions. He was on his way to hold a conference with General Shafter, and when the Spanish flagship appeared in the channel the New York was seven miles east of the Morro and four miles east of her blockading station; and although she turned back instantly and increased her speed as rapidly as possible she could not get within range of the large Spanish ships before they were beached. She joined, however, in the chase of the Christobal 

Colon; and getting steam on all her boilers was gaining rapidly on that vessel and was only eight or ten miles from her when she surrendered. The flagship was therefore present, although not within range, and the whole fight and chase was under the eye of the Commander-in- Chief. Nor was the New York entirely out of the fighting, as she ran the fire of the Morro batteries which were doing all they could to aid the escape of the Spanish ships.

The Spaniards came out in column and turned to the West..... 

This removed all tactical difficulties and made the task of each vessel a perfectly plain one to close in, fight, and chase. This situation had been laid down by the Admiral in previous instructions and the vessels needed no orders.......Schley did not take the initiative nor did he exercise le adership."

In face of these facts it seems extraordinary and tragic that Schley could have been advanced six numbers and made Rear Admiral for "eminent and conspicuous conduct in battle" while the recommendation of the Navy Department that Sampson be promoted eight numbers and over the head of Schley, failed of confirmation by Congress." 

Before leaving the subject of the Spanish War a quotation from a letter of Admiral Walker's, although written much later, in December 1901, after a Court of Inquiry had found Schley guilty of delay in locating Cervera's fleet, of carelessness in endangering the Texas by a peculiar "loop" movement of the Brooklyn and of disobedience to a departmental order, would seem of interest. Writing to a friend in Iowa he says: "I do not think there is any way in which the people of the country can be made to see the real truth of the Schley matter. It was

first taken up by certain newspaper men in an attempt to "get even" with Sampson, who would give them no information. It should have been stamped out in the beginning and I strongly urged the Secretary of the Navy more than three years ago to order a court of retired officers to investigate the whole conduct of the Spanish War in the West Indies and settle the question once and forever. If he had done so the truth would have been known and all this scandal avoided. I think the Secretary was inclined to follow my advice, but that he was held back by the President. There had been a frightful army scandal; the Navy had done well and Mr. McKinley was very desirous of avoiding a Navy scandal and thought that if the matter was let alone and allowed to drop it would die out in a short time. He did not understand the situation and methods underlying the business and could not be made to see them. 

There is politics in the Schley business, but I think the verdict of the court will gradually tell upon the people and that the matter will die out after a bit. 

Miles is more different kinds of a fool than any man living although Dewey seems to be a close second. Dewey is a galant fellow and a very nice fellow in many ways, but he is entirely without judicial sense, is impulsive, is inclined to jealousy, and is easily influenced by people about him, as in his absurd candidacy for the presidency. I think the McLean surroundings have done him great harm. 

The Democrats are in desperate straits for a popular candidate for the presidency and a section of them are inclined to boom Schley, thinking that he can be made popular enough to carry the election and that he is weak enough to be manipulated in the interests of the party. 

You have probably seen that Roosevelt gave Miles a severe taking down in person at the White House, but there is one thing about that incident which is not generally understood. Miles went to the 

White House to talk with the President about his reprimand from the Secretary of War. When the President saw him in the reception room, he asked him to wait for a few minutes and he would talk with him in a private room, but Miles very foolishly insisted upon talking to him in the reception room, in which there were a considerable number of people waiting to speak with the President, and the result was that the wigging he got was a public one. Roosevelt is a man who does not hesitate to hit out on occasions, and when he does he hits hard. Miles brought the whole thing upon himself and that fact should be more generally understood."

The work of the Nicaragua Canal Commission continued: In 

October, 1898 the members inspected the Chicago Drainage Canal and studied the canal and locks at Sault Sainte Marie. All these details were interesting and kept the Commission busy and in addition the members worked hard on their report which they hoped to have ready for the meeting of Congress in December.

Several magazines urged Admiral Walker to write an article upon canal matters but his position on the Commission made that improper

at that time even if he could have given the necessary time to it. 

In addition to working on the report of the Commission Admiral Walker had long talks with a number of Senators and Representatives explaining his views about the canal plan. "I tell all the people with whom I talk that I have no interest in any company or in any one particular plan, but that having been connected with this Commission so long, I know that it is feasible to build a canal, and I believe it can be done at a reasonable cost, and that I am only interested in seeing the canal built without regard to who builds it. " 

On March 4th, 1899 he sent his congratulations to Admiral

Dewey upon his confirmation as full Admiral. The letter follows: 

"My dear Dewey: I send you my heartiest congratulations upon your promotion to the grade of Admiral and also that you have nothing upon your hands in the way of a Sampson and Schley scandal. Your promotion has universal support and I have yet to see a man who does not approve of it most heartily. This is written just as Congress is adjourning and a more reckless, unpatriotic and indecent Congress never went out of existence. I am still pegging away at the Isthmian Canal question but as the President signed the River and Harbor Bill this morning, carrying an appropriation for further investigation, I am just now somewhat in the dark as to the future.........With best wishes 

Very sincerely your friend, J. G. Walker." 

To a friend in Iowa, that same month ".......I am pegging away at Nicaragua Canal matters, with some probability that I may have the problem of the whole American Isthmus to deal with. You know the 

River and Harbor Bill which was approved March 3rd appropriated one million dollars, and directs the President to have the whole Central American Isthmus examined, to enter into diplomatic relations with the proper Governments, and to look up the whole subject of an Isthmian canal and report to Congress. It is a pretty big bite and will require some time and a good deal of effort to masticate it properly." 

After the passing of the River and Harbor Bill President McKinley sent for Admiral Walker and had a long talk with him; it was decided that the three members of the Nicaragua Canal Commission, Admiral Walker, Colonel Hains and Professor Haupt should form the nucleus of the new commission and two or more new men added. "He has to some extent put the matter into my hands. I suggested that one engineer from the Army and one from civil life be added to the present Commission, if it is to be continued as a part of the new one, and urged strongly that they shall be the best men obtainable. " 

The Commission as finally appointed in June, 1899, under the title Isthmian Canal Commission, was larger than Admiral Walker thought wise; the engineers from civil life were men of large practices whom it would be hard to get together quickly when a decision on some point was needed and the work of the Commission would necessarily be slowed up.

The members of the Commission were:

Rear-Admiral John G. Walker, U.S.N. President Honorable Samuel Pasco 

Alfred Noble, C. E. 

Mr. George S. Morison, C. E.

Col. Peter C. Hains, U.S.A. 

Mr. William H. Burr

Lieutenant-Colonel Oswald H. Ernst, U.S.A. 

Lewis M. Haupt, C.E. 

Professor Emory R. Johnson.

The Commission began work at once and organized parties to go to the Isthmus and start the investigation of the various routes; unfortunately there was a bad outbreak of yellow fever at Panama which made it undesirable to send a party there until fall. 

On August 9th five of the Commissioners, Admiral Walker,

Colonel Hains and Messrs. Noble, Morison and Burr sailed from New York for Europe, Colonel Ernst having preceded them a week earlier, to study the affairs of the French Canal Company, maps, profiles, plans and data of all kinds which were in Paris. 

In addition to the careful going over of the French Canal records which took most of the two months they spent in Europe the Commissioners took advantage of being there to visit and study the German, Dutch and English ship canals, as they wished to be thoroughly conversant with the latest developments in canal construction, and also gave a day to an inspection of the Liverpool docks. 

In October, after his return from Europe Admiral Walker was surprised to receive the following telegram from the New York World, "Could you consider proposal to go to the Philippines for three months to write the truth about the situation there. It would be a real service to the American people because they would have faith in your unprejudiced judgment and of course the World would gladly offer you your own terms. Great importance of the Philippine problem to the 

Nation our excuse for making such a request of you. One of the editors of the World will go to Washington to see you if you can consider the proposal. Kindly telegraph answer to Bradford Merrill, Managing Editor, Marked Personal."

Flattering as this offer was it was, of course, impossible to even consider it as all his time for many months would be taken up by his work on the Canal Commission but the suggestion shows the esteem

in which his opinion was held and the widespread confidence in his judgment and wisdom. Another letter written at this time refers to another request for an article, this time upon a Naval matter. "Washington, D. C.

July 3, 1899 

H. M. Alden, Esquire Editorial Rooms, Messrs. Harper and Brothers 

Franklin Square, New York City, N. Y. 

My dear Mr. Alden:

Your note of June 27, with regard to an article with illustrations, showing what has been done in submarine naval warfare, was received two or three days since.

I suppose you refer particularly to submarine torpedo boats. 

It is quite out of the question for me to write such an article, as I have no hesitation in saying that I am not sufficiently well informed on the subject. Perhaps the best man in the Navy to prepare such an article is Lieutenant-Commander William W. Kimble, who is an enthusiast with regard to submarine torpedo boats. But I think it very doubtful if you can get such an article as you want. 

Little has been accomplished in that direction as yet. The boats built in this country, up to this time, are entirely inefficient and I have no reason to believe that anything much better has been produced in Europe. I have grave doubts whether we are likely to see anything at present in the way of a submarine torpedo boat which can be called efficient that is, dangerous to the enemy. 

Very sincerely yours, J. G. Walker."

Nicaraguans were much disappointed to have the decision in regard to a canal through their country postponed for further study by the new Isthmian Canal Commission; they felt that the report of the Nicaragua Canal Commission showed that the proposed route was practicable and that the work could be completed for a reasonable sum. On November 16, 1899 Admiral Walker wrote to William Lawrence Merry, U. S. Minister to

Costa Rica, a personal letter thanking him for the information received from him as to the expense of some work done on the Costa Rica Pacific Railway, in which he says: "It contains information that we want and are very glad to get. I have always felt that the estimate of the 

Nicaragua Canal Commission for building a canal across Nicaragua was ample to do the work, having regard to unforseen exigencies and contingencies that are sure to arise in a work of such magnitude. The engineers of the Army are notorious for making large estimates, and the

additional amount put upon the Nicaraguan estimate by Col. Hains was an arbitrary sum, based upon nothing. This present commission is taking up the subject carefully, and we mean to render a report which shall prevent the enemies of the canal from staving off action by the plea of non-information. We mean to make our report full and complete. 

Whether it will please anybody after it is made is another matter. I think whatever our conclusions are that we shall be able to give pretty good reasons for them.

I had a very interesting two months in Europe, studying the Panama Canal problem as presented to one in the office of the Panama Canal Company in Paris, and in examining the German, the Dutch and the English ship canals. The work was most interesting and instructive to me.

We now have a large force at work in Nicaragua; we have an exploring party on the Isthmus of Darien to the eastward of Panama, hunting for a possible route across that Isthmus, which many people believe exists; and also have some people along the Panama route; and more will probably be sent down within a short time. The Commission itself will go to Central America probably some time during the last half of December, and will stay there until some time in the spring. We shall want to examine the Nicaragua route carefully for the benefit of the new members of the Commission, the Panama route will have to be gone over and carefully studied on the ground, and probably some of us may go to the Isthmus of Darien if our exploring parties report any probability of a practicable route which I personally do not believe exists." The general plan of investigation determined upon by the Commission included, besides the work of the parties at all the suggested points - Nicaragua, Panama and Darien a personal examination of the different routes and so after careful study of former surveys and explorations of the entire isthmus as well as the work done in Europe, the Commission sailed from New York on January 6th, 1900 for what proved to be a three months stay, reaching the United States again on April 3rd, 1900. As soon as Admiral Walker reached 

Washington he had at once to attack the accumulation of mail, most of it concerned with quite uninteresting business matters. In a letter to Lieutenant-Commander N. Sargent, commanding U.S.S. Scorpion, then in Columbian waters, he says, "The only news of consequence in Washington is the announcement by Dewey of his candidacy for the Presidential nomination. It has apparently fallen very flat. It is a good deal ridiculed, and I think will do him harm. I am very sorry for it because I have always liked Dewey. 

The Nicaragua Canal Bill was still before Congress and 

Admiral Walker wrote, on April 28, 1900 to Mr. Lane, Secretary of

Legation at San Jose, Costa Rica, "The Nicaragua Canal Bill is to come up in the House the first day of May, as you will perhaps have seen by the newspapers. It is to be discussed on the first and second, and will probably be voted on late on the second. I have no doubt that it will pass by a very large vote, but in what shape no man can tell.

There is considerable opposition to it in one way and another. Some men are opposed to the bill entirely, others are opposed to its consideration at this time, but I have no doubt that it will go through. 

Many of the most sensible men have thought it wise to let it go over until December, fixing an early day in that month for taking it up, discussing it and voting on it, and in the meantime to make provisional treaties, get the report of the Commission and be enabled to know just what they are doing. I think that would have been the course taken if it had not been for Hepburn's fear of losing his re-nomination for Congress. It has been talked a good deal here and in his district that he is a railroad lawyer, that he has not been in earnest about passing the bill, that he has simply been playing with it, and purposely holding it back. The talk has been sufficient to frighten him and he now appeals to his friends to let him take the bill up and pass it in the House, claiming that otherwise he cannot be renominated and that his political career will be closed.

The Bill will undoubtedly be debated at some length and various amendments will be proposed. What will be adopted is impossible to tell. 

The thing that I most fear is that the question of fortifying the canal and the whole question of the Hay-Pauncefote treaty will be thrown into the ring, and in that way politics will be injected into the canal scheme, and the whole situation will be confused. However, my judgment is that the bill will pass the House in some shape, will not be taken up in the Senate until next winter, and at that time will be passed in the Senate, will go to conference and be knocked into pretty fair shape. Nothing is more uncertain, however, than legislation in the United States. " 

To a friend in Iowa Admiral Walker wrote on May 3rd, 1900, "I had three months experience in Central America during the winter, and while it was a rough experience, and there were many things that would be called hardships by most people I quite enjoyed it. Was well from start to finish and came home in good condition. I am now busy with office work here, and am hoping that we shall be able to clean up the work of the Commission and make our final report by the end of next November.

The House yesterday passed a bill for building the Nicaragua Canal by a very large majority. Many of the men who voted for it did so believing that it would not be touched by the Senate this session, and that the bill, which is now crude and absurd, would be knocked into shape before it is returned to the House. Our methods of legislation are more absurd than anything I know of."

In regard to this bill he wrote to Mr. W. L. Merry, U. S. 

Minister at San Jose, Costa Rica on May 8th, "I am a strong friend of the canal but I think it is better policy to let the matter stand over until early next session, and in the meantime for this government to make the necessary arrangements with Nicaragua and Costa Rica, as well as with Columbia and the New Panama Canal Company, receive the report of this Commission, which will be ready by the 1st of next December, and be in a position to act intelligently. I think the arrangements with Nicaragua and Costa Rica should be made while the question of the

location of the canal is still open. They could undoubtedly be made at much less expense than if we were to proclaim in advance just where we proposed to build the canal. I have no doubt that it is entirely feasible to build a ship canal both across Nicaragua and across the Panama route; and it is only a question in my mind as to which will be the most advantageous for the United States. There should be no sentiment in the matter, but it should be treated as a matter of business, just as you or any other man of affairs would treat personal matters or the matters of a great corporation. CAB I believe that the chances are greatly in favor of the Nicaraguan route, not because it would be less expensive but because of the rights, privileges and concessions which can be obtained if the Governments of Nicaragua and Costa Rica see their own interests and are open to reason. While I think Columbia would be ready to give such concessions as the United States may require, I think it will be extremely difficult to make satisfactory arrangements with the French Company, and that may be an insuperable bar to the Panama route. This, however, is only my personal opinion, and of course you will treat it as confidential." And again on June 5th, "The Nicaragua Canal Bill, as you know, passed the House by a very large vote, 225 35 or thereabouts. Mr. Morgan failed to get it up in the Senate.......The vote in the House does not represent anything much except politics. 

Many members who voted for the Bill stated openly that they would not have done so had they not felt sure that the Bill would be held up by the Senate. They voted for it in order to make themselves solid with their constituents.........I have all along been urging the propriety and good policy of having the report of the Commission before final action should be taken in Congress in order that Congress should know exactly what it is doing, and also of having provisional treaties with Costa Rica, Nicaragua and Columbia as well as an agreement with the New Panama Canal Company to the effect in each case that if the United States should determine to build a canal across the American Isthmus by what is commonly called the Nicaragua Route or by the Panama Route, as the case might be, then Nicaragua, Costa Rica, Columbia and the New Panama Canal Company would do so and so. As soon as Congress adjourns, which will probably be in a day or two, I shall urge upon the Administration the necessity of moving in these matters without unnecessary delay. I hope the move will be made and that such a course will be taken as may result in throwing out the blackmailers who are ready to come forward with concessions to sell to the United 

States Government. 

Without pretending to speak with any exactness, as our data is not yet in, I am inclined to think that the Panama Canal can be completed for considerably less money that the Nicaragua Canal can be built, but I believe that the route finally selected will be determined, not by expense of construction, but by the rights, privileges and concessions which may be obtained by the United States clean and clear of all complications. I think that if this Commission should report that satisfactory terms can be made for one route and not for the other, that report will settle the matter, both routes being feasible from an engineering standpoint. I fully expect to see a canal bill pass at the next session and I hope to see the preliminary steps taken towards the

construction of a canal within the next twelve months. There is a strong feeling in the country in favor of building the canal and I think that when our report is finally made there will be very little further delay. Of course I assume that during the recess steps may be taken to somewhat modify the Hay-Pauncefote treaty, but whether it is modified or not, it will be much easier to confirm it in the 

Senate after the elections are over."

Naturally Admiral Walker was always interested in all Naval matters and was often asked by friends what he thought of proposed legislation or acts of the administration. To his old friend Paymaster S. N. Whitehouse, then living in England, he wrote on June 5, 1900, "There is not much to tell you in a Navy way. The Navy appropriation bill has been held up for some time between the House and the Senate mainly on the question of the price to be paid for armor. 

I suppose it may go through today or tomorrow as Congress will certainly adjourn within the next 48 hours perhaps it may tomorrow. 

An amendment has been tacked on to the bill which has been accepted by both Houses, giving the Secretary of the Navy authority to put retired officers upon active duty wherever he pleases. It is without limit and will, in my opinion, work injury to the service. It was fathered and brought about by our mischievous friend Mr. Chandler. 

You and I may find ourselves assigned to some active duty yet before we die, and certainly before we wish either duty or death." 

In this same month, June, Admiral Walker and Mr. Pasco were asked by the Secretary of State, Mr. Hay, to prepare a scheme for treaties with the Central American States and the Admiral wrote to Mr. Pasco in Florida to suggest his coming to Washington as soon as he could as they both wished to facilitate canal business as much as possible. "Honorable William Lawrence Merry 

Minister of the United States 

San Jose, Costa Rica 

My dear Mr. Merry: 

Your interesting letter of the 21st of August was received a few days since. I have read it carefully and with great interest. 

I agree with you entirely in what you say as to the necessity for immediate action both in Nicaragua and Costa Rica in order to obtain provisional privileges for the construction of the proposed canal. I have taken great interest in this matter and have strongly urged action upon the President and the Secretary of State. Indeed I have gone as far as I dared, having a little fear of arousing antagonism in certain quarters. I think the President realizes the necessity for action and 

I trust that the matter will receive attention. I have not only urged this personally but I have prepared a careful paper giving my views as to the privileges and concessions which are necessary before legislation. This was done at the request of the Secretary of State and was very well received by him. I think it will have very considerable

weight in any negotiations which may be carried on. I have also talked confidentially with some of the strongest men of both Houses of Congress and have urged upon them the necessity of taking a business view of the matter, telling them we would be likely to be held up by the two Central American Governments if Congress passed a law directing a canal to be built by the Nicaragua route before the necessary concessions had been obtained. Just now, with the Presidential canvass on and the trouble in 

China to take up the time and attention of the President and the Department of State, it is a little hard to make progress with canal matters not strictly belonging to this Commission." 

To Paymaster Whitehouse, Oct. 15, 1900, "We, of course, now are very much interested in the Presidential convass, I think that the chances are largely in McKinley's favor, but I shall be glad when the election is over and a decision has been reached. I am hoping that 

Bryan will be beaten, and beaten so badly that he will be buried forever so far as politics go."

In the midst of the rush of work of the Commission, trying to get the report ready for the meeting of Congress, Admiral Walker was rather amused to receive from Mr. Gilson Willets of the Christian Herald, New York, a letter asking whether he would write an article for publication on "The Advantage of being a Country Boy." Ꮊf course it was quite out of the question for him to do so as his time was entirely taken up with canal matters and it is very doubtful whether he would have written such an article under any circumstances as he disliked writing and would have found such a piece of work trying. 

Mr. Merry, the United States Minister to Costa Rica, who of course was much interested in having the Nicaragua Canal built since Costa Rica bordered a portion of the route and who kept in touch with everything concerning it had been disturbed by some articles written by a Professor Heilprin and in a letter of October 30th, 1900 Admiral

Walker reassured him on that point and sends him the latest canal news. "Professor Hailprin has written several articles with regard to the loss of water in, and the subsidence of Lake Nicaragua. They are absurb, and I do not think they need any further denial after what has been said by Mr. Hayes, late Geologist of the Nicaragua Canal Commission, and by Mr. Davis, the Hydrographer for that Commission and for this one. I have not heard anything from Professor Heilprin since 

Davis called his attention to the fact that if the water in Lake Nicaragua had been subsiding at the rate he claimed, the Spanish fortifications at Granada, as well as the city itself, must have been built under water. I have asked one or two geologists with regard to Heilprin, and they give him rather a black eye. I think the articles he has written, several in number, carry no weight with anyone..........

Mr. Hutin and the Secretary of the New Panama Company came over from Paris to see this Commission, but not upon my invitation nor upon the invitation of the Commission. After staying a few days, Hutin sailed for Paris on the 23rd, one week ago, to consult with his people, and expects to return here on November 18th. I think that he has woke up to the fact that it is necessary to do something if he wishes to save anything from the wreck. I am rather sorry for the French canal

people because they are in a very tight place. If the United States should determine to build a canal across Nicaragua, the French company could never raise another dollar. No financier would put his money into a canal which would come into direct competition with one backed by the enormous wealth of the United States, a country that would not attempt to make money out of the canal by tolls, but would expect to be reimbursed by increased facilities for trade...... 

This Commission has completed all its work in Nicaaragua with the exception of its borings between Greytown and the Lake...... 

Some of the boring work still to be done is of first importance. We have had a good deal of difficulty finding a dam site on the San Juan River where a dam could be built of the proper size within reasonable limit of cost, but reports now look more favorable." 

The Admiral was naturally interested in the approaching election and early in November wrote to a friend, "I cannot help thinking that McKinley will be re-elected. It does not seem possible to me that the American people would be such fools as to elect Bryan, but still we cannot feel sure until the election is over. There is a very large unreliable and floating vote, and I shall feel much more comfortable after the election is over and McKinley is elected." 

The following letter to Senator Morgan, November 19, is of interest as showing how much Admiral Walker's judgment was relied upon. "Enclosed I send you a bill, corrected, so as to be in working shape, and not put our Government into the hands of the Nicaraguans. I should like to make the clause for that purpose stronger but perhaps if the bill merely orders the President to report back to Congress in case he cannot make satisfactory terms, it may be sufficient. 

Before the holidays I hope to be able to congratulate you upon the passage and signing of the bill for which you have worked so long and ably. "

The Isthmian Canal Commission's report in regard to the Nicaraguan route did not differ materially from that of the Nicaragua Canal Commission, except that it proposed a larger and deeper canal with much larger locks. The preliminary report of the Isthmian Canal 

Commission was handed to the President on the afternoon of November 30, 1900. 

The conclusion read: "In view of all the facts, and particularly in view of all the difficulties of obtaining the necessary rights, privileges, and franchises on the Panama route, and assuming that Nicaragua and Costa Rica recognize the value of the canal to themselves and are prepared to grant concessions on terms which are reasonable and acceptable to the United States the Commission is of the opinion that the most practicable and feasible route for an isthmian canal to be under the control, management and ownership of the United States' is that known as the Nicaragua route".

This came as a bombshell to the French who had been employing dilatory tactics and demanding an exorbitant sum for their rights.

The stockholders now awoke to the fact that they were in danger of losing everything they had put into the enterprise and in December they sent the Secretary of their company to Washington to sell out their property for anything the United States would give them. 

Hutin, the President of the company, who had carried on the negotiations so far kept up his attempt to "hold up the United States" and no straight, reasonable offer could be obtained from him. Mr.

In a letter of December 19, 1900 Admiral Walker writes to a friend "This Commission was obliged to report in favor of the Nicaragua route because we were ordered to report on a route which could be entirely owned and controlled by the United States, and the Panama people would make no offer of any kind to sell out. They would like the United States to go into partnership with them, but that our people would never agree to. The Panama route is entirely feasible and so is the Nicaragua route, and it would take about the same length of time to put the canal through either way. The controlling features as to time would be the building of the big dams, one on each route, and in addition to this on the Panama route is the great Culebra Cut." 

There were so many delays that the prospect of passing a canal bill before the end of the short session of Congress rapidly faded and Admiral Walker wrote on January 21st, 1901 "I fear that the prospects of the passage of the Canal Bill in the Senate at this session are very poor......The Bill would go through by a very large vote if it could be got up......I think the Canal Bill will be crowded out by the 

Appropriation Bills which must be passed in order to avoid an extra session......I hear reports that the Nicaraguan government is giving grants of large amounts of land along the proposed line of the canal. 

If that is the case, and it should be determined to build the canal, it would force the Government to buy land from private parties, and would, of course, increase prices very much above Government rates. 

This would entail a very large expenditure for canal purposes and may be just enough to turn the canal to Panama, because I believe that no Canal bill will go through Congress without a provision being attached to it that the President shall open negotiations for both routes; that is, with Columbia and the French Company, as well as with Nicaragua and Costa Rica. In that case the comparative cost would cut a very considerable figure in determining which route should be adopted. I suppose thare are people in Nicaragua as elsewhere who are ready to make what they can out of the matter, but it would be well for the Nicaraguan Government to bear in mind that they may be injuring their own case."

Admiral Walker's letters of this time, that have been preserved, are mostly concerned, quite naturally, with canal matters or Navy affairs but occasionally other things turn up; some sailor who served under him in the Civil War asks his help to secure a pension or he is appealed to for information in regard to some event of war days in which he took part. The following letters give an idea of the various subjects of correspondence. 

To a Washington lady he writes, "The expedition through the Yazoo Pass and down the Goldwater and Tallahatchie Rivers to the neigh-

borhood of Fort Pemberton.....was a combined expedition of the Navy and Army. My impression is that no cotton was taken by the Navy on that expedition. What may have been done by the Army transports I cannot say and at this late date it would probably be very difficult to obtain accurate information. The log books of the naval vessels are all kept in the Bureau of Navigation, Navy Department, but I imagine there are no log books of the Army transports in existence." 

He was decidedly opposed to the order of the Navy Department prohibiting the sale or issue of malt or alcoholic liquors on board ships of the Navy and at Naval stations. "I am on the retired list; have done no military duty for a considerable time and have no personal knowledge of the effect of the order to which you refer. 

In a general way, I think it is a mistake to legislate or to attempt to prohibit men from a reasonable use of malt liquors and wines. My experience has been that it is better to treat men as men, showing a reasonable amount of respect for and confidence in them, which tends to make them respect themselves. I believe that the prohibition of beer and light wines in canteens for the Army or Marine Corps is a great mistake. It induces men to frequent saloons in the neighborhood whenever they have the opportunity, where the vilest kinds of liquors are sold, and is productive of harm and subversive of discipline. The canteens are really clubs for the men where they can pass leisure time, read newspapers, magazines and books, play chess, checkers, backgammon, billiards, ten pins and other harmless games and drink a glass of beer or light wine if they desire. These canteens or clubs, tend to prevent the men from seeking for worse places of amusement. " 

A reply to Captain Sigsbee, then Chief Intelligence Officer of the Navy, written on April 15, 1901 shows still another subject on which he could give information: "Your letter of the 10th, with regard to the Navy Department's General Order No. 300, dated August 9, 1882, designating a flag for the President of the United States, has just reached me. The blue flag, with the arms of the United States in the centre, was designed by me when I was Chief of the Bureau of Navigation, and was adopted on my recommendation. The General Order was issued over the signature of the Secretary of the Navy, and without doubt had the approval of the President of the United States. No Secretary would ever issue an order of that kind, directly affecting the President, without consulting with, and obtaining, his full approval. 

The establishing of another flag by the War Department is most improper, but we must assume that the Secretary of War did not know of the President's flag, which has been in existence nearly nineteen years, and the President, if he approved of the War Department's flag, as it is to be assumed he did, probably had never had his attention called to the matter. I think the question should be taken up and one flag determined upon, and one only, for the President. I also think that the plain blue flag, with the coat of arms of the United States in the centre, is more appropriate than any we could devise, besides having been in use for the past nineteen years. "

A pleasant change from so much business is a brief account of McKinley's second inauguration. "Yesterday the session of Congress came to an end, and Mr. McKinley was inaugurated and started in for a

second term as President.

It was a mixed and rather disagreeable sort of day, being fair part of the time and rainy during the remainder, although it did not rain hard at any time. The crowd was something enormous. The procession was a fine one, there being fully twenty thousand troops in line, and a very large mumber of civil organizations. I think, upon the whole, it was the finest procession that I have ever seen in this city."

The Canal Bill was not passed at that session of Congress owing to the unwillingness of that body to move in the matter until the Clayton-Bulwer treaty should be modified. In April Admiral Walker wrote to Mr. Lane at San Jose, Costa Rica, "We are in a fine position now to make very favorable terms for either the Nicaragua or the Panama route for a canal, but just what Mr. Hay will do no one can yet tell. I understand him to be very desirous of making arrangements with England first.......Our people are very much in earnest about a canal, and we are rich now. The danger is of a financial collapse and hard times, which would reduce the revenues and bring many recruits to the ranks of the opponents of the canal. Still I cannot help hoping that next winter will bring things to a crisis. My own impression is that if we cannot get rid of the Clayton-Bulwer treaty in a pleasant way, Congress will pass a resolution abrogating it." 

In June he wrote, "Efforts are being made to come to an amicable arrangement with England over the Clayton-Bulwer treaty, and my own opinion is if that fails a determined effort will be made in Congress to abrogate the treaty and then go ahead with the canal. I trust that will not be necessary, however, as it is better to do things pleasantly than otherwise." 

In the summer of 1901 the Secretary of the Navy at that time, John D. Long, asked Admiral Walker's opinion of a suggestion that had been made that the standard at the Naval Academy, both for admission and for graduation were too high and his letter elicited the following reply, "The standard at the Naval Avademy is now low, both for entrance and for graduation, lower than that of any technical school of standing in the country. The question of still further lowering the standard is a very grave one. The absurdity of the officer who said to you, "it is not unlikely that the five boys in any class at the head of the list of those dropped would be of quite as much value in the service as the five boys at the head of the class," is I am sure, fully appreciated by yourself. He says in effect that brains, education and cultivation are not required in the Navy. If a class be divided into two equal parts, it goes without saying that as a body the men in the upper half are decidedly better than the men in the lower half, although there are occasional exceptions to this rule. It is sometimes the case that a man who graduates high in his class may lack practical ability, while another who graduates low, because of his carelessness in regard to studies, or from boyish pranks which bring him demerits, may develop into a man of good ability and become an excellent officer. The exception, however, does not affect the general rule. As a rule a man's standing in mathematics and kindred studies at the Academy is a good measure of his brain power, but I am inclined to think that too much weight has been given in years past to the mathematical branches and

too little to the more practical branches of a naval officer's seamanship, &c. and I think considerable weight should be given to a young man's military bearing and habits, to his promptness, executive ability, &c. all of which go to make him an efficient military man, who can lead and control men. In our Navy, where promotions are almost entirely by seniority, it is very desirable that men of poor ability should not be placed permanently in the service to become our future captains and admirals, upon whom delicate questions and grave responsibilities, international in character, may at any time rest..... 

I would suggest that the standard at the Naval Academy be not further lowered; that if practicable the standard for entrance be raised, and that greater weight be given to officer-like bearing, executive ability, capacity to lead and control men and all the practical parts of the profession."

On September 6th, while attending the Pan-American Exposition at Buffalo, President McKinley was shot by an anarchist named Czolgosz and Admiral Walker wrote to Mr. Merry, U. S. Minster to Costa Rica on the 12th of the month, "At this date everything looks well for the President and I have no doubt he will be back in Washington by the first of next month in good working condition. His case seems to have been handled promptly and with skill by the attending surgeons, and of course everything possible has been done for him. I see no reason to doubt his prompt recovery."

Mr. McKinley died on the 14th of September to the surprise and sorrow of the public. "The death of the President is very much felt here. The final breakdown was a great surprise to everybody, as it was generally supposed that he was progressing favorably." 

After brief religious services on September 15th, which were attended by President Theodore Roosevelt and members of the Cabinet, as well as personal friends, the body was taken to the Buffalo City Hall and the next day a special funeral train bore it to Washington where it rested in the East Room of the White House over night with a guard of honor. Admiral Walker was one of this guard. 

During the whole summer of 1901 there had continued to be very annoying delays and postponements on the part of Mr. Hutin who was acting for the New Panama Canal Company in their negotiations with the Isthmian Canal Commission; it seemed impossible to get from him a clear, plain answer to the question which the Commission had been asking since the spring of 1900; "For what sum of money, in cash, will the company transfer to the United States all of the rights, privileges, franchises, property, etc., of every description, which it owns or controls in connection with the construction of a canal across the isthmus of Panama." Mr. Hutin was in Washington in October "talking like a fast express. He is here with a sort of proposition to discuss and talk over, but after two days discussion the prospect of coming to anything reasonable doesn't seem to me good." But by 

December, after the appearance of the preliminary report of the Isthmian Canal Commission, the stockholders had taken fright, fearing the total loss of their investments, had deposed Mr. Hutin from the presidency of the company and sent its Secretary to Washington author-

ized to sell their property for anything the United States was ready to give. On December 30th Admiral Walker wrote to Mr. Noble, "Mr.

Lampre, whom you will of course remember, sailed, according to the newspapers from Havre on last Sturday, the 28th, for this country to negotiate the matter. If he comes over here and makes a clean cut straight offer to sell out everything the company owns for forty millions or less, the question of a route for the canal will become interesting." From a letter to Captain A. F. Rogers of December 30, 1901, "The question of an isthmian canal is a most interesting and absorbing one. The practicable routes seem to be confined to Nicaragua and Panama. There are great difficulties both ways but the officers of the New Panama Canal Company made it impossible for us to report in favor of that route by the unreasonable value that they placed upon their property. I don't think a tide level canal will ever be built by Panama. We estimate that it would take twenty years to complete it and the cost would be probably $300,000,000, perhaps more, no man can tell. An excellent canal with locks can be built by Panama and will take through it five times the commerce that will use it during the next 300 years. You know that Sault Ste Marie Canal, which connects the waters of Lakes Huron and Superior, passes during the seven months that it is open, nearly three times as much commerce through it as passes through the Suez Canal during the entire year. 

If it was in use the whole twelve months it could pass five times as much, and that is a lock canal. "I hope that some day we may come together again. I look back upon the winter spent in California on the Deep Water Harbor Board as one of the pleasantest in my experience. I was charmed with Southern California and should very much like to go out there for the winter, taking Mrs. Walker with me........perhaps I may do it some day." 

On November 18th, 1901, in a letter to Mr. Francis R. Hart

Admiral Walker writes: "For more than two years we have used every effort to get a straight offer for the sale of their property from the New Panama Canal Company, but we have been met with talk and letters, evidently intended simply to use up time and cause delay. 

I have no idea that Mr. Hutin has from the first intended to make a straight offer. He is relying on obstructive tactics such as he has used heretofore and a lobby in Congress. The most that we could get out of him was a long paper, received a few days ago, with regard to the value of their property, which footed up between one hundred and nine and one hundred and ten millions. Of course the sum was preposterous and will not be listened to by Congress for a minute. If he had come forward with a straight, business-like, reasonable proposition, he possibly might have put a very different face upon things but I have no idea that he intended from the first to do anything of the kind."

During this month, November, it became evident that there was a leak somewhere as items came out in the papers that should not have been known to anyone outside the Commission and its offices. At last the leak was fastened on a man named Hoffman, who had been recommended by Col. P. C. Hains, and he was turned out of the office and

directions given that he should not be permitted to enter any of the rooms of the Commission again. "He sold all he could of the report to the newspapers. He is now under bond to await the action of the Grand 

Jury and I hope to see him landed in the jug." The Admiral wrote to 

Professor Haupt on November 30th, "Portions of the Report and various papers were taken from the office by Hoffman, who sold them. I do not think there is a doubt of his guilt. The United States Attorney has had him arrested and he is now under bonds awaiting the action of the Grand Jury. I hope it will result in fining him severely and sending him to the penitentiary for a term of years. I think it would improve his health and it would not injure his morals."

The full report of the Isthmian Canal Commission was taken to the State Department on November 30th, 1901 in locked boxes the keys of which Admiral Walker handed to the Secretary of State, John Hay, himself. 

A straight offer by the Panama Canal Company to sell out everything it possessed for what the United States was willing to pay was expected in January, 1902 and Admiral Walker wrote to a Boston friend on the 4th, "The prejudices of the people of this country are strongly in favor of the Nicaragua Route and these prejudices are reflected in Congress, particularly in the House of Representatives" but he felt that the expected offer would somewhat confuse the situation and it was impossible to judge what the outcome would be. In February he felt that a canal was sure to be built and wrote to Professor Haupt, "I have no doubt that under present conditions the Panama route is the better route for the United States to adopt. I think that the chances for disagreeable surprises are much less than by Nicaragua and that we are more sure of building the canal by that route within a reasonable time than by the other. Nor have I any doubt that the French Company can give a clear title to their property and that we can obtain all necessary concessions from Columbia. The only unknown quantity is the price at which Columbia may hold the concessions. Of course if she puts her price up to an unreasonable figure, the canal will not go that way.......I see more or less articles that are published for the purpose of holding up the Nicaraguan scheme, but they are all based on want of knowledge or sentimentalism; they have no facts to stand upon. Of course this kind of thing is to be expected, because our people have been educated to the Nicaraguan line and the sentiment of the country is undoubtedly strongly that way."

Prince Henry of Prussia was in the United States at this time and Admiral Walker attended a lunch given to him by twelve wealthy New York men, "Captains of American Industry" in order that he should meet and talk with business men of large affairs in the country. Admiral 

Walker had been asked to send in advance some account of his career and activities for the information of the prince. The lunch was at Sherry's and was very handsome as well as interesting. 

The question of the carrying capacity of the proposed canal was a very important one and was much discussed; of course its use would gradually increase if it was successfully put through, but how rapid would the increase be and how great an amount of tonnage must the first construction allow for. As showing what the very best informed opinion on

the subject was in 1902 the following portion of a letter from Admiral Walker to his son in March of that year is worth quoting, "You know it will probably be many a long year before the traffic through an isthmian canal exceeds ten million tons annually. That is a little more than the amount that passes through the Suez Canal at the present time, and its geographical location is such that the Suez Canal will always have a much larger tonnage than an American isthmian canal. Should the tonnage of the Panama route or the amount which could be handled with the supply of water in Lake Bohio under the present plan exceed ten million tons, the construction of a dam at Alhajuela is a small matter, not a very expensive one, and would render possible a traffic of forty or fifty million of tons." Although the traffic through the Panama Canal increased more rapidly than had been expected, exceeding twenty more million tons in 1937, Admiral Walker was correct in thinking that, owing to its situation, the Suez Canal would carry more tonnage, which in that same year came to weel over thirty-two and a half million tons.

Admiral Walker constantly received requests for copies of the Canal Commission's Report but could supply only copies of the report proper and the supplementary report as Congress had not authorized the printing of the appendices, maps, plans and so forth; he evidently feared that the information contained in them might be lost as is evident from a letter to Senator Allison dated March 18, 1902, "The

Report is a very valuable one, as it gives a complete history of all of the projects for a canal across the American isthmus from the beginning, with concessions, contracts, laws, etc., etc., as well as a most valuable set of maps. In fact it contains all that is known of isthmian canal matters to the present time. It will be a great misfortune to have the report printed by piecemeal in a mangled condition for a specific purpose. Can you not get an order to print the entire report, with appendices, maps, profiles, plans, etc? Unless it is so printed, this valuable information, which has cost the government more than a million dollars to obtain, will not be in a permanent form, and will sooner or later be lost. After so much money has been expended in obtaining the necessary information and putting it into shape, it would be very bad policy to have it eventually disappear for the want of a comparatively small expenditure of money to print a complete edition to be distributed in the usual way, so that it may reach the libraries of the country, be preserved, and become a matter of permanent record." 

Interest in the questions of an isthmian canal was very great and widespread and Admiral Walker was constantly asked for information on the matter and his opinion as to what the outcome would be. 

He was asked to speak at a meeting of the Reform Club of Boston but wrote that because of the situation of canal matters in Congress he felt it would be impossible for him to make an engagement for any fixed time; "I am also somewhat hampered by the condition of negotiations for concessions with Columbia and the Central American States. I could not under any circumstances promise to deliver an address I am a better listener than talker - but I should be glad to be present at the meeting and to join in the general discussion as far as practicable. As things are now, however, I cannot safely make an engagement." In spite of declining 

---

this attractive invitation Admiral Walker was able in the end to attend the Reform Club dinner on April 24th for he was ordered to represent the Navy at the unveiling of a statue of Commodore Perkins at 

Concord, New Hampshire, the ceremony taking place April 25th.

As the spring advanced the chances of passing a canal bill before Congress adjourned grew less and the Admiral felt discouraged about the possibility of action; on May 3rd he wrote to Professor 

Johnson, "I should have said a month or two ago that we were sure to have a canal bill enacted this session, but as time goes on without getting it up, the chances for action become rapidly less and less. 

My own impression is that Senator Morgan has been during this session, the worst enemy of the canal, and the long, tedious and absurb hearing which he has insisted upon having, will result in knocking out all canal legislation for the present. I believe the President is earnestly in favor of a canal and that he prefers the Panama route, but he will probably not take any active part in the matter." 

Ever since the end of the Spanish War Admiral Sampson had been failing in health, broken down by his treatment and the neglect of his officers and men in Congress and at the hands of a portion of the newspapers owing to the injection of politics into the wretched Schley affair. On May 6, 1902 he died. The Navy, where he was respected and admired as man and officer and his ability appreciated, did everything possible to show him honor and his military funeral was one of the largest ever seen in Washington. "The escort consisted of the corps of cadets from the Naval Academy with the Naval Academy Band; a battalion of Marines with the Marine Band; two battalions of sailors from the North Atlantic Fleet with the band of the flagship, and a battery of field artillery from Fort Myer. The funeral was attended by the President and Cabinet, Judges of the Supreme Court; a large number of the Diplomatic Corps; a committee from the Senate and from the House of Representatives, besides many members who were not on the committees; practically all the Naval officers in town in uniform and a large number of Army officers in uniform. The services were at the Church of the Covenant......which was packed with people. 

The services were simple but impressive. The crowd outside was very large and the route of the procession all the way to the cemetery at Arlington, was lined with thousands of people, while the cemetery was full there must have been thousands in the cemetery. The cavalry at Fort Myer was drawn up in columns of companies, and saluted as the remains passed. Everything was done that could be done to make the funeral impressive, and it was very successful." 

At last, on June 19th, the Spooner amendment passed the Senate to the great satisfaction of Admiral Walker; on the 21st he wrote to Mr. Noble, "The Spooner amendment went through the Senate 

Thursday in fine shape. The Amendments to it which were adopted were all good and as it stands today is the best canal bill that has ever been considered by Congress. It is now in conference and if the Conferees do not monkey with it and spoil it, nothing could be better. 

If it once goes to a square vote in the house, I have no doubt it will be accepted by the House."

The Admiral was in the chamber of the House of Representatives on June 26th to see the passage in that body of the Canal Bill and on the same day he wrote to a friend in San Francisco "to-day Spooner amendment, so-called, passed the House by a vote of 252 to 8, exactly as it came from the Senate, without the change of a word or letter. It is a very complete bill, providing for every possible contingency, and furnishing the means through Government Bonds to be issued, for the work of construction. The bill now only requires the signature of the President. We are in a fair way at last to begin work on the great waterway connecting the Atlantic and Pacific oceans. It will be necessary to look up title to the French Company's property and to make a satisfactory treaty with Columbia. That treaty probably cannot be confirmed before next December so that work can hardly begin before some time next winter; but I assume that the preliminary matters, such as treaties, titles, etc., will be taken in hand very promptly and pushed through to a finish." And from a letter to his wife July 1st, "Mr.

Morgan was defeated in his resolution to investigate and advertise for claims against the Panama companies, and all that kind of thing, a very hasty resolution; but he came in yesterday with a resolution calling upon the Secretary of State to report to the Senate how the Canal Commission had spent its money, and the resolution went through without any objection, as such resolutions almost invariably do. Just how much work this resolution will put upon us I am not able to say at present, but it will probably involve a good deal. However, we have plenty of time to do it." Senator Morgan had been from the first an ardent advocate of the Nicaragua route and was very bitter about the decision to build the canal at Panama, "he has fought the Panama scheme and the present bill with all his strength; has acted very absurdly in many respects and has been utterly unreasonable. I suppose the end is not yet, because he will do everything he can to defeat the treaty" (with Columbia) "when it comes into the Senate, hoping in that way to force the canal to Nicaragua; but I think he will fail again. He is a monomaniac on the subject of the Nicaragua route; there is no reasoning with him and he is intolerant and abusive with everybody who differs from him on that subject. "

Admiral Walker was particularly pleased that the Canal bill went through in a form which he felt thoroughly sustained "the acts and findings of this Commission, which I believe has been able and conscientious in the performance of its duties. I am only desirous now that a commission as able and honest may be selected for the purpose of construction." as soon as the President's signature made the Canal Bill law, the question of what could be done to protect the future workers from the sicknesses so common on the isthmus arose. A quotation from a letter to General George M. Sternberg in July shows how important the Admiral felt this question to be; "I feel very strongly as you do, that when this Government undertakes the building of a canal across the American isthmus rigid sanitary rules must be made and enforced. In a memorandum of points made some months since for a treaty, I included this very subject the United States must have full quarantine and sanitary control, with the power to enforce its regulation.... 

I look upon the question of sanitary control as one of the things that

should be taken up at the earliest possible moment and definite plans and rule decided upon. If your services can be secured, I shall consider the Government very fortunate." 

It was decided, after careful investigation by the Attorney General, that the French Company could give a clear title to their Panama properties which left only the matter of a satisfactory treaty with Columbia to be settled. "That drags on slowly and whether we shall be able to negotiate one that is satisfactory remains to be seen. The 

Columbians are difficult to deal with, particularly at this time when their whole country is in an uproar and they are playing politics for all they are worth." 

On November 29, 1902 Admiral Walker wrote to Mr. Pasco that "it would seem that the negotiations between the Secretary of State and Mr. Concha have come to a standstill and that our minister in 

Bogota has been directed to call the attention of the Columbian Government to the impossible conditions here. I am awfully sorry that my advice to send a special commission to Bogota had not been taken last spring. I believe if it had we might have had a fairly satisfactory treaty by this." But on December 2nd he writes to Mr. Henry Loomis 

Nelson "Things are looking better because Mr. Concha, the Columbian 

Minister, who has been an obstructionist of the worst kind, has left Washington and will probably not return. The negotiations are now in the hands of Mr. Herran, at present the Secretary of Legation, who was educated in the United States, speaks English perfectly, is a very sensible man, and desirous of seeing a reasonable treaty concluded between the United States and Columbia. I think the chances for a successful negotiation are better now than they have been at any time heretofore."

And in a letter written to Commander S. A. Staunton the next day speaking of Mr. Concha's obstructionist ways the Admiral writes, "Just what his reasons have been I do not know. All kinds of suggestions have been made. One was that he, with others in Columbia, wanted to hold back a treaty until they could declare the French concessions at an end, so that Columbia might gather in the forty millions, as well as anything else that there was lying around........ 

Nothing has been done with regard to appointing a new Commission and I do not expect anything until a treaty has been agreed upon. The feeling is strongly in favor of Panama, and that has been intensified by the report of the Attorney General as to the ability of the French Company to give a clear title. His report was a very clean cut and convincing one......I hope for some good from the work of our vessels in the West Indies during the next two months. I should not have much confidence that the matter would be decently handled if Taylor was not going, but Taylor goes down as Dewey's chief-of-staff, and will really be the brains and the active man in the whole business, in which he takes great interest. He is the only man down there who is capable of handling the fleet in anything like a decent way and I have a good deal of confidence in him."

Senator Morgan was still angry with the Canal Commission on account of its preference for the Panama route and he presented a bill

for an organization to construct the isthmian canal, of which 

Admiral Walker wrote to Mr. Pasco on December 19th, "I think I know as much as anyone does about the proper organization for constructing a canal, but I should be very unwilling to have to make up a scheme for such an organization now and off hand. I do not believe there is a man living in the United States who could make such a scheme and have it anywhere near the right thing. Mr. Morgan's action reminds me of the old saying 'Fools rush in where angels fear to tread'. I suppose we have seen the last of the bill, however, as
