I have no idea it will ever come out of committee." 

And to Mr. Noble, on the same day, "I have no belief that 

Morgan's ideas will prevail, but if they should, such commission as he contemplates would wreck the administration and the Republican Party."

The treaty with Columbia was fought long and bitterly by 

Senator Morgan and on March 12th 1903 the Admiral wrote, "It looks now as if the Canal Treaty would be ratified by the middle of next week. When it will be ratified by the Congress of Columbia no one can yet say, but I imagine it will go through down there without much delay."

The treaty with Columbia was at last ratified on March 17th, 1903 by a vote of 73 to 5, in spite of the most persistent opposition on the part of Senator Morgan, and the only remaining bar to the immediate construction of the canal was the lack of ratification by Columbia. Their Congress was to come together early in April and it was hoped that in a month at most the treaty would be ratified; but when the Congress met ratification was opposed on various grounds. 

Admiral Walker was inclined to think that the main thing that was holding it up was an attempt on the part of the Columbians to squeeze the French Company and make them pay a large sum for ratification. 

Early in April of that year Admiral Walker, Colonel Hains and Mr. Burr went to the Isthmus to inspect the French work and go over the books, records and accounts of the French company; this trip was due to the fact that the French Company gave notice that they should ask to be reimbursed for expenses incurred in keeping the work going since their offer, and as it would be impossible to pay their claim unless someone in position to do so could certify to the correctness of the bills, these three members of the Commission went down and spent fifteen days on the Isthmus. They found that the officers of the French Company "were open and above-board in all their dealings with us, showed us their books, records and accounts, answered all questions asked them without hesitation, made copies of all papers that were required, and were apparently entirely honest in their dealings with us." 

On the 17th of June 1903, Admiral Walker received the honorary degree of L.L.D. from the University of Pennsylvania. He had never before been at a Commencement of one of the large universities and was much interested in the ceremonies and conferring of degrees which took place in the Academy of Music, "a very large

Opera House which was packed from floor to roof with the best looking audience that I have ever seen in a theatre, ladies and gentlemen, the best in Philadelphia. Every seat was taken, the aisles filled, and every inch of standing room utilized even in the 'peanut galery'. The whole thing ran as smoothly as possible, the ceremonies were dignified in every respect, and the oration was perfect. It was given by Dr. Howard Furness of Philadelphia, the Shakespearian scholar, a man of great cultivation. His manner and voice were perfect in every respect and the oration was charming...... After the honorary degrees had been conferred and the degrees in course had progressed for a considerable time, I was taken out of the Opera House, driven down to the river and went on board the yacht Waturus, belonging to Mr. Randall Morgan, who was kind enough to invite a lot of people to meet me at lunch. I think we sat down to lunch twenty-eight men, and the lunch was simply perfect." 

On the 22nd of June the Admiral wrote to Mr. W. Andrews, "I earnestly hope that the treaty will be ratified, and I cannot help thinking that it will be, although the reports are conflicting and we are not in a position here to form a very accurate judgment of the situation in Bogota. There is very strong feeling in the United States and in Congress in favor of the Nicaraguan route and if the Columbian Congress fails to ratify the treaty we shall probably be forced to 

Nicaragua, which I should much regret. "

The Navy League of the United States, on account of the widespread interest in the question of an isthmian canal, wished to have for their Journal an article by Admiral Walker on "The Navy and the Isthmian Canal," showing how necessary the Navy would be to the safe-guarding of the canal when once built but he felt that, aside from the time it would take him to write such an article, that with matters as they then stood it was better for him not to appear in print on that subject. "We don't want people to get the idea that the building of the Canal will necessitate a large and expensive Navy." 

A strong desire had grown up in the Navy by this time for the creation of a general staff for that branch of the military service and 

Admiral Taylor, then Chief of the Bureau of Navigation, who had given much attention to the matter for some years, hoped to get the necessary legislation through Congress at the coming session and Admiral Walker, in a letter to Senator Allison hopes that he will give the question his attention and interest himself in the proposed legislation as he considered it a subject of vital importance "to the Navy and therefore to the country.......Our Navy has grown a good deal in the last fifteen years and is likely to increase still more. To make it the efficient arm which it should be and which the country has a right to expect, a general staff is absolutely necessary."

This letter is only one example of the Admiral's constant interest in all that pertained to the Navy in spite of his absorption in canal matters for several years past and in the next letter in his letter book we find him writing to Senator Allison on another Naval matter, "Many range finders have been brought to the attention of the Navy Department and tested, but none of them have proved entirely satis-

factory. They have all failed in some one particular. The one now used in the Navy is English, is expensive, is not perfect, but the best to be had at present. The Bureau of Ordinance would be very glad to find a better one, of American make, and are ready to test any that look at all favorable.

To return to canal matters on September 22, 1903, Admiral Walker wrote in a "personal" letter to Henry Loomis Nelson, "This is the last day for the exchange of ratifications of the treaty. While it would not surprise me if we heard at any time that the treaty had been ratified by the Congress of Columbia, I do not think it probable; but I do not abandon hope. The whole trouble has been a hold-up for money. I do not think the Columbians have expected to get any more money from the United States, but they have been endeavoring to squeeze the Panama Canal Company and I have no doubt that if the Company had been willing to pay three or four million of dollars, the treaty would have been ratified. I also think that the Columbians will endeavor to keep negotiations alive and that sooner or later we shall get a fairly satisfactory treaty with them. The people of the 

Isthmus of Panama have been very much stirred up by the failure to ratify, and have been threatening to secede and declare themselves independent of Columbia. I have no doubt that if this Government would agree to quietly support them in an indirect way, they would promptly secede, but of course that is rather ticklish business." 

The Columbian Congress, especially called to ratify the treaty adjourned on October 31st without doing so and three days later, November 3rd, Panama declared her independence and the Republic of Panama was virtually recognized by the United States on 

November 7th by the reception of her diplomatic representative.

Commander John F. Hubbard of the U. S. gunboat at Colon, when Panama declared her independence, forbade the transportation of Columbian troops across the Isthmus as the United States was responsible for keeping the Isthmus open and civil war might make this impossible. 

On November 8th, 1903 Admiral Walker left Washington on the President's yacht, The Mayflower, for the Isthmus of Panama "on Confidential service"; he spent a month on the isthmus and the reason for the trip may be inferred from the following letter to the 

President, dated in Washington on Christmas Day, 1903. "The President,

Sir: 

I have to acknowledge the receipt late last evening of your note dated the 23rd, mailed the 24th, enclosing a memorandum from the General Board advising the occupation of a position near Yovisa, Republic of Panama, by a strong force of United States troops. 

Yovisa is the key to the military situation should Columbia attempt to invade Panama in force by land. I so reported to the Sec-

retary of the Navy from Colon, December 7, having advised Admiral Glass on the 4th to send a ship and a small force of marines to that point.

I think a force of 200 to 400 marines there, supported by ships in the Gulf of San Miguel, with Columbian and Indian scouts to bring early information of any advance by Columbian troops would control the situation and very likely deter the Columbians from attempting to occupy that region or from raiding it for the purpose of obtaining provisions and stores belonging to the mining company, or accurate information regarding conditions, or for the moral effect which such an advance would produce in the United States. 

I believe the quantity of provisions said to be stored at Real de Santa Maria (6 months for 5,000 men) is much exaggerated. 

I saw and talked for a considerable time on December 10, with an Englishman exmployed there who had that day arrived direct from the mine. He told me that a considerable quantity of stores had been accumulated there but gave me the impression of a much smaller quantity than stated in the memorandum enclosed in your note. 

From a military point of view the advice of the General 

Board is sound.

I can see no objections to our occupying that position excepting the danger of producing a bad impression in the United States and thus strengthening the hands of the opponents of the canal treaty. How great this is I am not in a position to say. 

Columbian troops advancing upon Panama in force must pass through Yovisa and the neighboring section, crossing rivers tributary to the Gulf of San Miguel.

Although a considerable distance away I think it can be maintained that an outpost at that point is only a proper military precaution for the defense of the canal and railroad strip. 

I am inclined to the opinion that a post of moderate strength should be established there, the commanding officer having strict orders not to act on the offensive unless attacked, but not to permit seizure by the Columbians of the steamer, animals and provisions belonging to the mining company. 

With the remainder of the marines in camp at Empire and Gorgona and Admiral Glass's ships always available, the post can be promptly strengthened by 1,000 to 1,500 men should the necessity arise, which I do not think probable at present. 

With the addition of the battalion of marines about to leave Philadelphia for the Isthmus, I think the United States force

certainly for a in that vicinity will be ample for all purposes; considerable time to come. 

I have the honor to be 

With great respect 

Your obedient servant 

J. G. Walker"

After the secession of Panama and its recognition by the United States as an independent country it became necessary to make a fresh treaty in regard to canal rights. This time there was delay in the United States Senate; a presidential election campaign was coming on and while there seemed little doubt that the treaty would be ratified in time, it was evident that much time would be wasted in talk in the hope of making some point that would help the opposition in the campaign. On January 11th Admiral Walker writes, "I spent all day yesterday (Sunday) in going about from one Senator to another explaining the bearing of the treaty and giving wished for information. This morning I went down to the Senate Committee Room for the same purpose, and suppose I may have to go again. I have no doubt that the treaty will be ratified when the vote is taken, but in the meantime it will be obstructed and points will be hunted for that may be used against Mr. Roosevelt and the Republican Party during the coming Presidential canvass." And writing on the same day to Senor 

Arias in Panama he expresses the opinion that the people of the United States were very strongly in favor of ratification of the treaty and that there was no reason to fear its defeat although there might be tedious delay caused by domestic politics. 

Admiral Walker was, of course, deeply interested in the question of the construction commission to be appointed when once the bill should pass Congress. "I am very desirous that it shall be the strongest possible body of experts and I think the President feels that way himself, but he has tremendous pressure brought to bear upon him......I am not worrying about myself. I have not asked anybody to bring my name before the President. I have never said a word to him myself as to my desire to be a member of the new commission and I don't intend to. If he appoints me he will do it of his own motion. If he does not appoint me I shall try to worry along during the remaining years of my life as easily and comfortably both mentally and physically as possible. " 

While awaiting the action of Congress Admiral Walker was making enquiries about various civil engineers who had been recommended to the President as desirable members of the new commission; his former connection with the C. B. & Q. Railroad was useful in this matter as he knew personally men in position to give him valuable information. 

The President planned to appoint the new commission as soon as the treaty was ratified and he was anxious to have the work taken up as soon as possible but the Admiral knew there would be some delay in

starting actual work. "I have no idea what the delay may be, but there are two or three vexatious suits in Paris which must be brushed out of the way before we can take over the property. That may delay us a month or two, but in the meantime I see no reason why the new commission should not go to the Isthmus and look the ground over, at all events."

The treaty with Panama was ratified by the Senate on February 1904 and on the 29th the names of the men chosen by the President for the new commission were sent to the Senate for confirmation; they were, Rear Admiral Walker, chairman; General George 

W. Davis, Mr. William H. Burr and William Barclay Parsons of New York; 

Benjamin M. Harrod of New Orleans; Carl B. Grunsky of San Francisco, all engineers; and Colonel Frank J. Hecker of Detroit, a business man.

They were promptly confirmed and on March 5, 1904 Admiral Walker sent to the Secretary of State the "Oath of Allegiance and Office" taken by him that day as Chairman of the Isthmian Canal Commission. His appointment was thoroughly approved by the country, it was generally felt that he was "probably the best posted man in the world on isthmian canal matters; he has been the head of three different commissions the Nic argua Canal Commission, the Isthmian Canal Commission and the present Panama Canal Commission." <3 President Roosevelt was naturally anxious to have work on the canal begun promptly, it was an undertaking in which he was deeply interested and which should reflect much credit on his administration but in view of his known impetuosity there was some doubt as to his wisdom in the matter. An editorial in the New York 

Evening Post of March 4, 1904 voices this feeling; it quotes the 

President as saying that he hoped to "infuse his own earnestness and energy into the Panama Commission" so that the excavation of the canal might be under way very soon. "But the Commission will make a very great mistake if it permits a hasty President to hurry it into the work at hand without taking proper time for the organization of its forces and the careful planning of its work. Leaving aside allthe technical engineering questions, the difficulty of getting tools, dredges, etc. to the scene of action, this is a case where a precipitate beginning is certain to involve a sacrifice of human life.......Admiral Walker is of the opinion that at least one and one half years will be required before the work can be in full swing. The Commission itself must first organize, then look over the ground and map out the work. The city of Panama must be drained and cleaned from end to end and given an adequate supply of good water. The harbor of Colon must be dredged and improved so that steamers can lie there all the year round. Colon itself must be supplied with a drainage system and fairly "lifted out of the marsh" in which it is situated. The drawing of plans and the perfecting of medical arrangements will take months. More months must then be given to the successful contractors in which to assemble their machinery and begin their gigantic task - just how gigantic probably nobody knows..........It will be seen that Rear-Admiral Walker cannot be accused of undue conservatism in estimating the time it will take to have the work going at full blast. Indeed, unless the contractors

are as fortunate as those who constructed the great Nile dam at Assuan, the work of building the canal is likely to take longer than the eight years Rear-Admiral Walker allots. So far as the labor problem is concerned........no men should be landed until there are adequate quarters provided---no matter what the President's desire to "make things hum". 

Fortunately for the new Canal Commission, it will begin its work with very complete information as to the physical conditions on the Isthmus .......at least two of its members, Rear-Admiral Walker and General

Davis, are familiar with the problem before them, and at least six are trained engineers, leaders in their profession, and wholly free from political influences. For these excellent selections Mr. Roosevelt deserves all praise. But he should not hamper the Commissioners by goading them on prematurely." The eight years mentioned was the shortest possible time suggested by the Admiral. He wrote to friends of eight or ten years or possibly longer.

Already, before the appointment of the Commissioners had been confirmed by the Senate, Admiral Walker was in communication with Dr. Gorgas, whom he had asked to have put in charge of sanitary matters on the Isthmus, wanting to see him and talk over the situation, and when the Commissioners sailed for the Isthmus on March 29, 1904, Dr. Gorgas accompanied them to go over the ground and plan a sanitary campaign.

Admiral Walker and General Davis, the two members of the new Commission who had a thorough knowledge of the problems facing them, were called upon by the House Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce to give their opinion with regard to a government for the Canal Zone and the following quotation from a letter to 

Senator T. C. Platt gives a clear idea of the Admiral's opinion. "I beg to say that I think no one in this country is now sufficiently well informed as to local conditions on the Isthmus of Panama to draw a bill in detail providing for a government of the Canal strip to be owned and controlled by the United States. The situation is peculiar. The people are different from our own, with very different habits, feelings and sympathies, and I think changes of law in that location should be made slowly and only after careful consideration by those on the ground familiar with the people and the peculiarities of the locality.......I suggest that the laws now in force in the strip be continued until modified; that the government of the strip be placed in the hands of the Isthmian Canal Commission with authority to make rules and regulations as found necessary which, when approved by the President shall have the force of law; and that the Commission shall be held strictly responsible for the execution of the laws and the preservation of good order and government within the limits of the ten mile strip, establishing such police force, courts, court officers and others as may be found necessary for carrying on a temporary government.. 

I would also suggest, if this course is followed, that the Commission be directed to make a full and careful report to the President for transmittal to Congress, setting forth fully its action in the matter of a temporary government, reasons therefor, and suggestions as to necessary legislation, if any, by Congress. By following this course the Congress would have before it, upon coming together in 1905, a carefully

171. drawn report giving it much information which would, I think, enable it to legislate, if thought proper, more intelligently and safely than is now possible. "Senate Bill 4513 sets up a government of three, entirely independent of the Isthmian Canal Commission and with no connection with the great works of the Isthmus. I think different interests and friction inevitable. It takes the very important question of sanitation, drainage, supply of potable water, and the health of the cities and strip from the Canal Commission and places it in the hands of this government of three, which must, of course, organize its own sanitary and engineering corps for doing the work which should naturally belong to the Commission in connection with its great work of constructing the canal. I think there is no doubt that this would cause delay, unnecessary expense, and probably crimination and recrimination. It is altogether bad. "I think that certainly during the early stages of this work, if not during the whole period of construction, all power on the Isthmus should be centered in the hands of the Canal Commission, keenly alive to the necessities of the situation, held to a strict accountability for success, with the reputation of its members at stake men who will have everything to lose by weakness, inefficiency and failure, while success will place them before the world as at the very head and front of their professions." 

The bill finally approved put the entire authority, military, civil and judicial, with power to make rules and regulations necessary for the government of the Canal Zone, into the hands of the President, to be vested by him in such person or persons as he should deem best.

The Admiral reached Washington on the Commission's return from Panama, on April 27th and on the next day told the President of the appointment by the Commission of Mr. Wallace to be its Chief 

Engineer which appointment met with his hearty approval. But even before this appointment the Commission had organized and sent to the Isthmus a number of engineering parties to make special examinations of some of the larger problems to be solved, such as a suitable harbor at Colon, the feasibility of a dam at Gatun and to study the alternate Tiger Hill line which had been suggested by Mr. George S. 

Morison, a member of the first Canal Commission; also the question of a dam and canal locks at Bohio, and the control of the Chagres River. This work was undertaken, as well as continuing the excavation work at Culebra, in advance of the employment of a Chief Engineer, it being thought of great importance that no time should be lost."

There were many details to be straightened out aside from the questions of sanitation and construction; great care had to be exercised in selecting men from the hundreds of applicants to fill positions, then there was the question of currency for the Isthmus and also the difficult matter of customs to be settled. Such things took much time, more than was appreciated by those who hoped to see "the dirt fly" at once.

During the visit of the Commissioners to the Isthmus in the summer of 1904 they worked diligently in framing laws to govern the Canal Zone, organizing the Executive Branch of the Canal Zone government and the Judiciary, prohibiting gambling, suppressing lotteries, adopting a penal code,prescribing sanitary regulations, and other acts of minor importance; while the new members of the Commission familiarized themselves with the situation and the plans for the future.

By September there were four or five hundred men at work in Culebra Cut excavating material by the use of the old machinery of the French Company and the Commission had already bought three steam shovels of the latest and most approved type and were advertising for eleven more for use "in this the great cut of the canal." 

Work was also going on in the valley of the upper Rio Grande on a reservoir to supply the city of Panama with pure water, while the survey for a system of sewers for that city had been completed and bids for pipes were soon to be called for, as well as for a thousand large dump cars and five hundred flat cars to be used in connection with the excavation at Culebra. At this time a large part of the more than two thousand men employed by the Commission were engaged upon sanitary work - cleaning, draining and waging war upon the mosquitoes.

In October Admiral Walker had an interview with the Civil Service Commission, under whose rules employees of the Commission were to be placed and Admiral Walker felt that he had no right to make more appointments while the change was pending. 

As fast as accommodations could be prepared for them, workmen were secured from the West Indies and Admiral Walker, in January 

1905 "had several strings to his bow for Japanese and Chinese labor" and expected soon to close a contract for two or three thousand men at least, which could be increased by further thousands as required. 

He wrote to Paymaster Tobey, Chief of Material and Supplies Department at Ancon, on February 14, 1905, "I am hoping to be able to send you quite a number of men from Great and Little Inaqua, but will probably have to feed them, after the manner that the Panama Railroad feeds its employees; and I wish you would so arrange matters that you can take them as they come along. I am trying to arrange to get men as soon as possible, and to send along others from time to time at short intervals. The labor question is a troublesome one, but I hope to get things in better shape before long." 

In February of this year the President had appointed Dr. 

C. A. L. Reed a member of the Committee, provided for by the treaty between Panama and the United States, to assess property on the Isthmus; he was at that time at the head of the American Medical 

Association and at the end of a fortnights stay in Panama sent to 

Mr. Taft, then Secretary of War, a very astounding report of the sanitary situation there. Mr. Taft forwarded the report to Admiral

Walker on March 6, 1905 with a letter in which he said of it, "It is so extreme in many of its statements that, though I should place it on my confidential files only, I feel that in justice to your Com-

1 mission even there it should be accompanied by such comment as your Commission might desire to make upon it. The bias of Dr. Reed is evident in the tone of the letter, but he makes a number of statements of fact which it is possible you or some member of the Commission may desire to controvert, and which certainly you should have an opportunity to deny if existing conditions justify and require it." 

The Commission at once took the matter up and carefully prepared an answer, taking up each statement in turn and disproving each by the records of the Commission as well as by the statements of the Commissioners concerned. The result was a reply sent to Secretary Taft on 

March 16th which entirely cleared the Commission of the charges of inefficiency and mismanagement. Both papers are too voluminous to include here, they are to be found in a publication of the Isthmian Canal Commission which gives all the correspondence in the matter. 

Dr. Reed's publication of his report in the Medical Journals before the Commission had an opportunity to answer his charges was, of course, most improper and difficult to understand in the case of a man holding the position he held at that time. In transmitting the reply to President Roosevelt Secretary Taft wrote, "I think that a reading of the two documents will show, what indeed was apparent on the face of Dr. Reed's statement that his charges aginst the Commission were biased and controversial, and not written in the judicial spirit that inspires confidence in their justice and accuracy--- The report of Dr. Reed was published without my knowledge or consent. 

The reply of the Commission seems to show that a large part of the plans and the action of the Commission which Dr. Reed criticises was fully concurred in and agreed to by Col. Gorgas and the other medical officers in the employ of the Commission. It is doubtless true that Colonel Gorgas and his staff believed that it would be wiser to give them an entirely free hand in the matter of ordering construction supplies and the employment of subordinates. It is quite probable that had the approval of the Commission in the matter of ordering construction and supplies been dispensed with there would have been fewer delays and less complaint on this account, but in the expenditure of such large sums as were necessary for the organization of the sanitary system, the construction of plant and the purchases of supplies, it may well be doubted whether the Commission would have been discharging its duty in withholding supervisory control.....There must be some restraint for purposes of economy upon expenditures, by an executive who has in mind the total expenditures and obligations being incurred. To allow each department to expend what in its judgment is necessary will certainly lead to extravagance and waste. " In the President's reply he says "it appears that the statements.....were in many instances unwarranted by proof.....it appears that the chief difficulties that have arisen have come from the inherent faultiness of the law under which the Commission was appointed." 

In spite of the complete exoneration of the Commission it became apparent that President Roosevelt wanted to hurry the visible work on the canal, to "make the dirt fly"; to a man of his impatient temperament the delay caused by the necessary preliminary work was annoying and not easily understood and Admiral Walker decided to

resign his position; he wrote to Mr. Godfrey in Honolulu, "I am still at the head of the Canal Commission, but am looking forward to severing my connection and giving up work shortly. I have had a pretty long pull at it and shall be glad to be relieved and be able to go and come at my own sweet will. Accordingly, on March 29th 

1905 he sent his resignation to the President, saying in part, "It is a satisfaction after my long connection with the great work of constructing a canal across the American Isthmus to be able to leave the enterprise in a condition so much more advanced than could have been reasonably expected after only one year's existence of the Commission, beset as our labors have been, by the many difficulties, delays and obstacles naturally attending the early stages of such magnitude." His resignation was accepted on April 3rd and writing to Mr. Pasco, a former member of the Isthmian Canal Commission, on that day, he says, "I have this morning received the acceptance of my resignation and I am only closing up two or three things here before leaving. The conditions have been impossible here during the entire winter. things."

REAR ADMIRAL WALKER DEAD 

Passes Away at Cape Neddick, Maine 

Cape Neddick, Me., Sept. 16 yesterday. 1-3 

Rear Admiral John Grimes Walker died here

Rear Admiral Walker was born in New Hampshire in 1835, and was appointed to the navy from Iowa on Oct. 5, 1850. Between 1851 and 1855 he was attached to the sloop of war Portsmouth, then on the Pacific station. He returned to the Naval Academy the year following and was graduated at the head of his class. On June 20 of the same year he was promoted to passed midshipman, and spent the following twelve months on the sloop of war Falmouth, on the Brazil station. While in these waters in 1858 he was transferred to the frigate St. Lawrence, the change being made on Jan. 23 of that year, when he was made a lieutenant. He remained on the St. Lawrence until the summer of 1859, when he was ordered home and went to the Naval Academy as instructor in mathematics. Here he stayed until late in 1860, and then joined the steamer Susquehanna. He spent the best part of 1861 along the Atlantic coast on the steamer Connecticut, and the following spring was assigned to duty on the steam gunboat, which was then attached to the West Gulf blockading squadron. He was especially mentioned for his bravery displayed in the passage of Forts Jackson and St. 

Philip, Chalmette batteries and the capture of New Orleans, also the operations against Vicksburg, which included the passage of the batteries both ways. On July 16, 1862, he received his commission as lieutenant commander, and shortly after this received his first command, the ironclad steamer Baron de Kalb. This craft was attached to the Mississippi squadron, and in her Walker was quite successful in his operations against Vicksburg during the winter of 1862-3. Walker further distinguished himself in two attacks on Haines's Bluff, the engagement at Arkansas Post, the Yazoo Pass expedition, the attack on Fort Pemberton, the capture of Yazoo City and the expedition up the Yazoo River to destroy steamers. In the last-named melee, Walker went through three sharp fights. At the seige of Vicksburg he also commanded the naval battery in the Fifteenth Army Corps. 

In 1864 he was given the command of the Saco, a steam gunboat, and was attached to the North Atlantic blockading squadron until the year following. While in charge of the Saco he participated in the capture of Wilmington, N.C., and forts, with the exception of Fort Fisher. At the close of the war Rear Admiral 

Walker was sent to the Brazil squadron, where he was in command of the steamer Shawmut. While in the tropics he was promoted to the berth of commander, on 

July 25, 1866, and shortly afterward was called home, and returned to the naval academy, where he remained until 1869. Then he had charge of the frigate 

Sabine, and in her was engaged in special service until 1870. The year following he was made lighthouse inspector, and in 1873 was made secretary of that board. In that capacity he remained until 1878. His next command was the 

Powhatan of the North Atlantic squadron, in 1881, and in the winter of that year he was placed at the head of the Bureau of Navigation. 

The personnel of the navy improved under his guidance. He was progressive and unfettered by tradition, discarding the obsolete and adopting the new and useful. The first ships of the new navy were then projected, and his knowledge and judgment were bent on making them the best in the world. Captain Walker

Cape Neddick, Me., Sept. 16 (2) brought forward the young men of the line who planned, invented and built ordnance, improved and tested armor-plate and applied electricity to the operation and fighting of warships. He was promoted commodore on Feb. 12, 1889, and the following November he took command of the new squadron of evolution, composed of the Chicago flagship, the Boston, Atlanta and Yorktown. The sailing of this fleet marked a new order of things, an epoch in the naval history of this country. The ships, since greatly surpassed by the big cruisers and battleships, were then as good as any afloat, and their visit to European ports proved a valuable and lasting object lesson. But 

Commodore Walker, who had been given the rank of acting rear admiral for the cruise, proved that he was out for business, and the men and officers of the famous "white squadron" so profited by his discipline and drill that they were a leaven to the whole navy on their return. Congress was stimulated by the excellent records of the men and ships to vote appropriations for new vessels and a larger navy. 

Relieved of the squadron, Commodore Walker was transferred to the South Atlantic coast and remained for some time in Brazilian waters. On his return north he was third in command of the great naval review in New York city, Admirals Gherardi and Benham being his seniors. In 1892 he was ordered to 

Venezuela to protect American interests, and when President Cleveland needed an officer to manage the affairs of the United States in the troubles of the young Hawaiian republic he sent Walker in command of the naval force in the Pacific. He made a keen, close study of the situation, but his reports, plainly though guardedly, favoring annexation, were directly opposed to President Cleveland's views and policy. Congress called for them, however, and their publication caused the commodore trouble. He was once more assigned to shore duty on his return, in which he was employed until his retirement. 

He was promoted to rear admiral Jan. 22, 1894. Rear Admiral Walker's knowledge of conditions and affairs in Central America, and the question of a transisthmian canal, won for him the appointment of chairman of the Nicaraguan Canal Commission. He was put on the retired list in 1897. 

Admiral Walker was at the head, also, of the old Isthmian Canal Commission, which succeeded in buying the Panama property from the French company.

WALKER FUNERAL IN BOSTON Special Services May Be Held in Washington 

88 

York, Me., Sept. 17 The funeral of Rear Admiral John Grimes Walker, who died at High Pasture, near Ogunquit, on Tuesday, will be held in Boston. 

It will be strictly private and for the relatives only. An official funeral may be held at Washington. It is the understood intention of the admiral's family to have the body cremated, after which the ashes will be sent to Washington for interment at the National Capital. 

Admiral Walker almost immediately after his retirement was made president of the Nicaraguan Canal Commission, which sailed for Central America on the gunboat Newport in December, 1897. The reports made by the commission revealed an extraordinary amount of exploration and surveying done by the engineers under Rear Admiral Walker's supervision; and the results of their investigations, coupled with that officer's intimate acquaintance with Central American affairs and conditions, caused him to prefer that route. When at the close of the Spanish-American war a new commission was appointed to investigate and report on all practicable routes for a canal across the Isthmus, Rear Admiral Walker was placed at its head, that the country might have the benefit of his recently reinforced experience. For a time he was inclined to favor the Nicaraguan route, but when the French company made its proposal to turn over its concessions, its property and its partly completed work to the United States on terms that he regarded as favorable, he urged Congress to accept them, and was thereafter a strong and consistent supporter of the canal now well under way. He was appointed chairman of the Panama Canal Commission by 

President Roosevelt in 1904, and thus had the honorable and arduous duty of organizing the new work and starting the actual construction. 

In March, 1905, the Commissioners, having completed the important preliminary labors, resigned in a body in order to permit the President to appoint a more compact board to carry on the practical work of building the great waterway. From that time Rear Admiral Walker was engaged in no public duties. The official navy register shows that Admiral Walker had a total sea service of seventeen years and two months, and total shore and other duty of twenty-three years and six months. He was unemployeed five years and seven months. In 

September, 1866, he was married to Miss Rebecca White Pickering, daughter of Henry White Pickering of Boston, who bore him five children. He was a member of the Metropolitan Club of Washington, and of the University Club of New York.

JOHN GRIMES WALKER 

The sturdy New Englander, whose funeral services will be held in this city tomorrow, Rear Admiral John Grimes Walker, retired, was more than a naval officer. His career rather affords an example of the wide range of duties and interests which may properly fall to an officer of our far-flung battle-line, who is at the same time gifted with strong common sense, energy and ability to handle in a genuinely statesmanlike way the great questions that confront the commander of a squadron at stations beyond the reach of minutely detailed instructions from Washington. Walker graduated at the head of his class, and after brilliant work during the Civil War, served for some years as secretary of the Lighthouse Board, and later as chief of the Bureau of Navigation, besides on a half-score of special assignments in the naval service. But he will probably be chiefly remembered in connection with the Panama Canal. He was the chairman of three Commissions successively appointed to deal with the canal problem, and it was his "Yankee jockeying," taken in connection with his peculiarly close relations with Senator Allison, the chairman of the Appropriations Committee of that body, that brought to pass the decision as to the route of the waterway, which is now regarded by everybody as peculiarly wise and fortunate. 

Although the first Commission of which Admiral Walker was chairman, investigated only the Nicaragua route, and although the second Commission, still under his leadership, directed to study all possible routes, virtually reported in favor of Nicaragua still, he became convinced quite early in these researches that the great waterway should be constructed at Panama. But at that time the French Government and the old French company were very intractable, and Admiral Walker did not purpose to give his case away by telling in a public report just what he tought of their property. For some months, if not years, he maintained a public and a private view of the canal question. 

All the time he was trying to lead up to Panama; but the French company wanted upwards of a hundred million dollars for its property, which was excessive. Sharing in a peculiar degree the confidence of a small group of 

Senate leaders, Walker boldly brought in a report from his commission in favor of the Nicaraguan route, recommending an immediate appropriation for undertaking it. This report stated that the French property and right of way was unobtainable, the price charged being prohibitive. The report did mention that, if the French property could be bought for forty million dollars or less, it might be worth taking, but since this offer would not be considered, Congress should proceed at once at Nicaragua. 

The French holders at last came to terms, and proposed to take that sum. Had they known how wholly disinclined were Admiral Walker and his associates who had been studying the question, to building at Nicaragua at all, they would doubtless have long held out for much higher valuation of their right of way. In those days the Commission, particularly the admiral himself, was severely criticised by friends of the Nicaraguan route, like the late Senator Morgan of Alabama, charging him with inconsistency--with having said all kinds of good things about the Nicaraguan route and then having virtually retracted them. But this did not disturb the admiral, who throughout his life was often the storm centre of controversy, and seemed to enjoy it. 

In fact, his career, and that of William B. Allison, now in feeble health in his seventy-ninth year, are monuments to the plans and foresight of the late Senator James Grimes of Iowa, who was one of the great Republican leaders of the Middle West in the Civil War period, Walker was his nephew. Allison married his adopted daughter. Walker, born in Hillsboro, N.H., on the death

of his mother went to Iowa to live with his uncle. For these two young men Senator Grimes mapped out careers; one he made his successor in the control of the Republican politics of the State and its long-time and much honored senator. Mr. Allison's prominence in the public affairs of the nation, exceeding considerably that of Grimes, fully equalled any expectations that might have been entertained of his journey on the political road. For Walker, Grimes planned a naval career, and this in turn became one of the most famous in the annals of that branch of the service. When William C. Whitney was Secretary of the Navy, the story gained currency that he was "run by Walker," his chief of the Bureau of Navigation. This reached Mr. Whitney's ears, and at a dinner one evening he took occasion to explain that he disliked to hear that he was being run by anybody, but that it was true that numberless appeals had been made to him from Walker's decisions, but that in investigating these he had found it invariably right to sustain what Walker had done, as the commonsense view of the situation. 

Admiral Walker will have a place in our naval history, or is at least entitled to one, for he was the first officer to hoist his flag as commander of a squadron of the new navy which is today becoming old. He commanded "the white squadron" which well on towards twenty years ago, after being exhibited in our ports, was sent abroad to show Europe that our long period of naval desuetude was over. Judged by the standards of today, that was not a particularly noteworthy force over which Commodore Walker flew his flag, but everything is relative, and it was the best squadron the United States had sent to sea for nearly two decades. It was composed when it lay in 

Boston harbor previous to its departure for Europe, of the Chicago, flagship; the Atlanta and Boston, cruisers, and the gunboat Yorktown. Its coming had long been anticipated, and its stay here was made as attractive as possible by a series of entertainments and attentions which today seem in memory to smack a little of provincialism. They were, however, rather the manifestation of local interest in a broad national movement. 

In these days the presence of three or four first-class battleships does not attract much attention. We are used to battleships; but when 

Commodore Walker came, the only navy we had been accustomed to for years was wooden, fair to look upon, and hopelessly obsolete, the lingering souvenir of a great past. For days nobody talked anything but "the white squadron," and you could not read about much of anything else in the papers. 

Up to that time all our warships had been painted black, though daubed a clay color in the Civil War. Nobody but Frenchmen painted warships white, and they had been for generations to Anglo-Saxons models of how not to do it on the sea.

The four trim cruisers, snowy as to their hulls and their canvas, for they all had considerable sail power, astonished Americans,and were popularly named "the white squadron." Now all our vessels are white except when they put on their dingy war paint; but that was "the white squadron" and ought so to have a berth of its own in our naval reminiscences. It sailed for Europe in due time and its movements were chronicled by the press with a particularity that seems unnecessary today, which could not have been exceeded had it been another invincible Armada. In all the happenings that

that attended the visit of the white squadron to Boston, Admiral Walker was at the fore, as became his rank, and there was a glittering interchange of ceremonies between the flagship and the State House and the City Hall that gave our people an idea of how very agreeable naval etiquette can be seen in its peaceable and social aspect. Those days seem far off from these when Congress votes $100,000,000 per annum for the navy, and he who kicks is called unpatriotic and is asked if he has never heard of our national destiny.
